# Gastroenterology

## Inflammatory Bowel Disease

**Q:** A 28-year-old has bloody diarrhea 8x/day, crampy abdominal pain, and fever. Colonoscopy shows continuous mucosal inflammation from rectum to splenic flexure. Diagnosis and initial management?

**A:** Ulcerative colitis (left-sided); initial treatment with oral and rectal 5-ASA for mild-moderate disease, or systemic corticosteroids for moderate-severe

UC is characterized by continuous mucosal inflammation starting at the rectum. Extent classifications: proctitis, left-sided (to splenic flexure), extensive (beyond splenic flexure). CCS IBD guidelines recommend 5-ASA for induction and maintenance of mild-moderate UC.

**Q:** How does Crohn disease differ from ulcerative colitis pathologically and clinically?

**A:** Crohn: transmural inflammation, skip lesions, any GI tract from mouth to anus, cobblestone mucosa, fistulae/abscesses, non-bloody diarrhea; UC: mucosal/submucosal, continuous from rectum, bloody diarrhea

Crohn disease complications include strictures, fistulae, and abscesses from transmural inflammation. UC complications: toxic megacolon, colorectal cancer. Both have extraintestinal manifestations (joints, eyes, skin, liver).

**Q:** A patient with Crohn disease is steroid-dependent. What is the next management step?

**A:** Immunomodulator (azathioprine/6-MP or methotrexate) or biologic therapy (anti-TNF: infliximab, adalimumab) — steroids should not be used for maintenance

CCS IBD guidelines recommend against long-term corticosteroid use in IBD. Thiopurines (AZA/6-MP) or anti-TNF are first-line for maintenance. Anti-TNF + immunomodulator combination therapy reduces immunogenicity and improves outcomes.

**Q:** What is the Montreal classification for ulcerative colitis severity?

**A:** Mild: <4 stools/day, no systemic symptoms; Moderate: 4-6 stools/day, minimal systemic features; Severe (Truelove-Witts): >6 bloody stools/day + systemic features (fever, tachycardia, anemia, elevated CRP)

Severe UC (Truelove-Witts criteria) requires hospitalization for IV corticosteroids (methylprednisolone 60 mg/day). Failure to respond within 3-5 days → IV cyclosporine or infliximab rescue or colectomy discussion.

**Q:** What is the indication for colectomy in ulcerative colitis?

**A:** Medically refractory UC, toxic megacolon (not responding to medical therapy), severe hemorrhage, dysplasia/cancer, or failure of rescue therapy (cyclosporine/infliximab)

Proctocolectomy with ileal pouch-anal anastomosis (IPAA/J-pouch) is the preferred surgical approach. Colectomy is curative for UC. CCS guidelines define rescue therapy failure as lack of response after 3-5 days of optimal medical therapy.

**Q:** What medications are used to maintain remission in Crohn disease?

**A:** Azathioprine/6-mercaptopurine, methotrexate (for methotrexate responders), anti-TNF (infliximab, adalimumab), vedolizumab (gut-selective), ustekinumab (IL12/23 blocker)

CCS guidelines recommend immunomodulators or biologics for maintenance in Crohn disease. Combination anti-TNF + immunomodulator superior for maintaining remission. Vedolizumab is preferred in patients with significant infections history. Ustekinumab for moderate-severe CD.

**Q:** What is toxic megacolon and how is it managed?

**A:** Toxic megacolon: acute non-obstructive dilation of colon ≥6cm on AXR with systemic toxicity (fever, tachycardia, leukocytosis) — usually complicates severe UC or Clostridioides difficile

Management: bowel rest, IV fluids, IV corticosteroids, IV antibiotics (if infectious), NG decompression, roll patient frequently, avoid opioids/anticholinergics that slow motility. Surgical consult immediately — colectomy if no improvement in 24-72 hours.

**Q:** What colorectal cancer surveillance is recommended for IBD patients?

**A:** Colonoscopy 8-10 years after IBD onset, then every 1-3 years depending on extent and activity; every 1-2 years for pancolitis or left-sided colitis with active inflammation

IBD is associated with increased CRC risk, especially pancolitis (relative risk 2x), increasing with disease duration. Chromoendoscopy improves dysplasia detection. CCS guidelines recommend surveillance starting 8-10 years after symptom onset.

**Q:** What extraintestinal manifestations are associated with IBD activity (parallel bowel disease) vs. independent?

**A:** Parallel (correlate with bowel activity): peripheral arthropathy, erythema nodosum, oral aphthous ulcers; Independent: axial arthropathy (ankylosing spondylitis), primary sclerosing cholangitis, uveitis

Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC) is strongly associated with UC (80% of PSC patients have UC). PSC increases CRC and cholangiocarcinoma risk. ALP and GGT elevated. Diagnosis: MRCP or ERCP.

**Q:** What screening/monitoring is required for patients on thiopurines (azathioprine)?

**A:** CBC regularly (risk of myelosuppression), LFTs, TPMT genotype before starting (predicts thiopurine metabolism), screen for lymphoma risk with regular skin exam

TPMT deficiency increases thiopurine toxicity (severe myelosuppression). Standard dosing: AZA 2.0-2.5 mg/kg or 6-MP 1.0-1.5 mg/kg. Monitor CBC every 2-3 months once stable. Risk of EBV-related lymphoma (EATL) with long-term use.

**Q:** What is the role of 5-ASA (mesalamine) in IBD?

**A:** First-line induction and maintenance for mild-moderate UC; combined oral + rectal route most effective; NOT effective in Crohn disease (no evidence for maintenance)

5-ASA acts locally on colonic mucosa by reducing prostaglandin synthesis. Oral formulations differ in release site. Rectal 5-ASA (enema/suppository) is superior for proctitis and left-sided UC. CCS guidelines: 5-ASA has no role in Crohn disease maintenance.

**Q:** A patient with Crohn disease has an enterocutaneous fistula. What medications are most effective?

**A:** Anti-TNF therapy (infliximab) has highest evidence for fistula healing in Crohn disease — combine with drainage of septic collections before starting biologic

Infliximab (ACCENT II trial) achieved fistula closure in ~55% of patients. MRI pelvis to assess fistula anatomy before treatment. Combined seton drain + infliximab for complex perianal fistulae. Surgical consultation for complex or septic fistulae.

**Q:** What is primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC) and its association with IBD?

**A:** PSC: chronic fibro-inflammatory biliary stricturing disease causing progressive biliary obstruction; strongly associated with UC (70-80% of PSC patients have IBD)

PSC diagnosed with MRCP showing beaded biliary tree. No effective medical therapy to halt progression. Annual colonoscopy in PSC-IBD for CRC surveillance. Liver transplantation for advanced PSC. Increased risk: cholangiocarcinoma, colorectal cancer.

**Q:** What vaccines should IBD patients on immunosuppressives receive?

**A:** Annual influenza (inactivated), pneumococcal, Hepatitis A and B, HPV, Tdap, COVID-19; AVOID live vaccines (MMR, varicella, yellow fever, oral typhoid) when on biologics/immunomodulators

CCS IBD guidelines recommend vaccination before starting immunosuppression when possible. Patients on biologics should not receive live attenuated vaccines. Inactivated vaccines are safe on immunosuppression but may have reduced immunogenicity.

**Q:** What is the Crohn Disease Activity Index (CDAI) and how is it used clinically?

**A:** CDAI: composite of stool frequency, abdominal pain, general well-being, extraintestinal features, antidiarrheals, abdominal mass, hematocrit, weight; <150 = remission; >450 = severe

CDAI is used in clinical trials and for treatment decisions. HBI (Harvey-Bradshaw Index) is simpler clinical tool. CCS guidelines recommend objective markers (CRP, fecal calprotectin, endoscopy) alongside clinical scores for disease monitoring.

**Q:** What distinguishes Crohn's from UC?

**A:** Crohn: skip lesions, transmural, any GI location, granulomas, fistulas. UC: continuous from rectum, mucosal, bloody diarrhea

Crohn complications: fistulas, B12 deficiency. UC: toxic megacolon, CRC risk after 8-10 years.

**Q:** What is step-up treatment for moderate-severe Crohn's?

**A:** Steroids for induction -> immunomodulators (azathioprine/MTX) -> biologics (infliximab, adalimumab, vedolizumab, ustekinumab)

Top-down approach increasingly favored for high-risk features. Combo anti-TNF+immunomod superior to mono.

**Q:** What extraintestinal IBD manifestations correlate with disease activity?

**A:** Erythema nodosum, peripheral arthritis type 1, oral aphthous ulcers, episcleritis

Activity-independent: pyoderma gangrenosum, AS, PSC, uveitis. PSC more common in UC.

**Q:** What are the key differentiating features between Crohn disease and ulcerative colitis on histology?

**A:** Crohn: transmural inflammation, non-caseating granulomas (30-50%), skip lesions, fissuring ulcers, fibrosis. UC: mucosal/submucosal inflammation only, crypt abscesses, crypt distortion, continuous involvement, pseudopolyps.

Transmural inflammation in Crohn explains complications like fistulae, strictures, and abscesses that are rare in UC. Granulomas, when present, are highly suggestive of Crohn but absent in ~50% of cases. 10% of colonic IBD remains indeterminate.

**Q:** What is the anatomic distribution pattern of Crohn vs UC?

**A:** Crohn: any GI segment mouth-to-anus; terminal ileum most common (70%); skip lesions; perianal disease (30%). UC: starts at rectum, extends proximally in continuous fashion; never above ileocecal valve (except backwash ileitis).

UC classification by extent: proctitis (rectum only), left-sided (to splenic flexure), extensive/pancolitis (beyond splenic flexure). Crohn phenotype: inflammatory (B1), stricturing (B2), penetrating (B3) per Montreal classification. Perianal disease modifier (p).

**Q:** What extraintestinal manifestations of IBD parallel disease activity vs are independent?

**A:** Parallel activity: peripheral arthritis (type 1), erythema nodosum, episcleritis, oral aphthous ulcers. Independent: axial spondyloarthropathy, uveitis, pyoderma gangrenosum, PSC (UC association).

PSC is strongly associated with UC (70% of PSC patients have UC) and increases CRC risk further. Axial arthropathy does not improve with colectomy. Erythema nodosum is the most common skin manifestation. Pyoderma gangrenosum may worsen with debridement (pathergy).

**Q:** What is the step-up vs top-down approach in Crohn disease management?

**A:** Step-up: start with 5-ASA/budesonide → immunomodulators (AZA/6-MP/MTX) → biologics. Top-down: early biologic ± immunomodulator for high-risk patients (young, perianal, deep ulcers, extensive disease, steroid-dependent).

Canadian consensus favors risk-stratified approach. High-risk Crohn patients benefit from early biologic therapy (anti-TNF: infliximab, adalimumab) to prevent structural damage. Combo therapy (infliximab + AZA) superior to monotherapy (SONIC trial). Treat-to-target: endoscopic remission.

**Q:** What are the indications for surgery in Crohn disease?

**A:** Failure of medical therapy, obstruction not responding to medical management, abscess requiring drainage, fistulae refractory to biologics, perforation, hemorrhage, malignancy, growth retardation in children

Surgery is NOT curative in Crohn — 50% recurrence within 5 years at anastomotic site. Strictureplasty preferred over resection when possible to preserve bowel length. Post-op prophylaxis: metronidazole 3 months + immunomodulator or biologic. Endoscopic assessment at 6-12 months post-op.

**Q:** What are the indications for colectomy in ulcerative colitis?

**A:** Fulminant/toxic megacolon not responding to IV steroids + rescue therapy (infliximab or cyclosporine), dysplasia/CRC, refractory disease despite biologics, intractable symptoms

Unlike Crohn, colectomy is curative for UC. Total proctocolectomy with ileal pouch-anal anastomosis (IPAA/J-pouch) is standard. Pouchitis occurs in 50% of patients (treat with ciprofloxacin or metronidazole). CRC surveillance: colonoscopy at 8 years post-diagnosis, then every 1-2 years.

**Q:** What defines toxic megacolon and how is it managed?

**A:** Transverse colon diameter >6 cm on AXR + systemic toxicity (fever >38.6, HR >120, WBC >10.5, anemia). Manage with NPO, NG suction, IV steroids (hydrocortisone 100 mg q8h), IV antibiotics. Surgery if no improvement in 48-72h.

Toxic megacolon can complicate UC (more common) or Crohn colitis. Avoid anticholinergics, opioids, colonoscopy, and barium enema (risk of perforation). Serial AXR to monitor colon diameter. C. difficile must be excluded. Mortality 30-50% if perforation occurs.

**Q:** What biologic therapies are available for IBD in Canada?

**A:** Anti-TNF: infliximab, adalimumab, golimumab (UC only). Anti-integrin: vedolizumab (gut-selective α4β7). Anti-IL-12/23: ustekinumab. Anti-IL-23: risankizumab, guselkumab. JAK inhibitors: tofacitinib, upadacitinib (UC). S1P modulator: ozanimod (UC).

Vedolizumab has a favorable safety profile (gut-selective, lower infection risk) and is often preferred in elderly or infection-prone patients. Ustekinumab is effective in both Crohn and UC. Tofacitinib carries risk of VTE and herpes zoster. Choice depends on phenotype, safety, and prior exposures.

**Q:** What is the role of 5-ASA in IBD?

**A:** First-line for mild-moderate UC (oral + topical rectal). Sulfasalazine useful for IBD-related arthritis. 5-ASA has minimal to no role in Crohn disease (except possibly mild colonic Crohn).

5-ASA works topically on colonic mucosa (anti-inflammatory via PPARγ and NF-κB). Mesalamine formulations: Asacol (pH-release, terminal ileum/colon), Pentasa (time-release, small bowel+colon), Salofalk. Topical (rectal) 5-ASA is more effective than oral for proctitis/left-sided UC.

**Q:** What are the thiopurine (AZA/6-MP) considerations including TPMT testing?

**A:** Check TPMT genotype/phenotype before starting: deficient (0.3%) → contraindicated (fatal myelosuppression); intermediate (11%) → dose reduce 50%; normal → standard dose. Monitor CBC q1-2 weeks initially, then q3 months. Risk: myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis, lymphoma.

TPMT testing is mandatory per Canadian guidelines. Thiopurines take 8-12 weeks for effect. NUDT15 testing also recommended (especially East Asian patients). Risk of hepatosplenic T-cell lymphoma in young males on combo therapy (thiopurine + anti-TNF) — consider alternatives.

**Q:** What is the CRC screening protocol for IBD patients?

**A:** Start screening colonoscopy 8 years after symptom onset (for extensive colitis) or 15 years (left-sided). Then every 1-2 years with chromoendoscopy or random biopsies (4 per 10 cm). PSC patients: annual from diagnosis.

IBD-associated CRC risk is 2-5x general population, higher with: extent of disease, duration, PSC, family history of CRC, persistent inflammation. Chromoendoscopy with targeted biopsies is superior to random biopsies. Dysplasia management: visible lesion → endoscopic resection if feasible; invisible flat dysplasia → colectomy.

**Q:** How is acute severe UC (ASUC) managed in hospital?

**A:** IV methylprednisolone 60 mg/day (or hydrocortisone 100 mg q8h). Assess response at day 3 (Travis criteria: >8 stools/day or 3-8 stools + CRP >45). Non-responders: rescue therapy with infliximab or cyclosporine. Surgery if rescue therapy fails.

ASUC is a medical emergency with 1% mortality. Travis criteria predict colectomy need. Exclude CMV colitis and C. difficile. VTE prophylaxis is mandatory (high VTE risk). Do not delay surgery if deteriorating — subtotal colectomy with end-ileostomy preserves future IPAA option.

**Q:** What is the management of perianal Crohn disease?

**A:** Simple fistula: antibiotics (metronidazole + ciprofloxacin), seton placement if needed. Complex fistula: EUA + seton + anti-TNF (infliximab preferred — ACCENT II trial). MRI pelvis for mapping. Abscess: surgical drainage before starting biologics.

Perianal Crohn affects 30% of patients and is a marker of aggressive disease. Always drain abscesses before immunosuppression. Combination of surgical (seton) and medical (anti-TNF) therapy is most effective. Vedolizumab and ustekinumab are alternatives if anti-TNF fails.

**Q:** What nutritional considerations are important in IBD?

**A:** Crohn: check iron, B12, folate, vitamin D, calcium, zinc; exclusive enteral nutrition (EEN) is first-line for pediatric Crohn (as effective as steroids for induction). UC: iron deficiency common. Both: monitor BMI, albumin; avoid prolonged steroids for growth in children.

EEN (polymeric formula for 6-8 weeks) achieves mucosal healing in pediatric Crohn without steroid side effects. In adults, supplemental EN can support nutrition. IV iron (ferric carboxymaltose) preferred for severe iron deficiency anemia (oral iron may worsen symptoms).

**Q:** What is the difference in fecal calprotectin use in IBD?

**A:** Fecal calprotectin >250 μg/g: highly suggestive of active IBD inflammation. Used to: distinguish IBD from IBS, monitor disease activity non-invasively, predict relapse, assess response to therapy. Does not distinguish Crohn from UC.

Calprotectin is released by neutrophils and correlates with endoscopic inflammation. More accurate than CRP for assessing intestinal inflammation. Target calprotectin <150-250 as treatment goal. Can reduce unnecessary colonoscopies. Elevated in infections, NSAIDs, CRC — not specific for IBD.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic approach to celiac disease?

**A:** Screen with tissue transglutaminase IgA (TTG-IgA) + total IgA. If positive: confirm with duodenal biopsy (villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, intraepithelial lymphocytes). Must be on gluten-containing diet for testing

Associated: type 1 DM, thyroiditis, dermatitis herpetiformis, IgA deficiency (use IgG-based tests). Treatment: strict lifelong gluten-free diet. Monitor: TTG-IgA, nutritional deficiencies (iron, B12, folate, calcium, vitamin D).

**Q:** How is celiac disease diagnosed and what are the associated conditions?

**A:** Diagnosis: Serology: tissue transglutaminase IgA (TTG-IgA) — most sensitive and specific screening test; check total IgA (2-3% of celiac patients are IgA-deficient → false-negative TTG-IgA, use TTG-IgG or deamidated gliadin peptide IgG instead). Biopsy: duodenal biopsy showing villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, intraepithelial lymphocytosis (Marsh classification). Patient MUST be on gluten-containing diet during testing. Genetic: HLA-DQ2 (95%) or HLA-DQ8 — high NPV (if absent, celiac very unlikely). Associated conditions: type 1 diabetes (8-10% have celiac), autoimmune thyroid disease, IgA deficiency, dermatitis herpetiformis (pathognomonic skin manifestation), iron deficiency anemia, osteoporosis, infertility, lymphoma (enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma — EATL).

Celiac disease affects ~1% of Canadians, though many are undiagnosed. The Canadian Celiac Association recommends screening first-degree relatives (10-15% prevalence). A strict lifelong gluten-free diet is the only treatment — even trace amounts can cause intestinal damage. Dermatitis herpetiformis (intensely pruritic vesicles on elbows, knees, buttocks) responds to dapsone and gluten-free diet.

**Q:** What are the dermatologic manifestations of celiac disease and how is dermatitis herpetiformis diagnosed and treated?

**A:** Dermatitis herpetiformis (DH): intensely pruritic grouped vesicles and papules on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees, buttocks, scalp, back). Considered the skin manifestation of celiac disease — nearly all DH patients have celiac on biopsy (though often asymptomatic GI). Diagnosis: skin biopsy from PERILESIONAL skin (not the lesion itself) showing granular IgA deposits at dermal papillae on direct immunofluorescence (gold standard); also check TTG-IgA. Treatment: dapsone (rapid symptomatic relief within 24-48h — check G6PD before starting) + strict gluten-free diet (takes months to control DH but is the definitive long-term treatment). Monitor for dapsone side effects: methemoglobinemia, hemolytic anemia, agranulocytosis.

DH is pathognomonic for celiac disease — its presence confirms celiac even without GI symptoms. The biopsy must be taken from uninvolved perilesional skin for immunofluorescence, as the vesicle itself shows only non-specific inflammation. Gluten-free diet alone can control DH in most patients but takes 6-12 months. Dapsone does not treat the underlying intestinal disease.

**Q:** What are the extra-intestinal manifestations of celiac disease and which conditions are associated?

**A:** Dermatologic: dermatitis herpetiformis. Hematologic: iron deficiency anemia (most common presentation in adults), folate/B12 deficiency, unexplained thrombocytosis. Bone: osteoporosis/osteopenia (from calcium and vitamin D malabsorption). Neurologic: peripheral neuropathy, cerebellar ataxia (gluten ataxia), epilepsy. Reproductive: infertility, recurrent miscarriage, delayed puberty. Hepatic: elevated transaminases (often mild, reversible with GFD). Dental: enamel defects. Associated autoimmune conditions: type 1 diabetes (screen all T1DM), autoimmune thyroid disease, IgA deficiency, primary biliary cholangitis, Sjogren syndrome, Turner syndrome, Down syndrome. Malignancy risk: enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma (EATL), small bowel adenocarcinoma.

Many patients with celiac disease present with extra-intestinal manifestations rather than classic diarrhea/malabsorption. Canadian Celiac Association recommends celiac screening in all patients with unexplained iron deficiency anemia, osteoporosis in young patients, and type 1 diabetes. Strict gluten-free diet reduces (but may not eliminate) the lymphoma risk.

**Q:** Is colonoscopy recommended as a screening test for colorectal cancer in average-risk patients?

**A:** No, colonoscopy is not recommended as a screening test for CRC (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Colonoscopy is not recommended for average-risk CRC screening due to limited evidence and potential harms including complications, cost, and resource implications.

**Q:** What is the recommended colorectal cancer screening for patients with a first-degree relative with CRC?

**A:** Colonoscopy starting at age 50, or 10 years earlier than the relative's age at diagnosis, whichever occurs first

Patients with one or more first-degree relatives with CRC require colonoscopy due to increased risk. Start at age 50 or 10 years before relative's diagnosis age.

**Q:** A patient has a first-degree relative with colorectal cancer. At what age should screening begin and with what modality?

**A:** Colonoscopy starting at age 50, or 10 years earlier than the relative's age at diagnosis, whichever occurs first

Patients with one or more first-degree relatives with CRC require earlier and more intensive screening with colonoscopy.

**Q:** What does the Canadian Task Force recommend regarding colonoscopy for colorectal cancer screening?

**A:** Recommend NOT using colonoscopy as a screening test for CRC (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Colonoscopy is not recommended as a primary screening tool for colorectal cancer in average-risk patients according to Canadian guidelines.

**Q:** A patient has a first-degree relative with colorectal cancer. At what age should colonoscopy screening begin?

**A:** Start colonoscopy at age 50, or 10 years earlier than the age their relative was diagnosed, whichever occurs first

Patients with one or more first-degree relatives with CRC should begin colonoscopy at age 50 or 10 years before the relative's diagnosis age, whichever is earlier.

**Q:** Which test is NOT recommended for colorectal cancer screening in average-risk patients?

**A:** Colonoscopy

Colonoscopy is not recommended as a screening test for average-risk patients; it is reserved for diagnostic evaluation of symptomatic patients or those at increased risk.

**Q:** What does the 2016 Canadian Task Force recommend regarding colonoscopy as a screening test for colorectal cancer?

**A:** Recommend NOT using colonoscopy as a screening test for CRC (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Colonoscopy is not recommended as a primary screening tool due to lack of evidence for its effectiveness and potential for harm compared to FOBT.

**Q:** Why is colonoscopy NOT recommended as a first-line screening test for average-risk colorectal cancer?

**A:** Weak recommendation, low quality evidence against its use; colonoscopy has higher risks, resource requirements, and no clear advantage over FOBT/FIT for population screening

The Canadian Task Force recommends against colonoscopy as screening due to lack of evidence for benefit over FOBT and significant resource implications.

**Q:** Is colonoscopy recommended as a screening test for colorectal cancer?

**A:** No - recommend not using colonoscopy as a screening test for CRC (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Despite its common use, colonoscopy is not recommended as a primary screening test for average-risk colorectal cancer by the Canadian Task Force.

**Q:** What screening test does the Canadian Task Force recommend AGAINST for colorectal cancer?

**A:** Colonoscopy (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Colonoscopy is not recommended as a primary screening test for CRC due to lack of evidence and resource implications.

**Q:** According to the 2016 Canadian Task Force guidelines, should colonoscopy be used for average-risk colorectal cancer screening?

**A:** No - colonoscopy is not recommended as a screening test for CRC (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Colonoscopy is not recommended for average-risk patients due to low quality evidence showing lack of benefit.

**Q:** What is the recommended colorectal cancer screening for a patient with a first-degree relative diagnosed with colorectal cancer?

**A:** Colonoscopy starting at age 50, or 10 years earlier than the relative's age at diagnosis, whichever occurs first

Patients with one or more FDRs with CRC should undergo colonoscopy. Take a complete family history and refer for genetic assessment if hereditary syndrome suspected.

**Q:** List 4 categories in the differential diagnosis of fever

**A:** Infection, Cancer, Medications, Other (e.g., inflammatory bowel disease, collagen vascular disease)

Fever has broad etiologies. Common medication causes include allopurinol, nifedipine, captopril, diuretics, and antihistamines.

**Q:** What is the recommended screening approach for colorectal cancer in patients with a first-degree relative with colorectal cancer?

**A:** Colonoscopy starting at age 50, or 10 years earlier than the relative's age at diagnosis, whichever comes first

If hereditary cancer syndrome suspected, refer for genetic assessment. Take complete family cancer history.

**Q:** Why is colonoscopy not recommended as a primary screening test for colorectal cancer in average-risk patients?

**A:** Low quality evidence does not support its use as a screening test for CRC in average-risk populations

The Canadian Task Force gives a weak recommendation against colonoscopy for average-risk screening due to low quality evidence.

**Q:** What are the 4 main categories in the differential diagnosis of fever?

**A:** Infection, Cancer, Medications, and Other (e.g., inflammatory bowel disease, collagen vascular disease)

This framework helps systematically consider causes of fever in clinical assessment.

**Q:** Which screening test does the Canadian Task Force recommend NOT using for colorectal cancer screening?

**A:** Colonoscopy (weak recommendation, low quality evidence)

Despite its common use, colonoscopy is not recommended as a screening test for CRC by the Canadian Task Force.

**Q:** A patient has a first-degree relative diagnosed with colorectal cancer at age 45. When should screening begin and what test should be used?

**A:** Colonoscopy starting at age 50, or 10 years earlier than relative's diagnosis (age 35), whichever occurs first

Patients with one or more first-degree relatives with CRC should undergo colonoscopy beginning at age 50 or 10 years before the relative's age at diagnosis.

**Q:** Which of the following is NOT a recommended test for colorectal cancer screening in average-risk patients: FOBT, flexible sigmoidoscopy, or colonoscopy?

**A:** Colonoscopy is NOT recommended for average-risk screening

For patients at average risk, colonoscopy is not recommended as a screening test for colorectal cancer.

**Q:** What are the four categories in the differential diagnosis of fever?

**A:** Infection, Cancer, Medications, and Other (e.g., inflammatory bowel disease, collagen vascular disease)

Fever has multiple etiologies spanning infectious, malignant, pharmacologic, and inflammatory causes.

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be considered in IBS?

**A:** If alarm features present, family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or age >50

Colonoscopy with random biopsies if normal appearance helps rule out microscopic colitis and other organic colonic disease.

**Q:** What test is likely more reliable than celiac serology to rule out IBD in suspected IBS?

**A:** Fecal calprotectin

Fecal calprotectin is a marker of intestinal inflammation and is more reliable for ruling out IBD than celiac serology.

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be considered in suspected IBS?

**A:** When alarm features are present, family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or age >50 years

Colonoscopy is not routine for IBS but indicated when there are red flags or increased risk for organic colonic disease.

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be considered in IBS evaluation?

**A:** Consider colonoscopy if alarm features present, family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or age >50 years. If normal, random colonic biopsies should be taken.

Colonoscopy rules out organic colonic pathology. Random biopsies can detect microscopic colitis even when the colon appears normal.

**Q:** What is the role of fecal calprotectin in IBS workup?

**A:** Fecal calprotectin is a reliable test to rule out IBD (inflammatory bowel disease) if available

Fecal calprotectin is a marker of intestinal inflammation and helps differentiate IBS (functional) from IBD (organic inflammatory disease).

**Q:** When is colonoscopy indicated in suspected IBS?

**A:** If alarm features present, family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or age >50 years

Colonoscopy with random biopsies should be performed if organic disease is suspected.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for severe traveler's diarrhea requiring treatment?

**A:** Chronic renal failure, CHF, diabetes mellitus, IBD, immunosuppression

Patients with these conditions are at higher risk for severe complications from traveler's diarrhea and may warrant more aggressive treatment.

**Q:** Which test is more reliable than CBC to rule out IBD in a patient with suspected IBS symptoms?

**A:** Fecal calprotectin

Fecal calprotectin is a marker of intestinal inflammation and is more reliable than basic blood tests for ruling out IBD in patients with IBS-like symptoms.

**Q:** When should colonoscopy with random biopsies be considered in suspected IBS?

**A:** If alarm features are present, family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or patient age >50 years

Colonoscopy is not routinely needed for IBS diagnosis but should be performed when red flag symptoms or risk factors for organic disease exist.

**Q:** What is the role of fecal calprotectin in the workup of suspected IBS?

**A:** Fecal calprotectin is used to rule out inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) and is likely more reliable than other tests in this setting

Fecal calprotectin is a marker of intestinal inflammation and helps differentiate IBS (functional) from IBD (organic inflammatory condition).

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be considered in a patient with suspected IBS?

**A:** Colonoscopy is indicated if alarm features are present, there is a family history of IBD or colorectal cancer, or patient age >50 years

If colonoscopy is performed and appears normal, random colonic biopsies should be taken to rule out microscopic colitis.

**Q:** What investigations are required for suspected IBS?

**A:** If Rome IV criteria met with no alarm symptoms and no family history of IBD/colorectal cancer: limited to CBC and celiac serology. Consider fecal calprotectin, TSH, stool cultures if indicated. Colonoscopy if alarm features, family history, or age >50.

Goal is to rule out diseases that mimic IBS (celiac disease, IBD). Minimal testing needed in typical IBS without red flags.

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be performed in constipation workup?

**A:** If red flag symptoms present (rectal bleeding, weight loss, anemia), family history of IBD, or age >50. Random colonic biopsies should be taken if colonoscopy appears normal.

Colonoscopy rules out organic causes like colorectal cancer, IBD, or microscopic colitis.

**Q:** What is the role of risankizumab in Crohn's disease based on the FORTIFY trial?

**A:** Risankizumab is effective as maintenance therapy for moderately to severely active Crohn's disease, demonstrated in the phase 3 FORTIFY maintenance trial

The FORTIFY trial (Lancet 2022) was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study showing risankizumab's efficacy for Crohn's disease maintenance

**Q:** What is the impact of inflammatory bowel disease in Canada according to the 2018 scientific report?

**A:** The 2018 impact report from the Canadian gastrointestinal epidemiology consortium to Crohn's and Colitis Canada provides comprehensive data on IBD prevalence, incidence, and burden in Canada

This scientific report (J Can Assoc Gastroenterol 2019) documents the epidemiology and effects of IBD across Canadian populations

**Q:** Which condition is more commonly associated with ulcerative colitis: PSC or PBC?

**A:** Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC)

PSC has a strong association with IBD, especially ulcerative colitis, while PBC is associated with other autoimmune disorders like Sjogren's, CREST, and RA.

**Q:** What autoimmune conditions are associated with Acute Pancreatitis?

**A:** IgG4-related disease, SLE, polyarteritis nodosa (PAN), Crohn's disease

Autoimmune pancreatitis can be part of systemic autoimmune conditions or present as isolated IgG4-related disease.

**Q:** What cutaneous manifestations are seen in inflammatory bowel disease (Crohn's/UC)?

**A:** Pyoderma gangrenosum, erythema nodosum, Sweet's syndrome (acute febrile neutrophilic dermatosis).

Pyoderma gangrenosum presents as painful ulcers with a violaceous undermined border and is more common in UC. Erythema nodosum is the most common extraintestinal skin manifestation of IBD and correlates with disease activity.

**Q:** What are the main causes of chronic secretory diarrhea?

**A:** Stimulant laxatives, post-ileal resection/cholecystectomy (bile salt malabsorption), bacterial toxins, neoplasia (e.g., VIPoma causing vasoactive intestinal peptide excess, carcinoid), Addison's disease, congenital syndromes.

Secretory diarrhea persists with fasting (unlike osmotic diarrhea which improves). VIPomas cause profuse watery diarrhea. Bile salt diarrhea after ileal resection is treated with cholestyramine. Addison's disease causes GI symptoms due to cortisol deficiency.

**Q:** What is a wireless endoscopy capsule and what is its main contraindication?

**A:** A wireless capsule endoscope is a 26 x 11 mm swallowed capsule that transmits images to a computer — used to visualize the small bowel. Main contraindication: bowel obstruction or known strictures (risk of capsule retention).

Capsule endoscopy is the most sensitive method for visualizing small bowel mucosa, especially for obscure GI bleeding and Crohn's disease assessment. It does not provide therapeutic intervention. Prior small bowel imaging to rule out strictures is recommended before use.

**Q:** What are the main causes of chronic steatorrhea?

**A:** Giardia lamblia (fat malabsorption), celiac disease, chronic pancreatitis (exocrine insufficiency), chronic cholestasis (bile salt deficiency for fat emulsification).

Steatorrhea indicates fat malabsorption. Pancreatic exocrine insufficiency (chronic pancreatitis, cystic fibrosis) impairs lipase secretion. Celiac disease damages intestinal villi reducing absorptive surface. Bile salt deficiency (cholestasis, ileal disease) impairs fat emulsification. Sudan stain and fecal fat quantification aid diagnosis.

**Q:** Which 'red flag' features make a diagnosis of IBS less likely and warrant further investigation?

**A:** Weight loss, anemia, fever, blood or pus in stool, nocturnal defecation, abnormal gross findings on endoscopy.

These alarm features suggest organic pathology (e.g., IBD, colorectal cancer, infection) rather than functional IBS. Their presence mandates investigation including colonoscopy and biopsy rather than empirical IBS management.

**Q:** What is the recommended initial investigation workup for a patient meeting Rome IV IBS criteria with no alarm features and no family history of IBD or colorectal cancer?

**A:** Limited workup: CBC and celiac serology. Fecal calprotectin (if available) is a reliable test to rule out IBD. Consider TSH and stool cultures depending on clinical circumstances. Colonoscopy only if alarm features, family history of IBD, or age >50.

The goal of limited investigation is to rule out IBS mimickers, particularly celiac disease and IBD. Extensive workup is not cost-effective in low-risk patients meeting Rome IV criteria. Fecal calprotectin is more reliable than serology alone for excluding IBD.

**Q:** What dietary intervention has the best evidence for IBS symptoms of pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols).

FODMAPs are poorly absorbed short-chain carbohydrates that are fermented by gut bacteria, producing gas and osmotic effects. A low-FODMAP diet reduces fermentable substrate and has good evidence for overall IBS symptom relief across subtypes.

**Q:** What are the pharmacological treatment options for IBS-D (diarrhea-predominant IBS)?

**A:** Increase soluble fibre (bran or psyllium), eluxadoline, rifaximin. Antispasmodics before meals (hyoscine, pinaverium, trimebutine) for pain. TCAs, SNRIs for pain. SSRIs only if anxiety/depression is the main contributor.

Rifaximin is a minimally absorbed antibiotic that targets gut flora; phase 3 trials showed it provides adequate relief of global IBS-D symptoms and bloating vs placebo. Eluxadoline is a mixed opioid receptor agonist/antagonist approved for IBS-D.

**Q:** What are the pharmacological treatment options for IBS-C (constipation-predominant IBS)?

**A:** Increase soluble fibre, PEG 3350 (alters stool form but not pain), linaclotide, plecanatide, tenapanor, osmotic or other laxatives.

Linaclotide and plecanatide are guanylate cyclase-C agonists that increase water secretion into the intestinal lumen and have both laxative and visceral analgesic effects. Tenapanor is an NHE3 (sodium-proton) inhibitor. These are preferred over osmotic laxatives for abdominal pain component.

**Q:** What is the definition of constipation, and what is the most common cause?

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (straining, sensation of anorectal blockage). Most common cause: functional/idiopathic. Among organic causes, medication side effects (especially narcotics, antidepressants) are most common.

Constipation is defined symptomatically rather than by stool frequency alone. Organic causes are unlikely if constipation is the only symptom. The mnemonic DOPED covers: Drugs, Obstruction, Pain, Endocrine dysfunction, Depression.

**Q:** List the metabolic and neurologic causes of constipation.

**A:** Metabolic: DM, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, uremia. Neurologic: intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, MS, collagen vascular disease (e.g. scleroderma), painful anal conditions (e.g. fissures).

Metabolic and neurologic causes of constipation should be considered in patients with systemic symptoms or in elderly patients. Hypothyroidism is particularly important to exclude as it is common and reversible.

**Q:** What investigation is indicated when constipation is the only presenting symptom?

**A:** CBC (per 2013 American Gastroenterology Association recommendation). Also consider TSH, calcium, glucose, and abdominal X-ray in the correct clinical context. Colonoscopy/CT colonography if red flag symptoms (rectal bleeding, weight loss, anemia) or age >50.

Underlying disease is rarely found when constipation is the sole symptom, so extensive workup is not warranted. However, CBC screens for anemia (occult bleeding or hemolytic cause), and other basic tests rule out common metabolic contributors.

**Q:** List the treatment options for constipation in order of increasing potency.

**A:** 1. Dietary fibre (30 g/day, increase slowly — useful for mild/moderate). 2. Surface-acting agents (docusate, mineral oils — limited efficacy). 3. Osmotic agents (PEG 3350, lactulose, sorbitol, magnesium salts — effective in 2–3 days). 4. Cathartics/stimulants (senna, bisacodyl — effective in 24 h). 5. Enemas/suppositories. 6. Prokinetics (prucalopride, HT4 agonist). 7. Secretagogues (linaclotide, plecanatide). 8. NHE3 inhibitors (tenapanor).

Treatment should be stepped up based on severity. Dietary fibre is ineffective for severe constipation. Prucalopride targets the 5-HT4 receptor to enhance colonic motility. Secretagogues increase luminal water secretion via guanylate cyclase-C activation.

**Q:** What are the surgical indications for Crohn's disease, and what are the key surgical principles?

**A:** Indications: failure of medical management; SBO from stricture/inflammation (50% of surgical cases); abscess, fistula (enterocolic, vesicular, vaginal, cutaneous), perforation, hemorrhage, quality of life, chronic disability, failure to thrive (children), perianal disease. Key principles: surgery is NOT curative (~70% of Crohn's patients will eventually need surgery). Resection margins only need to be free of gross disease (microscopic disease does not affect prognosis). Surgery should be elective and ideally performed off steroids.

Unlike UC, Crohn's disease recurs after resection. Conservative resection (limited margins) is preferred to preserve bowel length and avoid short gut syndrome. Stricturoplasty is used for chronically scarred bowel to relieve obstruction without resection. Contraindicated in acute inflammation. Three major patterns: ileocecal (40%), small bowel (15%), colonic (25%).

**Q:** What are the metabolic complications following terminal ileum resection in Crohn's disease?

**A:** Gallstones: decreased bile salt reabsorption → increased cholesterol precipitation. Kidney stones: diarrhea causes calcium loss in stool → increased oxalate absorption → hyperoxaluria → calcium oxalate stones. Fat malabsorption (steatorrhea): bile salt deficiency. Vitamin B12 deficiency: megaloblastic anemia (terminal ileum is the only site of active B12 absorption).

The terminal ileum is uniquely responsible for active bile salt and vitamin B12 absorption. Loss of this segment has systemic consequences beyond malabsorption. The gallstone risk is from lithogenic bile (increased cholesterol:bile acid ratio). Oxalate stones form because calcium normally binds oxalate in the gut; when calcium is lost in stool, free oxalate is absorbed in the colon.

**Q:** What are the surgical options for ulcerative colitis and when is surgery indicated?

**A:** Indications: failure of medical management (including inability to taper steroids); emergencies — hemorrhage, colonic perforation, toxic megacolon, fulminant/toxic colitis; obstruction and failure to thrive; cancer risk reduction (1-2% per year after 10 years of disease). Procedures: restorative proctocolectomy + ileal pouch-anal anastomosis (IPAA) + rectal mucosectomy (operation of choice). Total proctocolectomy + permanent end ileostomy (poor sphincter function or medically ill). Total abdominal colectomy + ileorectal anastomosis (young women wishing to preserve fertility). Emergency: total colectomy + ileostomy + Hartmann closure of rectum.

Unlike Crohn's, UC is curable with total proctocolectomy. IPAA (ileal J-pouch) preserves continence and is the preferred elective operation. Pouchitis is a common late complication. Total proctocolectomy eliminates cancer risk. Perforation of the colon is the leading cause of death in UC. Emergency surgery uses Hartmann procedure to minimize anastomotic leak risk in an acutely inflamed, unprepared colon.

## Liver Disease & Cirrhosis

**Q:** A 55-year-old alcoholic has jaundice, ascites, spider angiomata, palmar erythema, and asterixis. Labs: bilirubin 85 μmol/L, INR 2.1, albumin 22 g/L. Child-Pugh score?

**A:** Child-Pugh C (severe cirrhosis): ascites (2), encephalopathy (2), bilirubin >51 μmol/L (3), INR >2.2 (3 pts? score >10 = class C)

Child-Pugh score assesses cirrhosis severity using 5 parameters: bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, encephalopathy. Class A (5-6): compensated; Class B (7-9): moderately advanced; Class C (10-15): severely decompensated. Guides liver transplant listing.

**Q:** What is the MELD score and how is it used?

**A:** MELD = 3.78×ln(bilirubin μmol/L÷17) + 11.2×ln(INR) + 9.57×ln(creatinine μmol/L÷88.4) + 6.43; predicts 90-day mortality — used for liver transplant prioritization

MELD score was originally developed to predict mortality after TIPS. It is now used for organ allocation in liver transplantation. MELD >15 indicates benefit from transplant. MELD >25: high 90-day mortality without transplant.

**Q:** A cirrhotic patient develops increasing abdominal girth. Paracentesis shows fluid with SAAG >11 g/L. What is the diagnosis and initial management?

**A:** Ascites from portal hypertension (SAAG ≥11 g/L); first-line treatment: sodium restriction (88 mmol/day) + spironolactone 100mg ± furosemide 40mg

SAAG (serum-ascites albumin gradient) ≥11 g/L indicates portal hypertension. Spironolactone blocks aldosterone and is more effective than furosemide alone in cirrhotic ascites. AASLD/CJS guidelines: spironolactone:furosemide ratio 100:40 to maintain normokalemia.

**Q:** What is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) and how is it diagnosed and treated?

**A:** SBP: infection of ascites without apparent source; diagnose by paracentesis with PMN >250 cells/μL; treat with IV cefotaxime 2g q8h or ceftriaxone for 5-7 days + IV albumin

SBP occurs in 10-30% of hospitalized cirrhotics with ascites. Most common organism: E. coli. IV albumin (1.5 g/kg on Day 1, 1 g/kg on Day 3) reduces hepatorenal syndrome and mortality. Secondary prophylaxis: norfloxacin or TMP-SMX.

**Q:** What is hepatic encephalopathy (HE) and how is it managed?

**A:** HE: neuropsychiatric dysfunction from liver failure; manage with lactulose (goal 2-3 soft stools/day), rifaximin for secondary prophylaxis, identify and treat precipitants

Precipitants of HE: infection (SBP), GI bleeding, constipation, electrolyte abnormalities, benzodiazepines, sedatives. Lactulose reduces ammonia absorption. Rifaximin 550mg BID added for recurrent/persistent HE. Protein restriction is outdated — maintain adequate protein intake.

**Q:** A cirrhotic patient vomits large amounts of fresh blood. What is the immediate management?

**A:** Resuscitate with blood products (target Hgb 70-80 g/L), IV terlipressin or somatostatin analogue (octreotide), prophylactic antibiotics (ceftriaxone), urgent endoscopy within 12 hours

Acute variceal hemorrhage has 20-30% in-hospital mortality. Octreotide/terlipressin reduces portal pressure. Ceftriaxone prevents bacterial infections that precipitate HE and renal failure. TIPS considered for refractory bleeding. Propranolol/ISMN or EVL for secondary prophylaxis.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for alcoholic hepatitis and how is severity assessed?

**A:** Acute liver injury in heavy alcohol use: jaundice, hepatomegaly, AST:ALT ratio typically >2:1 (both usually <400 IU/L); severity: Maddrey Discriminant Function (MDF) ≥32 = severe disease

MDF = 4.6 × (PT - control time) + bilirubin (μmol/L) / 17.1. MDF ≥32 or MELD ≥21 indicates severe alcoholic hepatitis with high 30-day mortality. Treatment: prednisolone 40mg/day for 28 days if no infection. Pentoxifylline no longer recommended.

**Q:** What is non-alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD) and when does it progress to NASH?

**A:** NAFLD: hepatic steatosis without significant alcohol use; NASH (non-alcoholic steatohepatitis): steatosis + inflammation + ballooning ± fibrosis — risk of cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma

NAFLD is the most common liver disease in Canada (25% prevalence). Risk factors: obesity, T2DM, metabolic syndrome. CJS guidelines: weight loss (>7-10% body weight) is most effective treatment. No approved pharmacotherapy; semaglutide and pioglitazone show promise.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of acute liver failure in Canada and how is it managed?

**A:** Acetaminophen overdose (most common); treat with N-acetylcysteine (NAC) regardless of time since ingestion; transfer to transplant centre for King criteria assessment

NAC replenishes glutathione and prevents hepatotoxicity. Effective up to 24h; still beneficial in late presentation. King College Criteria for transplant: pH <7.30 OR all three: PT >100s, creatinine >300 μmol/L, grade III-IV encephalopathy. Call Poison Control.

**Q:** What hepatitis B serology pattern indicates acute infection vs. immunity?

**A:** Acute infection: HBsAg+, anti-HBc IgM+, anti-HBs−; Recovery/immunity (natural): HBsAg−, anti-HBc IgG+, anti-HBs+; Vaccine immunity: anti-HBs+ only (no anti-HBc)

HBV serology interpretation is essential. HBeAg indicates high viral replication. HBV DNA >2000 IU/mL = active replication. Chronic HBV: HBsAg+ >6 months. AMMI Canada guidelines recommend treatment when HBV DNA and ALT elevated with significant fibrosis.

**Q:** When should antiviral treatment be initiated for chronic hepatitis B?

**A:** HBeAg-positive: HBV DNA >20,000 IU/mL + elevated ALT; HBeAg-negative: HBV DNA >2,000 IU/mL + elevated ALT; treat regardless of ALT if cirrhosis, HCC family history, immunosuppression

AMMI Canada HBV guidelines recommend tenofovir alafenamide (TAF) or entecavir as first-line agents. Pegylated interferon for selected patients (finite duration). Monitor for HCC with ultrasound every 6 months in high-risk patients.

**Q:** A 45-year-old has positive HCV antibody and HCV RNA. What is the treatment in Canada?

**A:** Direct-acting antivirals (DAAs): sofosbuvir-based regimens (e.g., sofosbuvir/velpatasvir) or glecaprevir/pibrentasvir — 8-12 weeks, >95% sustained virological response (SVR)

CJS guidelines recommend pan-genotypic DAA therapy for all patients with chronic HCV regardless of fibrosis stage. SVR is considered virological cure and reduces liver-related mortality, cirrhosis progression, and HCC risk. Testing should be universal in Canada given high rate of undiagnosed HCV.

**Q:** What is the screening and treatment approach for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) in cirrhosis?

**A:** Ultrasound ± AFP every 6 months; if nodule >1cm on US → multi-phase CT or MRI; LI-RADS 5 = definite HCC → treat (resection, ablation, TACE, sorafenib, liver transplant based on Milan criteria)

Milan criteria for liver transplant: single nodule ≤5cm or ≤3 nodules each ≤3cm, no vascular invasion. CJS and CCA guidelines recommend HCC surveillance in all cirrhosis patients and chronic HBV carriers at high risk.

**Q:** What is hepatorenal syndrome (HRS) and how is it treated?

**A:** HRS: acute renal failure in cirrhosis from splanchnic vasodilation reducing renal perfusion; Type 1 (AKI >50% creatinine rise in <2 weeks); treat with IV albumin + vasoconstrictors (terlipressin or norepinephrine)

HRS is diagnosed by: cirrhosis with ascites, serum creatinine >133 μmol/L, no improvement with fluid resuscitation, absence of shock/nephrotoxins, no proteinuria or hematuria. Terlipressin + albumin is most effective. Liver transplantation is definitive treatment.

**Q:** What are the indications for TIPS (transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt)?

**A:** Refractory ascites, recurrent variceal hemorrhage refractory to endoscopic/pharmacological therapy, Budd-Chiari syndrome, hepatic hydrothorax

TIPS creates a shunt reducing portal pressure. Contraindications: heart failure, severe hepatic encephalopathy (HE), active sepsis. TIPS worsens HE in ~25% of patients. MELD >18 predicts poor survival post-TIPS.

**Q:** What is Child-Pugh classification?

**A:** Bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, encephalopathy. Each 1-3. A(5-6), B(7-9), C(10-15 decompensated)

Class C: 1-year mortality ~55%. MELD score preferred for transplant listing.

**Q:** How is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis managed?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 2g IV. Diagnose by ascitic PMN >250. Albumin 1.5g/kg day1 + 1g/kg day3

Common organisms: E. coli, Klebsiella. Prophylaxis: norfloxacin for prior SBP or GI bleed.

**Q:** What are indications for TIPS?

**A:** Refractory variceal bleeding, refractory ascites, hepatic hydrothorax, Budd-Chiari

Contraindications: severe encephalopathy, heart failure. Main complication: hepatic encephalopathy 30-50%.

**Q:** What is the Child-Pugh classification and how is it calculated?

**A:** 5 variables, each 1-3 points: bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, encephalopathy. Class A: 5-6 (well-compensated); Class B: 7-9 (significant functional compromise); Class C: 10-15 (decompensated). 1-year survival: A=100%, B=80%, C=45%.

Child-Pugh is used for prognosis, surgical risk assessment, and liver transplant listing. Limitations: subjective grading of ascites/encephalopathy, does not include renal function. MELD score has largely replaced Child-Pugh for transplant allocation in Canada.

**Q:** What is the MELD score, how is it calculated, and what is it used for?

**A:** MELD = 3.78×ln(bilirubin mg/dL) + 11.2×ln(INR) + 9.57×ln(creatinine mg/dL) + 6.43. Range 6-40. Used for liver transplant allocation (higher score = higher priority) and 3-month mortality prediction. MELD-Na includes sodium (improves accuracy).

MELD is the standard for organ allocation in Canada (Canadian Blood Services). MELD >15: consider transplant referral. MELD >20: transplant listing. Exception points given for HCC, hepatopulmonary syndrome. MELD-Na better predicts mortality in hyponatremic patients.

**Q:** What is the management of acute variceal bleeding?

**A:** Resuscitate (target Hb 70-80 g/L), IV octreotide (bolus 50 μg then 50 μg/h x 2-5 days), IV ceftriaxone 1g daily x 7 days prophylaxis, emergent endoscopy within 12h for band ligation. TIPS if refractory.

Restrictive transfusion (Hb target 70) improves survival by avoiding portal pressure increase. Antibiotic prophylaxis reduces mortality and rebleeding. PPI is NOT indicated for variceal bleeding. Pre-emptive TIPS within 72h for Child-Pugh C or B with active bleeding on endoscopy reduces mortality.

**Q:** What is the primary and secondary prophylaxis for esophageal varices?

**A:** Primary prophylaxis: NSBB (propranolol/nadolol, target HR 55-60) OR carvedilol 6.25-12.5 mg daily for medium/large varices. Secondary prophylaxis: combination NSBB + endoscopic band ligation (EBL) until varix obliteration.

Screen with EGD at cirrhosis diagnosis. Small varices without red signs: NSBB or surveillance. Carvedilol has both beta-blocking and alpha-blocking effects, reducing portal pressure more effectively. NSBB contraindicated in refractory ascites with SBP <90 or AKI.

**Q:** How is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) diagnosed and treated?

**A:** Diagnosis: ascitic fluid PMN >250 cells/mm³. Treatment: IV ceftriaxone 2g daily (or cefotaxime 2g q8h) x 5 days + IV albumin 1.5 g/kg day 1, 1 g/kg day 3 (if Cr >88 μmol/L, BUN >10.7 mmol/L, or bilirubin >68 μmol/L).

SBP occurs in ~15% of cirrhotics with ascites. Most common organisms: E. coli, Klebsiella, S. pneumoniae. IV albumin reduces hepatorenal syndrome and mortality. Secondary prophylaxis: norfloxacin 400 mg daily or TMP-SMX indefinitely. Culture-negative SBP (PMN>250, negative culture) treated identically.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic approach to ascites including the serum-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG)?

**A:** SAAG ≥11 g/L: portal hypertension (cirrhosis, heart failure, Budd-Chiari). SAAG <11 g/L: non-portal hypertension (peritoneal carcinomatosis, TB peritonitis, nephrotic syndrome, pancreatitis).

SAAG replaces the old transudate/exudate classification for ascites. Total protein helps further classify: SAAG ≥11 + protein >25 g/L = cardiac ascites; SAAG ≥11 + protein <25 g/L = cirrhotic ascites. Always send cell count, culture (inoculate blood culture bottles at bedside), albumin, total protein.

**Q:** What is the management of refractory ascites in cirrhosis?

**A:** Salt restriction (<2g Na/day) + diuretics (spironolactone:furosemide 100:40 mg ratio, max 400:160). Refractory: serial large-volume paracentesis (LVP) with albumin replacement (6-8 g per L removed if >5L) or TIPS. Consider liver transplant.

Diuretic-resistant ascites (no response to max diuretics) or diuretic-intractable (complications prevent adequate doses) defines refractory ascites. TIPS improves ascites but risks encephalopathy — contraindicated if MELD >18, severe encephalopathy, or heart failure. Median survival 6 months without transplant.

**Q:** What are the grades of hepatic encephalopathy and its management?

**A:** West Haven: Grade 1 (mild confusion, short attention), Grade 2 (lethargy, disorientation), Grade 3 (somnolent but arousable, marked confusion), Grade 4 (coma). Treat with lactulose (titrate to 2-3 soft stools/day) + rifaximin 550 mg BID for secondary prophylaxis.

HE is caused by ammonia and other toxins bypassing hepatic clearance. Precipitants: GI bleeding, infection, constipation, dehydration, sedatives, hypokalemia, alkalosis. Lactulose (first-line) acidifies colon lumen trapping NH4+. Rifaximin reduces ammonia-producing bacteria and reduces HE recurrence by 50%.

**Q:** What is hepatorenal syndrome (HRS) and how is it classified and treated?

**A:** HRS-AKI (formerly Type 1): rapid Cr rise ≥26.5 μmol/L in 48h or ≥50% in 7 days, progressive renal failure. HRS-CKD (Type 2): stable moderate renal failure. Treatment: IV albumin + terlipressin (first-line) or norepinephrine + albumin. Definitive: liver transplant.

HRS is functional renal failure due to splanchnic vasodilation and renal vasoconstriction — kidneys are structurally normal. Must exclude other causes: no improvement with albumin challenge (1g/kg x 2 days), no nephrotoxins, no obstruction, no parenchymal disease. Terlipressin is now approved in Canada.

**Q:** What is the workup for newly diagnosed cirrhosis?

**A:** Etiology: hepatitis B/C serology, alcohol history, ferritin/transferrin sat (hemochromatosis), ceruloplasmin (Wilson), ANA/ASMA/IgG (autoimmune), AMA (PBC). Assess severity: bloodwork (CBC, INR, albumin, bilirubin, Cr), EGD for varices, US for HCC screening, MELD score.

Liver biopsy may not be needed if diagnosis is clear clinically. Transient elastography (FibroScan) is non-invasive assessment of fibrosis. HCC screening: abdominal US ± AFP every 6 months. Bone density screening (osteoporosis risk). Vaccinate for HepA/B if non-immune.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for alcohol-associated hepatitis?

**A:** Heavy alcohol use (>40 g/day women, >60 g/day men) for >6 months with <60 days abstinence, jaundice onset <8 weeks, AST >50 (AST:ALT ratio >1.5), bilirubin >50 μmol/L. Maddrey discriminant function ≥32: severe, consider prednisolone 40 mg x 28 days.

Maddrey DF = 4.6 × (PT patient - PT control) + bilirubin (mg/dL). MELD >20 also predicts poor prognosis. Lille score at day 7 assesses steroid response: >0.45 = non-responder, stop steroids. Pentoxifylline is no longer recommended (STOPAH trial). Contraindications to steroids: active GI bleeding, uncontrolled infection.

**Q:** What is the approach to HCC screening and management in cirrhosis?

**A:** Screen with abdominal ultrasound ± AFP every 6 months. Suspicious lesion >1 cm: contrast-enhanced CT or MRI (arterial enhancement + washout = diagnostic). Management: BCLC staging → surgical resection, liver transplant (Milan criteria: single ≤5cm or ≤3 nodules each ≤3cm), ablation, TACE, sorafenib/atezolizumab-bevacizumab.

Milan criteria define transplant eligibility for HCC. Beyond Milan: some centres use UCSF criteria. First-line systemic therapy for advanced HCC: atezolizumab + bevacizumab (IMbrave150 trial). HCC screening applies to all cirrhotics and chronic HBV carriers even without cirrhosis.

**Q:** What are the features and management of non-alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD/MASLD)?

**A:** Spectrum: steatosis → steatohepatitis (NASH/MASH) → fibrosis → cirrhosis. Risk factors: metabolic syndrome, obesity, T2DM. Diagnosis: exclude alcohol (>20g/day women, 30g/day men) and other causes. FibroScan or FIB-4 for fibrosis assessment. Management: weight loss ≥7-10%, exercise, treat metabolic syndrome. Pioglitazone or vitamin E for biopsy-proven NASH without diabetes.

NAFLD is the most common liver disease globally. Renamed MASLD (metabolic-associated steatotic liver disease) in 2023. Resmetirom (thyroid hormone receptor agonist) is FDA-approved for NASH with fibrosis. GLP-1 agonists (semaglutide) show benefit. Bariatric surgery improves NASH in obese patients.

**Q:** What drugs should be avoided or dose-adjusted in cirrhosis?

**A:** Avoid: NSAIDs (renal, bleeding), ACEi/ARBs (hypotension, renal), aminoglycosides, metformin (in decompensated), sedatives/opioids (encephalopathy). Dose-adjust: acetaminophen (max 2g/day), statins (monitor closely). Prefer: lactulose, rifaximin, spironolactone.

NSAIDs cause renal vasoconstriction (reduce prostaglandin-mediated afferent dilation) worsening ascites and precipitating HRS. ACEi/ARBs further reduce effective circulating volume. Acetaminophen in reduced doses is safer than NSAIDs for pain. Avoid gadolinium MRI contrast if GFR <30 (NSF risk).

**Q:** What are the indications and contraindications for TIPS (transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt)?

**A:** Indications: refractory variceal bleeding despite endoscopic therapy, refractory ascites, Budd-Chiari syndrome, hepatic hydrothorax. Contraindications: severe hepatic encephalopathy, heart failure (right-sided), MELD >18 (relative), hepatic malignancy, uncontrolled sepsis.

TIPS creates a portosystemic shunt reducing portal pressure. Main complication is hepatic encephalopathy (30-50%). Covered stents (PTFE) have better patency. Pre-emptive TIPS within 72h of variceal bleeding in high-risk patients (Child B with active bleeding or Child C 10-13) reduces mortality.

**Q:** How do you interpret liver function tests (LFTs)?

**A:** Hepatocellular: ALT>AST (viral hepatitis), AST>ALT 2:1 (alcoholic). Cholestatic: elevated ALP+GGT+bilirubin (obstructive). Synthetic: low albumin, elevated INR (chronic liver disease)

Isolated elevated ALP: check GGT (if normal, bone source). Very high ALT (>1000): acute viral hepatitis, ischemic hepatitis, toxin (acetaminophen). Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia = post-hepatic obstruction.

**Q:** A newborn presents with jaundice on day 1 of life. Indirect (unconjugated) bilirubin is markedly elevated. What is the most likely category of jaundice, the most important diagnosis to consider, and the urgency?

**A:** Pre-hepatic (hemolytic) jaundice. The most important diagnosis is hemolytic disease of the newborn (Rh or ABO incompatibility). This is urgent because severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia can cause kernicterus (bilirubin encephalopathy). Immediate workup includes blood type, Coombs test, CBC with reticulocyte count, and peripheral smear.

Jaundice within the first 24 hours of life is ALWAYS pathological and requires urgent evaluation. Physiological jaundice does not appear until day 2-3. Pre-hepatic causes produce unconjugated (indirect) hyperbilirubinemia because the liver cannot conjugate the excess bilirubin load from hemolysis.

**Q:** How do you classify jaundice as pre-hepatic, hepatic, or post-hepatic using bilirubin fractions, urine, and stool findings?

**A:** Pre-hepatic: elevated unconjugated bilirubin, normal/dark urine urobilinogen, dark stool, no bilirubinuria. Hepatic: elevated both conjugated and unconjugated, bilirubinuria present, variable urine urobilinogen. Post-hepatic (obstructive): elevated conjugated bilirubin, bilirubinuria (dark urine/tea-colored), pale/clay-colored stool (acholic), absent urine urobilinogen.

Unconjugated bilirubin is albumin-bound and cannot be filtered by the kidneys, so bilirubinuria only occurs with conjugated hyperbilirubinemia. Clay-colored stools indicate complete biliary obstruction preventing bilirubin from reaching the gut. These simple bedside clues direct the workup efficiently.

**Q:** A 60-year-old presents with painless progressive jaundice, weight loss, and a palpable non-tender gallbladder. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Pancreatic head cancer (periampullary carcinoma). The palpable non-tender gallbladder with painless jaundice is Courvoisier's sign/law, which states that painless jaundice with a palpable gallbladder is unlikely to be due to gallstones (which cause a fibrosed, contracted gallbladder) and more likely malignant obstruction.

Courvoisier's sign has limited sensitivity but reasonable specificity for malignant biliary obstruction. Initial workup includes abdominal ultrasound followed by CT pancreas protocol or MRCP. CA 19-9 is a tumor marker that supports but does not confirm the diagnosis. ERCP with stenting may be needed for biliary decompression.

**Q:** What are the key laboratory patterns that differentiate hepatocellular jaundice from obstructive (cholestatic) jaundice?

**A:** Hepatocellular: markedly elevated AST/ALT (transaminases, often >1000 in acute hepatitis), moderately elevated ALP/GGT, elevated conjugated and unconjugated bilirubin, prolonged PT (not corrected by vitamin K). Cholestatic/obstructive: markedly elevated ALP/GGT (>3x normal), mild-moderate AST/ALT elevation, elevated conjugated bilirubin, prolonged PT (corrected by vitamin K since fat-soluble vitamin malabsorption is the cause).

The AST/ALT vs ALP pattern is the critical discriminator. In obstructive jaundice, PT prolongation responds to vitamin K because the coagulopathy results from malabsorption of fat-soluble vitamins due to absent bile salts. In hepatocellular disease, the liver itself cannot synthesize clotting factors, so vitamin K is ineffective.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of cirrhosis and portal hypertension?

**A:** Chronic liver injury causes fibrosis (stellate cell activation), architectural distortion, increased intrahepatic resistance, portal HTN (HVPG >5mmHg), splanchnic vasodilation, and hyperdynamic circulation

Consequences: varices (HVPG>10), ascites (HVPG>12), hepatorenal syndrome (splanchnic steal), hepatic encephalopathy (portosystemic shunting of ammonia), coagulopathy (decreased synthetic function).

**Q:** What is the SAAG and how does it classify ascites?

**A:** SAAG = serum albumin - ascitic albumin. >=11g/L: portal hypertension (cirrhosis, CHF, Budd-Chiari). <11g/L: non-portal HTN (peritoneal carcinomatosis, TB, nephrotic, pancreatitis)

Diagnostic paracentesis: cell count (PMN>250 = SBP), albumin, protein, culture. Therapeutic: large volume (>5L) with albumin replacement (6-8g per liter removed) to prevent circulatory dysfunction.

**Q:** What is the approach to chronic hepatitis C treatment?

**A:** Direct-acting antivirals (DAAs): sofosbuvir-based regimens achieve >95% SVR (cure) in 8-12 weeks. Pan-genotypic: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir or glecaprevir/pibrentasvir. Screen all born 1945-1975

CASL guidelines. No more interferon. Minimal side effects. Check: genotype, fibrosis stage (FibroScan), HBV co-infection status before treatment. SVR at 12 weeks post-treatment = cure. Reinfection possible.

**Q:** What is the current approach to screening and treating chronic hepatitis B and hepatitis C?

**A:** Hepatitis B screening: HBsAg, anti-HBs, anti-HBc; screen immigrants from endemic areas, IVDU, MSM, HIV+, household/sexual contacts. Chronic HBV treatment indications: elevated ALT + HBV DNA >2000 IU/mL (HBeAg-negative) or >20,000 (HBeAg-positive), significant fibrosis, cirrhosis regardless of ALT. Treatment: tenofovir (TDF or TAF) or entecavir — lifelong in most cases; pegylated interferon-alpha (48 weeks, finite) for select patients. Hepatitis C screening: one-time for baby boomers (born 1945-1975), IVDU, HIV+, incarcerated, recipients of blood products before 1992. Treatment: direct-acting antivirals (DAAs) — cure rate >95% in 8-12 weeks; pan-genotypic regimens: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir (Epclusa) or glecaprevir/pibrentasvir (Maviret). Treat ALL patients with chronic HCV (no longer need to wait for advanced fibrosis).

DAAs have transformed HCV from a chronic disease to a curable one. Canada has committed to WHO hepatitis elimination targets by 2030. Maviret (8-week course) is the most commonly used pan-genotypic regimen in Canada. For HBV, abrupt discontinuation of antivirals can cause severe hepatitis flares — close monitoring required. CASL (Canadian Association for the Study of the Liver) provides national hepatitis treatment guidelines.

**Q:** What is the significance of hepatitis B serologic markers: HBsAg, anti-HBs, anti-HBc IgM vs IgG, HBeAg, and HBV DNA?

**A:** HBsAg+: active infection (acute or chronic — chronic if persists >6 months). Anti-HBs+: immunity (from vaccination if isolated; from resolved infection if with anti-HBc). Anti-HBc IgM+: acute infection (or reactivation). Anti-HBc IgG+: past exposure (resolved or chronic). HBeAg+: high replication, high infectivity. Anti-HBe+: lower replication (seroconversion). HBV DNA: quantifies viral load, guides treatment decisions. Patterns: Immune (vaccinated): anti-HBs+ only. Resolved infection: anti-HBs+ AND anti-HBc+. Chronic active: HBsAg+, anti-HBc IgG+, HBeAg+ or HBeAg-, HBV DNA detectable. Occult HBV: HBsAg-, anti-HBc+, low-level HBV DNA (risk of reactivation with immunosuppression).

This is one of the most commonly tested serology interpretation topics on licensing exams. Occult HBV (isolated anti-HBc positive) is important — rituximab, chemotherapy, and other immunosuppressants can reactivate HBV, requiring prophylactic antivirals. CASL guidelines recommend HBV screening before all immunosuppressive therapy.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for hepatitis C with direct-acting antivirals (DAAs)?

**A:** Pan-genotypic regimens (treat all genotypes): Glecaprevir/pibrentasvir (Maviret): 8 weeks for treatment-naive without cirrhosis (all genotypes); 12 weeks with compensated cirrhosis. Sofosbuvir/velpatasvir (Epclusa): 12 weeks for all genotypes ± cirrhosis. Pre-treatment workup: HCV genotype (may affect regimen choice), fibrosis assessment (FIB-4 index, FibroScan, or liver biopsy), hepatic function (Child-Pugh — decompensated cirrhosis needs specialist), HBV co-infection screening (risk of HBV reactivation during DAA treatment), drug interactions (especially with HIV antiretrovirals, PPIs, statins). SVR12 (sustained virologic response at 12 weeks post-treatment) = CURE (>95%). Post-cure: if cirrhosis, continue HCC surveillance (ultrasound q6 months) — SVR does not eliminate HCC risk in cirrhotics.

DAAs have a >95% cure rate and are well-tolerated (minimal side effects compared to interferon era). All Canadian provinces now cover DAAs without fibrosis restrictions — treat all patients with chronic HCV. HBV reactivation during DAA treatment can be fatal — screen for HBV and consider prophylaxis. CASL guidelines recommend treating HCV as early as possible to prevent transmission and liver disease progression.

**Q:** What are the complications of chronic hepatitis B and when is liver transplant considered?

**A:** Complications: cirrhosis (15-40% of chronic HBV over 20 years), hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC — can occur even WITHOUT cirrhosis in HBV, unlike HCV), portal hypertension (varices, ascites, hepatic encephalopathy, hepatorenal syndrome), decompensated liver disease. HCC surveillance: abdominal ultrasound ± AFP every 6 months for: all cirrhotics, Asian males >40, Asian females >50, Africans >20, family history of HCC, HBV with high viral load. Liver transplant indications: decompensated cirrhosis (Child-Pugh ≥B7, MELD ≥15), HCC within Milan criteria (single ≤5cm or ≤3 nodules each ≤3cm, no vascular invasion, no extrahepatic disease), acute liver failure. Post-transplant: HBV recurrence prevented with HBIG + antiviral (tenofovir/entecavir) — excellent outcomes.

Unlike HCV, HBV can cause HCC without cirrhosis through direct viral oncogenesis (HBV DNA integration into hepatocyte genome). This is why HCC surveillance is recommended for non-cirrhotic HBV patients based on demographic risk. Canadian Organ Replacement Register tracks transplant outcomes — 5-year survival for liver transplant is ~75%. CASL guidelines provide detailed HCC surveillance recommendations stratified by risk.

**Q:** What clinical conditions are associated with spider angiomas due to hyperestrogenic states?

**A:** Liver disease (cirrhosis), pregnancy, and oral contraceptive pill (OCP) use.

Hyperestrogenic states increase the likelihood of developing spider angiomas, making them a physical exam finding suggestive of underlying liver disease.

**Q:** What are the risk factors/associations with Porphyria Cutanea Tarda?

**A:** Hemochromatosis, alcohol abuse, diabetes mellitus, estrogen therapy, NSAIDs, HIV, hepatitis C, and elevated iron indices

These conditions share oxidative stress and iron accumulation mechanisms that worsen uroporphyrinogen decarboxylase inhibition

**Q:** What AST/ALT ratio suggests alcohol as the cause of hepatitis?

**A:** AST/ALT ratio >2

Alcoholic hepatitis typically causes higher AST than ALT with this characteristic ratio

**Q:** What conditions are associated with gynecomastia? (4 major categories)

**A:** Drugs, congenital (Klinefelter syndrome), tumors (especially germ cell tumors), and chronic diseases (cirrhosis, CKD)

These conditions alter estrogen/androgen balance or metabolism.

**Q:** Why does cirrhosis cause gynecomastia?

**A:** Reduced ability of liver to metabolize and inactivate estrogens

Liver dysfunction leads to increased estrogen levels relative to androgens, stimulating breast tissue.

**Q:** Which STIs should individuals at increased risk be screened for?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis

These blood-borne STIs have significant morbidity and require early detection and treatment.

**Q:** What is the recommended hepatitis B vaccine indication in STI management?

**A:** Offer hepatitis B vaccine if not immune

Hepatitis B is a vaccine-preventable blood-borne STI that can be transmitted sexually.

**Q:** List the less common STIs mentioned in Toronto Notes

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These are less common than chlamydia, gonorrhea, HPV, and genital herpes but still clinically significant.

**Q:** List 4 less common STIs

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These infections have significant morbidity and require prompt treatment.

**Q:** What investigations are recommended for individuals at increased STI risk?

**A:** Screen for hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis

These bloodborne infections have significant long-term health implications if untreated.

**Q:** What are the less common STIs mentioned in Toronto Notes?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These infections are less common but have significant health implications and require specific screening.

**Q:** What STI screening is recommended for individuals at increased risk?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis screening

These blood-borne infections require specific screening in high-risk individuals.

**Q:** What STI vaccines are recommended and to whom?

**A:** Hepatitis B vaccine if not immune; Gardasil for females ages 9-27, and women ≥27 if at risk of ongoing exposure

Vaccination provides primary prevention against these infections and their complications.

**Q:** What are the STI prevention strategies through vaccination?

**A:** Offer hepatitis B vaccine if not immune; offer Gardasil to females 9-27 or women 27+ at risk

Primary prevention through vaccination is more effective than treating STI sequelae.

**Q:** What are the less common STIs in Canada?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These STIs are less common than chlamydia, gonorrhea, and HPV but still seen regularly in clinical practice.

**Q:** What STIs should individuals at increased risk be screened for?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis

These infections have significant morbidity and transmission implications, warranting routine screening in high-risk populations.

**Q:** What is the approach to primary prevention of STIs?

**A:** Vaccination (hepatitis B, HPV), education, condom use, reducing number of sexual partners

Primary prevention is more effective than treating STIs and their sequelae, including infertility and cancer.

**Q:** What less common STIs should be aware of?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis; rare: chancroid, granuloma inguinale, lymphogranuloma venereum

While less common, these infections still occur and may have serious complications if untreated.

**Q:** What less common STIs are mentioned?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These infections are less common but have significant health implications.

**Q:** What are the less common and rare STIs?

**A:** Less common: hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis; Rare: chancroid, granuloma inguinale, lymphogranuloma venereum

These STIs are less prevalent but important to recognize due to their potential complications.

**Q:** What are the screening recommendations for STIs in Canada?

**A:** Annual screening for gonorrhea and chlamydia in all sexually active people under 30; Pap test every 3 years for anyone with a cervix aged 25-69 who has ever been sexually active; individuals at increased risk should be screened for hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis

Regular screening is essential for early detection and prevention of STI complications and transmission.

**Q:** What is the approach to STI management in terms of prevention?

**A:** Primary prevention is vastly more effective than treating STIs and their sequelae; offer hepatitis B vaccine if not immune

Prevention through vaccination and counseling is more effective than treatment of established infections.

**Q:** What are the less common STIs?

**A:** Hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, trichomoniasis

These infections are less common but have significant health implications.

**Q:** What STI vaccines are available?

**A:** Hepatitis B vaccine and Gardasil (HPV vaccine)

Gardasil is indicated for females ages 9-27, and women ≥27 if at risk of ongoing exposure.

**Q:** What is the recommended hepatitis B vaccination approach?

**A:** Offer hepatitis B vaccine if not immune

Hepatitis B vaccination should be offered to all non-immune individuals as part of STI prevention.

**Q:** What are the three most common clinical manifestations of Wilson's disease affecting the liver, eyes, and CNS?

**A:** Liver: chronic active hepatitis/cirrhosis; Eyes: Kayser-Fleischer rings; CNS: basal ganglia degeneration (wing-flapping tremor, Parkinsonism)

Kayser-Fleischer rings are copper deposits in Descemet's membrane, visible via slit-lamp exam, and occur in ~50% of liver-only presentations.

**Q:** What are the three histological features of alcoholic hepatitis on liver biopsy?
