**A:** 1) Ballooned (swollen) hepatocytes; 2) Mallory bodies (eosinophilic intracellular aggregates of cytokeratins); 3) Chicken-wire fibrosis (pericellular network)

These findings distinguish alcoholic hepatitis from other causes of liver injury.

**Q:** What is the approximate percentage of individuals with alcohol use disorder who develop each stage of alcohol-related liver disease?

**A:** Steatotic liver disease: common (most); Acute alcoholic hepatitis: 35%; Cirrhosis: 10-15%

Steatosis is reversible with alcohol cessation, but cirrhosis represents permanent architectural changes.

**Q:** What is the risk of hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) in patients with hemochromatosis and cirrhosis?

**A:** 200x increased risk compared to general population

This justifies HCC screening in all patients with hemochromatosis-related cirrhosis.

**Q:** List the clinical manifestations of Wilson's disease involving the liver

**A:** Acute hepatitis, acute liver failure, chronic active hepatitis, cirrhosis; low risk of HCC

Liver involvement is the most common presentation, ranging from acute hepatitis to chronic active hepatitis and cirrhosis. Patients typically present before age 40.

**Q:** When is liver biopsy indicated in hemochromatosis?

**A:** To detect cirrhosis or if there is a potential for other causes of liver disease (e.g., NAFLD, excess EtOH, hepatitis); especially in compound heterozygotes

Biopsy is indicated when there are markers of advanced fibrosis (age >40, elevated LFTs, ferritin >1000 ng/mL) or when other liver diseases are suspected.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of hemochromatosis with treatment?

**A:** Normal life expectancy if treated before development of cirrhosis or diabetes

Early diagnosis and treatment prevent irreversible organ damage. Once cirrhosis or diabetes develops, prognosis is significantly worse.

**Q:** What is the spectrum of alcohol-related liver disease?

**A:** 1) Steatotic liver disease (reversible with abstinence), 2) Acute alcoholic hepatitis (35% of cases, often reversible), 3) Cirrhosis (10-15%, permanent architectural changes)

Alcohol-related liver disease exists on a spectrum from reversible fatty liver to irreversible cirrhosis.

**Q:** A patient with history of heavy alcohol use presents with jaundice. Labs show elevated AST > ALT (ratio >2), and biopsy shows Mallory bodies. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Alcoholic hepatitis

AST:ALT ratio >2, history of heavy alcohol use, and presence of Mallory bodies on biopsy are classic for alcoholic hepatitis.

**Q:** What is the risk of HCC in hemochromatosis with cirrhosis?

**A:** 200x increased risk compared to general population

Patients with hemochromatosis and cirrhosis require HCC screening due to this markedly elevated risk.

**Q:** When should HCC screening be initiated in hemochromatosis?

**A:** Once cirrhosis is present, screening for HCC should begin

HCC surveillance is recommended in all patients with cirrhosis, including those with hemochromatosis.

**Q:** What is the role of liver transplant in Wilson's disease?

**A:** Used in severe cases of acute liver failure or end-stage liver disease not responding to medical therapy

Liver transplant can be curative in Wilson's disease as it provides normal ATP7B function.

**Q:** What are the hepatic manifestations of Wilson's disease?

**A:** Acute hepatitis, acute liver failure (ALF), chronic active hepatitis, cirrhosis, and low risk of HCC

Liver involvement is often the initial presentation, ranging from mild hepatitis to fulminant liver failure.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of treated hemochromatosis?

**A:** Normal life expectancy if treated before development of cirrhosis or diabetes

Early detection and treatment prevents complications. Once cirrhosis or diabetes develops, prognosis is significantly worse.

**Q:** What is the spectrum of Alcohol-Related Liver Disease (ALD)?

**A:** Steatotic liver disease (reversible) → Acute alcoholic hepatitis (35%, often reversible) → Cirrhosis (10-15%, permanent architectural changes)

ALD progresses through three stages. Cirrhosis is irreversible though fibrosis may improve with alcohol cessation.

**Q:** What is the HCC risk in hemochromatosis?

**A:** 200x increased risk of hepatocellular carcinoma if cirrhosis develops

Cirrhosis from hemochromatosis carries a very high HCC risk, making surveillance essential.

**Q:** What are the late-stage manifestations of Primary Biliary Cholangitis?

**A:** Jaundice, melanosis (darkening skin), xanthelasma, hepatocellular failure, portal HTN, ascites

Chronic cholestasis leads to skin changes including melanosis and xanthelasma, with end-stage disease showing hepatocellular failure and portal hypertension.

**Q:** Does treatment with non-selective beta-blockers (propranolol or carvedilol) reduce decompensation in compensated cirrhosis patients with clinically significant portal hypertension?

**A:** Yes. Beta-blockers significantly reduce decompensation (16% vs 27% with placebo, HR 0.51, p=0.041), primarily by reducing ascites incidence (HR 0.42, p=0.03).

The PREDESCI trial (Lancet 2019) showed that lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers increases decompensation-free survival in cirrhosis patients with CSPH.

**Q:** What are the histologic features of alcoholic hepatitis?

**A:** Ballooned (swollen) hepatocytes, Mallory bodies (intracellular eosinophilic aggregates of cytokeratins), and chicken-wire fibrosis (network pattern)

These histologic findings are characteristic but not specific to alcoholic hepatitis. Mallory bodies can also be seen in other conditions.

**Q:** What is the risk of HCC in hemochromatosis patients with cirrhosis?

**A:** 200x increased risk compared to general population

Patients with cirrhosis from hemochromatosis require HCC screening. This dramatically elevated risk justifies regular surveillance.

**Q:** What are the three components of the alcohol-related liver disease spectrum?

**A:** Steatotic liver disease (reversible), Alcoholic hepatitis (35% of cases, often reversible), Cirrhosis (10-15%, chronic architectural changes)

ALD progresses from reversible fatty liver to alcoholic hepatitis, and finally to cirrhosis. The latter involves permanent architectural changes, though fibrosis may improve with abstinence.

**Q:** What are Mallory bodies in the context of alcoholic hepatitis?

**A:** Intracellular eosinophilic aggregates of cytokeratins (ballooned degenerated hepatocytes)

Mallory bodies are characteristic histologic findings in alcoholic hepatitis, representing cytoskeletal aggregates in damaged hepatocytes.

**Q:** What is 'chicken-wire fibrosis' in alcoholic liver disease?

**A:** A network-like pericellular fibrosis surrounding hepatocytes, characteristic of alcoholic hepatitis

Chicken-wire fibrosis refers to the delicate perisinusoidal fibrous network that develops around central veins in alcoholic hepatitis, representing early architectural changes.

**Q:** What was the primary endpoint in the PREDESCI trial regarding beta-blockers in cirrhosis?

**A:** Incidence of cirrhosis decompensation or death

The PREDESCI trial tested whether lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers decreases decompensation risk in compensated cirrhosis with clinically significant portal hypertension.

**Q:** What specific complication of cirrhosis was reduced by beta-blockers in the PREDESCI trial?

**A:** Ascites

The difference in the primary endpoint was driven by a reduced incidence of ascites (HR 0.42, 95% CI 0.19-0.92, P=0.03).

**Q:** What was the conclusion of the PREDESCI trial regarding beta-blocker therapy in cirrhosis with CSPH?

**A:** Long-term treatment with beta-blockers increases decompensation-free survival in patients with cirrhosis and CSPH, mainly by reducing the incidence of ascites

The trial demonstrated that beta-blockers significantly reduced the risk of decompensation or death, with a favorable safety profile (similar adverse events to placebo).

**Q:** What is the effect of beta-blockers on decompensation in cirrhosis patients with clinically significant portal hypertension (CSPH)?

**A:** Beta-blockers (propranolol/carvedilol) reduce decompensation risk from 27% to 16% (HR 0.51, P=0.041), primarily by reducing ascites incidence (HR 0.42, P=0.03)

The PREDESCI trial randomized 201 patients with compensated cirrhosis and CSPH to beta-blockers vs placebo, showing improved decompensation-free survival with treatment

**Q:** What was the primary endpoint in the PREDESCI trial for cirrhosis with CSPH?

**A:** Incidence of cirrhosis decompensation (ascites, variceal bleeding, hepatic encephalopathy) or death

The PREDESCI trial studied whether lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers prevents clinical deterioration in compensated cirrhosis patients

**Q:** What does the PREDESCI trial demonstrate about beta-blockers in cirrhosis with clinically significant portal hypertension (CSPH)?

**A:** Beta-blockers (propranolol/carvedilol) significantly reduce cirrhosis decompensation and death (16% vs 27%, HR 0.51, p=0.041), primarily by reducing ascites incidence (HR 0.42, p=0.03).

The PREDESCI trial (Lancet 2019) enrolled 201 patients with compensated cirrhosis and CSPH, showing that HVPG-lowering with beta-blockers improves decompensation-free survival.

**Q:** What was the primary endpoint of the PREDESCI trial?

**A:** Incidence of cirrhosis decompensation (ascites, variceal bleeding, hepatic encephalopathy) or death.

The PREDESCI trial measured decompensation-free survival as its primary outcome, comparing beta-blocker therapy to placebo in patients with clinically significant portal hypertension.

**Q:** What is the mechanism by which beta-blockers reduce decompensation in cirrhosis with CSPH?

**A:** Lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient (HVPG), which reduces portal pressure and prevents progression to decompensation.

Beta-blockers reduce cardiac output and splanchnic blood flow, decreasing portal venous inflow and thereby lowering HVPG, which is the pathophysiological driver of decompensation.

**Q:** What diagnostic criteria define non-alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD) according to US gastroenterology guidelines?

**A:** NAFLD is defined by hepatic steatosis in the absence of secondary causes (alcohol, medications, viral hepatitis), with NASH representing the inflammatory subset

The AGA/AASLD/ACG practice guideline (Gastroenterology 2012) establishes diagnostic criteria requiring imaging or biopsy confirmation of steatosis plus exclusion of other etiologies

**Q:** What viral infections can cause Acute Pancreatitis?

**A:** Mumps, rubella, varicella, viral hepatitis, CMV, EBV, HIV, Coxsackie virus, echovirus, adenovirus

Multiple viruses have been implicated in viral-induced pancreatitis as part of the GET SMASHED mnemonic.

**Q:** What does the PREDESCI trial demonstrate about beta-blockers in cirrhosis patients with clinically significant portal hypertension (CSPH)?

**A:** Long-term beta-blocker treatment (propranolol/carvedilol) increases decompensation-free survival, primarily by reducing ascites incidence (HR 0.42, p=0.03). Primary endpoint occurred in 16% vs 27% (HR 0.51, p=0.041).

The PREDESCI trial (Lancet 2019) studied 201 patients with compensated cirrhosis and CSPH, showing that lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers decreases decompensation risk.

**Q:** What was the primary endpoint in the PREDESCI trial?

**A:** Incidence of cirrhosis decompensation (ascites, variceal bleeding, hepatic encephalopathy) or death.

The trial aimed to determine if lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers decreases the risk of decompensation in compensated cirrhosis patients with CSPH.

**Q:** In the PREDESCI trial, what was the relative risk reduction for ascites with beta-blockers?

**A:** 58% relative risk reduction (HR 0.42, 95% CI 0.19-0.92, p=0.03)

The reduction in ascites incidence accounted for the primary difference between treatment and placebo groups, making it the main benefit of beta-blocker therapy.

**Q:** What is the primary endpoint of the PREDESCI trial?

**A:** Incidence of cirrhosis decompensation or death

The PREDESCI trial studied whether lowering hepatic venous pressure gradient with beta-blockers decreases risk of decompensation in patients with compensated cirrhosis and clinically significant portal hypertension.

**Q:** What was the primary mechanism by which beta-blockers reduced decompensation in the PREDESCI trial?

**A:** Reduced incidence of ascites (HR 0.42, 95% CI 0.19-0.92, P=0.03)

The difference in the primary endpoint was primarily due to a reduced incidence of ascites in the beta-blocker group compared to placebo.

**Q:** What is the conclusion of the PREDESCI trial regarding beta-blocker therapy in cirrhosis with CSPH?

**A:** Long-term treatment with beta-blockers increases decompensation-free survival in patients with cirrhosis and CSPH, mainly by reducing the incidence of ascites

The trial demonstrated that beta-blockers (propranolol or carvedilol) significantly reduce the risk of first decompensation event, primarily through prevention of ascites development.

**Q:** What are the exclusion criteria for Sjogren's syndrome classification?

**A:** Prior head/neck radiation, active hepatitis C (PCR-confirmed), AIDS, sarcoidosis, amyloidosis, graft-versus-host disease, or IgG4-related disease

These conditions can cause similar sicca symptoms and must be excluded.

**Q:** What skin manifestations are associated with liver disease?

**A:** Pruritus, jaundice, hyperpigmentation, spider nevi, palmar erythema, Terry's nails (white nails), porphyria cutanea tarda, xanthomas, hair loss.

Spider nevi (>5 may be pathological) and palmar erythema are signs of hyperestrogenism in cirrhosis. Porphyria cutanea tarda causes photosensitive blistering on sun-exposed skin and is associated with HCV and alcohol.

**Q:** What AST/ALT ratio suggests alcoholic hepatitis rather than other causes of hepatitis?

**A:** AST/ALT ratio >2 suggests alcohol as the cause of hepatitis.

Alcohol preferentially damages mitochondria (which are rich in AST) and depletes pyridoxine (needed for ALT synthesis), leading to a disproportionate rise in AST. In viral hepatitis, both enzymes typically rise proportionately.

**Q:** What are the common pathogens in spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP), and what is the diagnostic procedure?

**A:** Common pathogens: E. coli, Klebsiella, Streptococcus. Diagnosis: paracentesis (PMN >250 cells/mm³ is diagnostic). Albumin infusion improves outcomes in SBP patients.

SBP occurs in cirrhotic patients with ascites, where bacterial translocation from the gut leads to peritoneal infection. Paracentesis is essential for diagnosis and culture. IV albumin at the time of diagnosis reduces hepatorenal syndrome and mortality.

**Q:** What is the treatment for variceal GI bleeding in a patient with known cirrhosis?

**A:** Octreotide (somatostatin analogue) for variceal bleeds. For minor GI bleeds: PPI. For severe or recurrent bleeds: endoscopy.

Octreotide reduces splanchnic blood flow and portal pressure, decreasing variceal bleeding. Endoscopic band ligation or sclerotherapy provides definitive hemostasis. Most GI bleeds in cirrhotic patients are actually from gastritis or ulcers even if varices are known.

**Q:** What is the blood supply to the liver and what is the proportion from each source?

**A:** The liver receives blood from two sources: the portal vein (75–80%) and the hepatic artery (20–25%).

The dual blood supply makes the liver unique. The portal vein delivers nutrient-rich blood from the GI tract; the hepatic artery provides oxygenated blood. This dual supply also means the liver is relatively protected from arterial occlusion alone.

**Q:** What are the common causes of upper GI bleeding (UGIB)?

**A:** Common: peptic ulcers (H. pylori, ASA, NSAIDs), esophageal varices, Mallory-Weiss tears, erosive esophagitis, erosive gastritis. Uncommon: tumours, arteriovenous malformation (AVM), Dieulafoy's lesion, GAVE (gastric antral vascular ectasia). Rare: aorto-enteric fistulae, hemobilia.

Peptic ulcer disease is the most common cause of UGIB (~50%). Risk stratification (Rockford/Blatchford score) guides management. Prior to EGD, endotracheal intubation is indicated if massive UGIB, acidemia, or inability to protect airway.

**Q:** What are the causes of conjugated hyperbilirubinemia (jaundice)?

**A:** Hepatocellular disease: drugs, cirrhosis (any cause), hepatitis (any cause), infiltrative disease (hemochromatosis), familial disorders (Rotor syndrome, Dubin-Johnson syndrome, cholestasis of pregnancy), sepsis, post-operative/TPN. Biliary obstruction — Intraductal: gallstones, stricture, parasites, malignancy (cholangiocarcinoma), PSC, PBC. Extraductal: malignancy (pancreatic cancer, lymphoma), metastases in periportal nodes, pancreatitis.

Conjugated bilirubin is water-soluble and appears in urine (tea-coloured urine). The key distinction is hepatocellular (elevated ALT/AST) vs. cholestatic (elevated ALP/GGT) pattern. Imaging (ultrasound) is used to distinguish intra- vs. extra-hepatic obstruction.

**Q:** What are the causes of unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia?

**A:** Overproduction: hemolysis, ineffective erythropoiesis (megaloblastic anemias). Decreased conjugation: drug inhibition (e.g., chloramphenicol), Crigler-Najjar syndromes type I and II, Gilbert's syndrome, neonatal jaundice.

Unconjugated bilirubin is not water-soluble and does not appear in urine. Gilbert's syndrome is the most common inherited cause (benign, intermittent jaundice with fasting/illness). Crigler-Najjar type I is severe (requires liver transplant); type II is less severe (responds to phenobarbital).

**Q:** Define upper GI bleeding (UGIB) and list the most common etiologies in order of frequency.

**A:** Bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz (75% of GI bleeds). Etiology by frequency: gastric/duodenal ulcer (most common, ~40%), erosive gastritis (20%), esophageal varices (10–30%), Mallory-Weiss tear (10%), esophagitis, gastric cancer, Dieulafoy's lesion (rare), aortoenteric fistula (rare).

The ligament of Treitz is the suspensory ligament at the junction of the 4th portion of the duodenum and jejunum. Peptic ulcer disease remains the most common cause of UGIB. Varices carry the highest mortality per bleeding episode.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of UGIB listed in order of likely decreasing severity?

**A:** Hematochezia (brisk UGIB) > hematemesis > melena > coffee-ground emesis > occult blood in stool.

Hematochezia from an upper GI source implies brisk bleeding with rapid transit. Coffee-ground emesis indicates slower bleeding with hemoglobin oxidation in the stomach. Melena (black tarry stool) results from digestion of blood during intestinal transit.

**Q:** What is the Forrest classification for peptic ulcer bleeding, and what are the associated rebleed risks?

**A:** Class Ia: arterial bleeding (spurting) — 55–100% rebleed risk. Class Ib: oozing — 55–100%. Class IIa: visible vessel — 43%. Class IIb: sentinel clot — 22%. Class IIc: hematin-covered flat spot — 10%. Class III: no stigmata of recent hemorrhage — 5%.

The Forrest classification guides endoscopic intervention. High-risk stigmata (Ia, Ib, IIa) require endoscopic therapy. IV PPI therapy is used to stabilize clots and reduce rebleeding in patients with high-risk features.

**Q:** What is the recommended management of acute UGIB including pre-endoscopic and endoscopic steps?

**A:** 1. Stabilize: 1–2 large-bore IVs, IV fluids, NPO, monitoring. 2. Labs: CBC, cross/type, platelets, PT, PTT, electrolytes, BUN, Cr, LFTs. 3. IV PPI (to stabilize clot). 4. IV octreotide + antibiotics for suspected variceal bleed. 5. Consider IV erythromycin/metoclopramide for gastric emptying. 6. Endoscopy within 24h — epinephrine injection + thermal hemostasis (dual therapy standard of care). Hb transfusion threshold <80 g/L (higher if cardiovascular disease).

Dual endoscopic therapy (epinephrine + thermal or mechanical) outperforms single therapy. H2-antagonists are not useful as they have minimal impact on rebleeding. The restrictive transfusion strategy (threshold <80 g/L) showed better survival than liberal strategy in trials.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of UGIB from peptic ulcers, and what endoscopic predictors indicate high risk of rebleeding?

**A:** 80% stop spontaneously. Mortality is low (2%) unless rebleeding occurs (25% of patients, 10% mortality). High-risk endoscopic predictors: spurting/oozing blood, visible vessel, fibrin clot (Forrest class Ia, Ib, IIa). Patients with clean ulcer base and no comorbidities can be discharged after endoscopy.

Identifying high-risk endoscopic stigmata allows risk stratification and guides the decision to admit vs discharge. High-dose IV PPI for 72h post-endoscopy is recommended for high-risk lesions that have been treated endoscopically.

**Q:** What is the risk of variceal bleeding and rebleeding, and what is the initial management of variceal hemorrhage?

**A:** Bleeding risk: 30% in 1st year. Rebleeding risk: 50–70% (20% mortality at 6 weeks). Management: (1) Hemodynamic stabilization and resuscitation. (2) IV octreotide (splanchnic vasoconstriction, decreases portal pressure). (3) Broad-spectrum antibiotics (e.g. ceftriaxone — lowers sepsis and SBP risk, reduces rebleed rate and mortality). (4) Endoscopic variceal ligation (EVL) or sclerotherapy.

Early empirical antibiotics are strongly recommended as soon as variceal bleeding is suspected — they reduce infectious complications and rebleeding mortality. Octreotide decreases portal collateral circulation. EVL is preferred over sclerotherapy for esophageal varices.

**Q:** What is a Mallory-Weiss tear, and how is it managed?

**A:** Longitudinal laceration in the gastric mucosa on the lesser curvature near the GE junction, caused by rapid increases in gastric pressure from retching/vomiting against a closed glottis. Often associated with hiatus hernia. Classically: hematemesis following an episode of retching without blood. Management: 90% stop spontaneously; if persistent — endoscopy with epinephrine injection + clips or surgical repair.

The classical history of initial retching without blood followed by hematemesis distinguishes Mallory-Weiss tear from other UGIB causes. The tear is caused by the pressure differential during forceful vomiting. Spontaneous resolution is the rule.

**Q:** What are the causes of lower GI bleeding (LGIB), and what is the mnemonic?

**A:** Mnemonic CHAND: Colitis (radiation, infectious, ischemic, IBD — UC > CD), Hemorrhoids/fissure, Angiodysplasia, Neoplasm (polyps, post-polypectomy, cancer), Diverticular disease. Also includes vascular malformations (AVM).

Diverticular bleeding is the most common cause of significant LGIB (painless, acute hematochezia, 80% resolve spontaneously). Angiodysplasia is common in elderly patients. Hemorrhoids are the most common cause of minor rectal bleeding.

**Q:** What is the initial investigation of choice for lower GI bleeding, and when should angiography be considered?

**A:** Initial investigation of choice: colonoscopy. If bleeding is severe or ongoing, consider radionuclide imaging or angiography (requires estimated bleeding rate >0.5 cc/min for angiography). Always exclude upper GI source before localizing to lower GI tract.

Upper GI source must be excluded first as brisk UGIB can present as hematochezia. Colonoscopy identifies the bleeding site and allows therapeutic intervention. Radionuclide scan is more sensitive than angiography but cannot guide treatment.

**Q:** What are the features of diverticular bleeding?

**A:** Painless hematochezia with acute onset. Stool ranges from bright red to dark maroon; gelatinous clots often mixed in. Bleeding is most common from the right colon (thinner walled). 80% resolve spontaneously. Due to herniation of diverticula exposing vasa recta.

The painless nature differentiates diverticular bleeding from ischemic or inflammatory causes. Right-sided diverticular bleeding explains why the blood can be maroon rather than bright red. Colonoscopy rarely identifies the specific bleeding site.

**Q:** What are the main liver function tests (LFTs), what do they reflect, and how should an elevated PT/INR be interpreted?

**A:** PT/INR: hepatic protein synthesis (all coagulation factors except VIII); elevated with hepatocellular dysfunction or vitamin K deficiency. Serum albumin: hepatic protein synthesis; decreased by hepatocellular dysfunction, malnutrition, renal/GI losses, inflammation, malignancy. Direct bilirubin: hepatic excretion; increased with liver dysfunction (conjugation preserved even in end-stage liver failure). PT/INR corrects with vitamin K if deficiency is the cause, but NOT if due to hepatocellular dysfunction.

Distinguishing vitamin K deficiency from hepatocellular dysfunction via PT/INR response to vitamin K administration is clinically important. Factor VIII is synthesized in the endothelium (not just liver), so it is NOT decreased in cirrhosis — this is why bleeding risk does not correlate closely with INR in cirrhosis.

**Q:** How do you interpret a hepatocellular vs. cholestatic pattern on liver enzyme testing?

**A:** Hepatocellular: elevated ALT and AST (both elevated highly suggestive of liver injury). Cholestatic: elevated ALP and GGT (if ALP elevated alone, rule out bone disease by fractionating ALP and/or checking GGT). If ALP elevation is disproportionate to ALT/AST elevation, consider: obstruction of CBD (pancreatic cancer, stones, cholangiocarcinoma, sclerosing cholangitis), destruction of microscopic ducts (PBC), bile acid transporter defects (drugs, intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy), liver infiltration (metastases, lymphoma, granulomas, amyloid).

GGT is used to confirm hepatic origin of elevated ALP (as ALP also comes from bone, placenta, intestine). ALT is more liver-specific than AST. In acute biliary obstruction from a stone, a hepatocellular pattern may predominate initially as ALP is an inducible enzyme that takes time to rise.

**Q:** What causes transaminases >1000 U/L?

**A:** Viral hepatitis, drugs/toxins (especially acetaminophen overdose), autoimmune hepatitis, hepatic ischemia ('shock liver'), and less commonly, acute biliary obstruction from a common bile duct stone.

Markedly elevated transaminases (>1000) typically indicate acute hepatocellular necrosis. Alcoholic hepatitis usually causes a more modest rise (typically AST <300) with AST:ALT ratio >2:1. The pattern helps narrow the differential.

**Q:** What is the AST:ALT ratio pattern in alcoholic hepatitis vs. other hepatitis causes?

**A:** ALT > AST: most causes of hepatitis (viral, autoimmune, drug-induced, MASLD). AST > ALT (ratio typically >2:1): alcoholic liver disease or other causes that have progressed to advanced cirrhosis. AST in alcoholic hepatitis is usually <300 U/L.

The AST:ALT ratio >2 in alcoholic hepatitis is due to alcohol-induced mitochondrial damage (where AST localizes) and pyridoxine deficiency (which is required for ALT synthesis more than AST). This pattern is a useful clinical clue.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of acute viral hepatitis?

**A:** Most are subclinical. Flu-like prodrome 1–2 weeks before jaundice: N/V, anorexia, headaches, fatigue, myalgia, low-grade fever, arthralgia, urticaria. Only some progress to icteric phase (lasting days to weeks): pale stools and dark urine 1–5 days prior to jaundice, hepatomegaly with RUQ pain, splenomegaly and cervical lymphadenopathy (10–20%).

The prodrome reflects systemic immune activation. Pale stools (acholic) and dark urine indicate conjugated hyperbilirubinemia (intrahepatic cholestasis). Not all patients develop jaundice — subclinical infection is common, especially in children.

**Q:** What investigations are done in acute viral hepatitis and what are poor prognostic indicators?

**A:** Investigations: AST and ALT (>10–20x normal in hepatocellular necrosis), ALP minimally elevated, viral serology (anti-HAV IgM, anti-HBc IgM). Poor prognostic indicators: comorbidities, persistently high bilirubin (>340 mmol/L), increased INR, decreased albumin, hypoglycemia. Indications for hospitalization: encephalopathy, coagulopathy, severe vomiting, hypoglycemia.

Markedly elevated bilirubin, prolonged INR, and hypoglycemia reflect failing hepatic synthetic function. Coagulopathy in the context of acute hepatitis implies significant hepatocellular necrosis and risk of acute liver failure.

**Q:** What are the key features of Hepatitis A Virus (HAV)?

**A:** RNA picornavirus. Fecal-oral transmission. Incubation 4–6 weeks. Diagnosed by elevated transaminases + positive anti-HAV IgM. In children: characteristically asymptomatic. In adults: fatigue, nausea, arthralgia, fever, jaundice, hepatomegaly. Can cause ALF and death (<1–5%). Can relapse (rarely) but NEVER becomes chronic. Treatment: supportive. Preventable by vaccination (Havrix, 2 doses q6mo, or Twinrix combined). Post-exposure prophylaxis available.

HAV's fecal-oral transmission makes it endemic in areas with poor sanitation. Unlike HBV and HCV, HAV never becomes chronic. The vaccine is recommended for travellers to endemic regions and high-risk groups.

**Q:** Interpret the Hepatitis B serology pattern for: (a) Acute HBV, (b) Chronic HBe-Ag positive HBV, (c) Chronic HBe-Ag negative HBV, (d) Resolved infection, (e) Immunization.

**A:** (a) Acute HBV: HBsAg+, anti-HBs−, HBeAg+, anti-HBe−, anti-HBc IgM+. (b) Chronic HBeAg+: HBsAg+, anti-HBs−, HBeAg+, anti-HBe−, anti-HBc IgG+. (c) Chronic HBeAg−: HBsAg+, anti-HBs−, HBeAg−, anti-HBe+, anti-HBc IgG+. (d) Resolved: HBsAg−, anti-HBs+, HBeAg−, anti-HBe+, anti-HBc IgG+. (e) Immunization: HBsAg−, anti-HBs+, HBeAg−, anti-HBe−, anti-HBc−.

Anti-HBc is the key marker distinguishing resolved infection from immunization (both have anti-HBs+). Anti-HBc IgM indicates acute infection. HBsAg positivity defines current infection. Anti-HBs is the protective antibody.

**Q:** Describe the 4 phases of chronic hepatitis B infection.

**A:** 1. Immune tolerance: very high HBV-DNA (>20,000 IU/mL), HBeAg+, normal ALT/AST; minimal liver damage; characteristic of perinatal infection. 2. Immune clearance (immunoactive): high HBV-DNA (>20,000 IU/mL), HBeAg+; immune attack causes liver damage, progressive fibrosis — benefits from treatment. 3. Chronic inactive (immune control): low HBV-DNA (<2,000 IU/mL), HBeAg−, anti-HBe+, normal ALT/AST; risk of reactivation with immunosuppression. 4. HBeAg-negative chronic hepatitis (immune escape): elevated HBV-DNA (>2,000 IU/mL), HBeAg− (pre-core mutant), anti-HBe+, elevated ALT/AST; progressive fibrosis — benefits from treatment.

Phases 2 and 4 require treatment. Phase 3 patients need monitoring for reactivation, especially if receiving immunosuppressive therapy (e.g. corticosteroids, chemotherapy, biologics). Not all carriers progress through all phases.

**Q:** What are the indications for pharmacological treatment of chronic hepatitis B, and what drugs are used?

**A:** Indications: (1) HBeAg+ with HBV-DNA >20,000 IU/mL and elevated ALT. (2) HBeAg− with HBV-DNA >2,000 IU/mL and elevated ALT ± stage ≥2 fibrosis. (3) Before immunosuppressive therapy (steroids, chemotherapy, biologics) to prevent flare. Treatment drugs: tenofovir (preferred), entecavir (preferred), lamivudine (not preferred — high resistance rate). Treatment goal: undetectable serum HBV-DNA.

Tenofovir and entecavir have high barrier to resistance and are first-line. Reducing HBV-DNA to undetectable levels reduces risk of cirrhosis, HCC, and liver-related death. HAV vaccination is also recommended if serology is negative.

**Q:** Who should receive HCC surveillance in the context of hepatitis B, and what is the recommended test and interval?

**A:** Ultrasound (U/S) every 6 months, especially for patients with: high serum HBV-DNA levels, cirrhosis, male sex, Asian men age >40, Asian women age >50, African descent age >20.

HCC risk in HBV increases with age (surrogate for progressive fibrosis/cirrhosis) and with serum HBV-DNA levels. HCV and HBV treatment both lower HCC risk. Surveillance U/S allows detection at an early, potentially curable stage.

**Q:** What is Hepatitis D virus (HDV), and what is the difference between coinfection and superinfection?

**A:** HDV is a defective RNA virus requiring HBsAg for hepatocyte entry — it only infects patients with HBV. Coinfection: acquire HDV and HBV simultaneously (increased acute severity but HDV does NOT increase risk of chronic hepatitis). Superinfection: new HDV acquisition in a patient with chronic HBV (increases progression to cirrhosis, can cause ALF). Treatment: low-dose interferon (20% response) and liver transplant for end-stage disease.

The distinction between coinfection and superinfection is clinically important: superinfection has a worse prognosis because the patient already has established chronic HBV infection, allowing HDV to cause accelerated disease progression.

**Q:** What are the key features of Hepatitis C virus (HCV) including transmission, diagnosis, and treatment?

**A:** RNA virus, 7 genotypes (genotype 1 most common in North America). Blood-borne transmission; sexual transmission is 'inefficient'. Major risk factor: injection drug use. Other risks: blood transfusion before 1992, tattoos, intranasal cocaine use. Diagnosis: elevated ALT/AST + anti-HCV (screening), confirmed by detectable HCV-RNA in serum. Note: normal enzymes do NOT rule out active disease or advanced fibrosis. Treatment: direct-acting antivirals (DAAs) — glecaprevir/pibrentasvir (Maviret) or sofosbuvir/velpatasvir (Epclusa); check for HBV before starting DAAs (risk of HBV reactivation).

HCV is now curable in >95% of patients with 8–12 weeks of pan-genotypic DAA therapy. Importantly, normal liver enzymes can occur with chronic HCV, so clinical suspicion should drive serology testing regardless of enzyme levels.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of Hepatitis C and what extrahepatic complications can occur?

**A:** 70–80% of acute HCV becomes chronic; of these, 20% develop cirrhosis within 20 years. Risk of HCC if cirrhotic. Extrahepatic: cryoglobulinemia, membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis, B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma.

HCV is unique among hepatitis viruses in that it is often asymptomatic in the acute phase, leading to undiagnosed chronicity. The extrahepatic manifestations reflect immune complex deposition and B-cell dysregulation driven by chronic viral infection.

**Q:** What is autoimmune hepatitis (AIH), what are the key diagnostic features, and how is it treated?

**A:** Chronic inflammatory liver disease from loss of tolerance to liver antigens. Diagnosis of exclusion (rule out viral, drug/alcohol, metabolic, genetic causes). 40% mortality at 6 months without treatment. Key features: hypergammaglobulinemia (elevated IgG), positive ANA and/or anti-smooth muscle antibody (SMA), anti-LKM (especially in children), false-positive anti-HCV, biopsy showing periportal (zone 1) interface inflammation and necrosis. Extrahepatic: sicca, Raynaud's, thyroiditis, Sjogren's, arthralgias. Treatment: corticosteroids (80% respond) + azathioprine (prevents relapse during steroid taper).

AIH can overlap with PSC and PBC. Azathioprine is added to allow steroid tapering — most patients relapse if steroids are withdrawn without azathioprine. Anti-LKM is more common in children (Type 2 AIH) while ANA/SMA characterize Type 1 AIH.

**Q:** What are the two types of hepatotoxins (predictable vs. idiosyncratic), and give examples of each?

**A:** Predictable (intrinsic): dose-dependent, short latency (hours to days), host factors not important. Examples: acetaminophen, CCl4. Idiosyncratic: not dose-dependent, longer latency (weeks to months), host factors very important. Examples: phenytoin, isoniazid (INH).

Most drug-induced liver injury is idiosyncratic, making it unpredictable. Acetaminophen is the major exception — it causes predictable hepatotoxicity above threshold doses (10–15 g in healthy individuals, 4–6 g in those with depleted glutathione from alcohol use disorder, malnutrition, or anticonvulsant use).

**Q:** What are the key drug-induced liver injury patterns for isoniazid (INH), methotrexate (MTX), chlorpromazine, and amiodarone?

**A:** INH: 20% develop elevated transaminases, <1% clinically significant disease; susceptibility increases with age (idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity). MTX: causes fibrosis/cirrhosis; risk increased with obesity, DM, alcoholism (pre-existing fatty liver); develops without symptoms or enzyme changes — may need biopsy in long-term use. Chlorpromazine: cholestasis in 1% after 4 weeks, often with fever, rash, jaundice, pruritus, eosinophilia. Amiodarone: can cause same histology and clinical outcome as alcoholic hepatitis.

Antimicrobials are the most common overall class causing drug-induced liver injury (amoxicillin-clavulanate, INH, nitrofurantoin, TMP-SMX). MTX hepatotoxicity is silent — routine LFTs are unreliable for monitoring; cumulative dose guides decision for biopsy.

**Q:** Compare the hepatitis viruses: which have RNA vs DNA genomes, which are fecal-oral vs parenteral, and which cause chronic infection?

**A:** DNA: HBV. RNA: HAV, HCV, HDV, HEV, CMV (DNA), EBV (DNA). Fecal-oral: HAV, HEV. Parenteral/sexual: HBV, HCV, HDV. Chronicity: None (HAV, HEV). HBV: 5% adults, 90% infants. HCV: 70–80%. HDV: 5%. CMV/EBV: common latent infections.

The route of transmission drives prevention strategies. HAV and HEV prevention relies on hygiene and vaccination; HBV/HCV/HDV requires blood and body fluid precautions. Chronicity risk is inversely related to age at HBV acquisition (reflects immune maturity).

**Q:** What are the unique features of Hepatitis E virus (HEV)?

**A:** RNA calicivirus. Fecal-oral transmission. Endemic in Africa, Asia, Central America, India, Pakistan. Incubation 2–8 weeks. Acute onset. Does NOT cause chronic infection. Mortality: 1–2% overall, but 10–20% in pregnancy (third trimester). Serology: anti-HEV IgG/IgM. No vaccine available.

HEV is particularly dangerous in pregnancy, especially in the third trimester, where it can cause fulminant hepatic failure with up to 20% mortality. This makes it an important consideration in pregnant travellers returning from endemic regions.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic tests used for specific liver diseases (quick reference)?

**A:** Viral hepatitis: anti-HAV IgM, HBsAg, anti-HCV. Autoimmune hepatitis: ANA, SMA (anti-smooth muscle antibody), anti-LKM, IgG levels. Wilson's disease: ceruloplasmin, liver biopsy. Hereditary hemochromatosis: iron saturation, ferritin, HFE genetic testing. Alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency: alpha-1-antitrypsin levels, genotyping, liver biopsy. Drug-induced liver injury: careful medication history, liver biopsy. MASLD: uric acid, metabolic testing, liver biopsy after excluding other causes.

The diagnostic approach to liver disease starts with identifying the pattern (hepatocellular vs. cholestatic) then using targeted serologic tests. Liver biopsy remains the gold standard for fibrosis staging but non-invasive tests (FibroScan) are increasingly used.

**Q:** What is the DDx for hepatomegaly?

**A:** Congestive: right heart failure, Budd-Chiari syndrome. Malignant: primary (HCC), secondary (metastases), lymphoproliferative disease, leukemia. Benign: fatty liver, cysts, hemochromatosis, extramedullary hematopoiesis, amyloid. Infectious: viral hepatitis, tuberculosis, abscess, echinococcus. Inflammatory: granulomas (sarcoidosis), histiocytosis X.

A systematic approach to hepatomegaly uses CMIBI categories. Congestive hepatomegaly from right heart failure often presents with tender hepatomegaly and elevated JVP. Budd-Chiari (hepatic vein thrombosis) can present acutely with RUQ pain and ascites.

**Q:** What is the approach to iron deficiency anemia with a GI source, and when is wireless capsule endoscopy indicated?

**A:** If overt GI bleeding (hematochezia or melena): upper and lower endoscopy. Rule out non-GI sources (menorrhagia, hemolysis). If source found — treat. If no source found: wireless endoscopy capsule or double balloon endoscopy for small bowel. If overt bleeding is hemodynamically significant (estimated >0.5 cc/min): angiography. CT enterography if capsule/double balloon not available.

Wireless capsule endoscopy is the investigation of choice for suspected small bowel bleeding after negative upper and lower endoscopy. It helps localize the lesion to guide double balloon enteroscopy for therapeutic intervention.

**Q:** What is the venous drainage of the GI tract, and where does it ultimately drain?

**A:** GI venous blood drains into the portal vein, which receives:
- Superior mesenteric vein (SMV): drains small bowel and right colon
- Splenic vein: drains spleen, stomach; receives the inferior mesenteric vein (IMV) which drains left colon and rectum
- Left gastric (coronary) vein, right gastric vein, cystic vein, and paraumbilical veins
The portal vein drains to the liver via the hepatic sinusoids, then to hepatic veins → inferior vena cava.

Portal hypertension causes reversal of flow in portosystemic anastomoses (e.g., esophageal varices from left gastric-azygos, caput medusae from paraumbilical veins, anorectal varices from superior rectal-middle/inferior rectal). Understanding portal venous anatomy is essential for managing variceal bleeding and planning liver surgery.

**Q:** How do you differentiate hepatocellular jaundice from obstructive (cholestatic) jaundice biochemically?

**A:** Hepatocellular jaundice: elevated bilirubin + elevated ALT/AST
Obstructive/cholestatic jaundice: elevated bilirubin + elevated ALP/GGT + duct dilatation on biliary ultrasound
Hemolytic jaundice: decreased haptoglobin + elevated LDH (indirect hyperbilirubinemia)

This biochemical differentiation guides further investigation. Cholestatic jaundice with bile duct dilatation on ultrasound suggests mechanical obstruction (stone, stricture, malignancy) and warrants ERCP or MRCP. Hepatocellular pattern with elevated transaminases suggests viral hepatitis, drug toxicity, or alcoholic hepatitis.

**Q:** What is the PREDESCI trial and its key findings regarding beta-blockers in cirrhosis?

**A:** PREDESCI (Lancet 2019): randomized double-blind placebo-controlled trial of 201 patients with compensated cirrhosis and clinically significant portal hypertension (CSPH).
Intervention: propranolol or carvedilol vs. placebo
Result: Primary endpoint (decompensation or death) occurred in 16% of beta-blocker group vs. 27% placebo (HR 0.51, 95% CI 0.26-0.97, p=0.041)
Effect driven by: reduced incidence of ascites (HR 0.42, p=0.03)
Conclusion: Long-term beta-blocker treatment increases decompensation-free survival in compensated cirrhosis with CSPH, mainly by reducing ascites.

Clinically significant portal hypertension (HVPG ≥10 mmHg) in compensated cirrhosis predicts decompensation. Beta-blockers (especially carvedilol) reduce portal pressure by decreasing cardiac output (β1) and splanchnic vasoconstriction (β2/α1 for carvedilol). This trial supports primary prophylaxis with beta-blockers before the first decompensating event (ascites, variceal bleed, hepatic encephalopathy).

**Q:** What are the major risk factors for cholangiocarcinoma?

**A:** Ages 50-70, gallstones, ulcerative colitis (UC), primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC), choledochal cyst, Clonorchis sinensis (liver fluke), hepatolithiasis, Lynch syndrome, CF, BAP1 tumour predisposition syndrome.

PSC is one of the most important risk factors for cholangiocarcinoma in Canada. The overall 5-year survival is poor: 30% localized, 24% regional, and only 2% for distant disease.

**Q:** What is the surgical treatment for lower third vs. upper third cholangiocarcinoma?

**A:** Upper third: duct resection + Roux-en-Y hepaticojejunostomy ± liver resection. Middle third (uncommon): duct resection + Roux-en-Y hepaticojejunostomy. Lower third: Whipple procedure (pancreaticoduodenectomy).

Resectability requires wide excision margins. Unresectable lesions are palliated with biliary stent or choledochojejunostomy (surgical bypass) plus chemotherapy and/or radiotherapy.

**Q:** A 55-year-old presents with painless progressive jaundice, pruritis, dark urine, and pale stools. CA 19-9 is elevated. What is the most likely diagnosis and what investigation best determines resectability?

**A:** Cholangiocarcinoma. ERCP or percutaneous transhepatic cholangiography (PTC) or endoscopic U/S (EUS) is used to determine resectability and obtain biopsies.

Cholangiocarcinoma classically presents with gradual biliary obstruction symptoms. LFTs show an obstructive pattern. CT and U/S show dilated ducts. ERCP/PTC allows tissue sampling and assessment of extent of disease.

## GERD & Peptic Ulcer Disease

**Q:** A 45-year-old has heartburn and regurgitation for 6 months, worse after meals and when lying down. No alarm symptoms. Diagnosis and first-line treatment?

**A:** GERD; first-line: lifestyle modifications + PPI (omeprazole, pantoprazole) for 4-8 weeks — endoscopy only if alarm symptoms or PPI failure

CGA guidelines recommend empiric PPI trial as first-line management for typical GERD without alarm symptoms. Lifestyle changes: elevate head of bed, avoid triggers (caffeine, alcohol, fatty foods), lose weight, avoid late meals.

**Q:** What alarm symptoms in GERD warrant immediate upper endoscopy?

**A:** Dysphagia, odynophagia, unexplained weight loss, GI bleeding, iron deficiency anemia, vomiting, age >60 with new symptoms, failure to respond to PPI

Alarm symptoms indicate possible malignancy or complicated GERD (stricture, Barrett esophagus). CGA guidelines recommend endoscopy for alarm features to exclude esophageal adenocarcinoma or other serious pathology.

**Q:** What is Barrett esophagus and how is it managed?

**A:** Intestinal metaplasia of esophageal squamous epithelium from chronic acid exposure; surveillance endoscopy: no dysplasia q3-5 years, low-grade dysplasia q6-12 months, high-grade dysplasia → endoscopic ablation

Barrett esophagus increases esophageal adenocarcinoma risk 30-40x. Endoscopic eradication therapy (radiofrequency ablation) for dysplastic Barrett. Indefinite PPI therapy to suppress acid. CGA guidelines recommend H. pylori testing in all dyspepsia patients.

**Q:** A 50-year-old has epigastric pain, early satiety, and belching for 3 months. H. pylori test is positive. What is the treatment?

**A:** Test-and-treat strategy for H. pylori: triple therapy (PPI + amoxicillin + clarithromycin x14 days) or bismuth quadruple therapy — confirm eradication 4 weeks after treatment

CGA guidelines recommend H. pylori eradication for all infected patients with peptic ulcer disease and functional dyspepsia. Bismuth quadruple therapy preferred in regions with high clarithromycin resistance or prior macrolide use.

**Q:** What is the difference between GERD and functional dyspepsia?

**A:** GERD: acid reflux causing heartburn and regurgitation; Functional dyspepsia: epigastric pain/discomfort, early satiety, fullness without organic cause on endoscopy — two Rome IV subtypes: EPS and PDS

Rome IV criteria define functional dyspepsia. Functional dyspepsia has no reflux predominance. Trial of PPI for 4 weeks; if no response, consider H. pylori testing, prokinetics (domperidone), or low-dose TCA.

**Q:** What are the complications of long-term PPI use?

**A:** C. difficile infection, hypomagnesemia, vitamin B12 deficiency, calcium and iron malabsorption, osteoporosis/fractures, community-acquired pneumonia, fundic gland polyps

PPIs alter gastric acid pH, affecting mineral absorption and microbiome. CGA recommends using PPIs at lowest effective dose for the shortest duration. H2 blockers are alternatives for mild GERD. De-prescribe PPIs when indication is unclear.

**Q:** How does peptic ulcer disease differ in NSAID-induced vs. H. pylori-associated ulcers?

**A:** NSAID ulcers: often asymptomatic until perforation/bleeding, more gastric ulcers, anterior surface; H. pylori: duodenal ulcers more common, epigastric pain relieved by eating

NSAIDs inhibit COX-1 reducing prostaglandin synthesis and mucosal protection. Prophylaxis: PPI with chronic NSAID use. H. pylori eradication heals 95% of duodenal ulcers. Test all peptic ulcer patients for H. pylori.

**Q:** What is the management of an actively bleeding peptic ulcer?

**A:** IV PPI (pantoprazole bolus 80mg then 8mg/h infusion), IV fluids, transfuse to Hgb >70-80 g/L, urgent endoscopy within 24h — high-risk stigmata: active bleeding or visible vessel require endoscopic hemostasis

Forrest classification guides endoscopic management: Ia (spurting) and Ib (oozing) = active bleeding requiring treatment; IIa (visible vessel) and IIb (adherent clot) = high risk for rebleeding. Rebleeding or failed endoscopy → surgery or interventional radiology.

**Q:** When is H. pylori eradication confirmed and how?

**A:** Confirm eradication ≥4 weeks after completing antibiotics and ≥2 weeks after stopping PPI; use urea breath test (UBT) or stool antigen test — serology is NOT useful for confirming eradication

False-negative UBT and stool antigen tests occur if PPI not stopped ≥2 weeks before testing (PPI suppresses H. pylori activity without eradicating). Serology remains positive for years after eradication.

**Q:** What is Zollinger-Ellison syndrome (ZES) and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** ZES: gastrin-secreting tumor (gastrinoma) causing multiple recurrent peptic ulcers and diarrhea; diagnose with fasting serum gastrin >1000 pg/mL (or gastrin >10x ULN with gastric pH <2)

Gastrinomas are in the pancreas or duodenum (gastrinoma triangle). 25-30% are part of MEN1 syndrome. Secretin stimulation test: paradoxical gastrin rise. CT/MRI/octreotide scan to localize. Treatment: high-dose PPI, surgical resection when possible.

**Q:** What are GERD alarm features requiring endoscopy?

**A:** Dysphagia, odynophagia, weight loss, GI bleeding, persistent vomiting, age >50 new onset

Rule out Barrett's, stricture, malignancy. Barrett surveillance q3-5y if no dysplasia.

**Q:** What are the alarm features in GERD that warrant urgent endoscopy?

**A:** Dysphagia, odynophagia, weight loss, anemia/GI bleeding, recurrent vomiting, age >50 with new symptoms, family history of upper GI cancer

Alarm features suggest possible complications (stricture, Barrett, adenocarcinoma) or alternative diagnoses (peptic ulcer, malignancy). CAG recommends EGD for alarm features rather than empiric PPI trial. Most GERD is diagnosed clinically without endoscopy.

**Q:** What is Barrett esophagus and its surveillance protocol?

**A:** Intestinal metaplasia (goblet cells) replacing normal squamous epithelium in distal esophagus. Surveillance: no dysplasia → EGD every 3-5 years; low-grade dysplasia → every 6-12 months or endoscopic ablation; high-grade dysplasia → endoscopic eradication therapy (RFA or EMR).

Barrett carries 0.5% annual risk of progression to esophageal adenocarcinoma. Risk factors: chronic GERD, male, Caucasian, age >50, obesity, smoking. High-dose PPI indefinitely. Endoscopic eradication with RFA has replaced esophagectomy for HGD and intramucosal carcinoma.

**Q:** What is the stepwise approach to GERD management?

**A:** Step 1: lifestyle modifications (elevate HOB, weight loss, avoid triggers, avoid eating 3h before bed). Step 2: PPI once daily 30 min before breakfast x 8 weeks. Step 3: double-dose PPI. Step 4: pH testing/impedance if refractory. Step 5: anti-reflux surgery (Nissen fundoplication).

PPIs are superior to H2RAs for erosive esophagitis healing. After 8 weeks, step down to lowest effective PPI dose or H2RA. Refractory GERD: reassess diagnosis (functional heartburn, eosinophilic esophagitis, achalasia). Long-term PPI risks: C. difficile, osteoporosis, hypomagnesemia, B12 deficiency.

**Q:** What is the H. pylori test-and-treat strategy?

**A:** Test patients with: dyspepsia <60 without alarm features, PUD history, gastric MALT lymphoma, before long-term NSAID/ASA, ITP, unexplained iron deficiency anemia. Test with urea breath test (UBT) or stool antigen. Treat with 14-day quadruple therapy: PPI BID + bismuth + metronidazole + tetracycline.

CAG recommends bismuth quadruple therapy as first-line in Canada due to high clarithromycin resistance (~15-20%). Confirm eradication 4 weeks after treatment with UBT or stool antigen (stop PPI 2 weeks prior, antibiotics 4 weeks prior). Concomitant therapy (PPI + amoxicillin + clarithromycin + metronidazole) is alternative.

**Q:** What are the complications of long-term PPI use?

**A:** C. difficile infection (1.5-2x risk), community-acquired pneumonia, hypomagnesemia, B12 deficiency, calcium malabsorption/osteoporotic fractures (hip), fundic gland polyps (benign), acute/chronic interstitial nephritis

Despite these associations, PPIs remain safe when indicated. CAG recommends periodic reassessment of need, using lowest effective dose, and not stopping PPIs based solely on long-term use concerns when clearly indicated (Barrett, severe erosive esophagitis, high-risk NSAID use).

**Q:** What diagnostic tests are used for refractory GERD?

**A:** 24h ambulatory pH monitoring (gold standard for acid exposure), pH-impedance testing (detects non-acid reflux), high-resolution esophageal manometry (rule out motility disorders like achalasia), EGD with biopsy (rule out eosinophilic esophagitis).

Refractory GERD: first ensure PPI adherence and proper timing (30 min before meals). pH-impedance testing done ON PPI differentiates true refractory GERD from functional heartburn. If DeMeester score >14.7 or acid exposure >4.2%: pathologic reflux confirmed.

**Q:** What are the indications for anti-reflux surgery (Nissen fundoplication)?

**A:** Confirmed GERD (objective pH testing) with: intolerance to PPIs, preference against long-term medications, large hiatal hernia with mechanical symptoms, refractory symptoms with proven acid reflux, volume regurgitation not controlled by PPI

Nissen (360° wrap) is the most common procedure; Toupet (270° posterior) for patients with impaired motility. Laparoscopic approach is standard. Pre-op manometry mandatory to exclude achalasia. Complications: dysphagia (10-15%), gas-bloat, inability to belch/vomit.

**Q:** How is eosinophilic esophagitis (EoE) differentiated from GERD?

**A:** EoE: dysphagia to solids (especially), food impaction, atopic history, male predominance. EGD shows rings (trachealization), linear furrows, white plaques. Biopsy: ≥15 eos/hpf in esophageal mucosa despite 8-week PPI trial. GERD: heartburn predominant, responds to PPI.

EoE is increasingly recognized as a cause of refractory dysphagia. Treatment: PPI (40% respond), swallowed topical corticosteroid (fluticasone or budesonide slurry), elimination diet (6-food or 2-food), dupilumab (anti-IL-4Rα, approved for EoE). Esophageal dilation for strictures.

**Q:** What are the differences between erosive and non-erosive reflux disease?

**A:** Erosive (ERD): mucosal breaks on EGD, graded by LA classification (A-D). Non-erosive (NERD): typical GERD symptoms but normal EGD (~60% of GERD patients). NERD responds less well to PPI and has higher functional heartburn overlap.

LA grade A: mucosal breaks <5mm. B: >5mm, not bridging folds. C: bridging folds, <75% circumference. D: ≥75% circumference. LA C/D require long-term PPI (high relapse). NERD patients should be evaluated with pH-impedance if refractory to confirm true reflux.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of GERD?

**A:** Primary mechanism: transient lower esophageal sphincter relaxations (TLESRs) — non-swallow-related LES relaxation allowing reflux. Contributing factors: reduced LES tone, hiatal hernia (disrupts crural diaphragm barrier), impaired esophageal clearance, delayed gastric emptying, obesity.

TLESRs account for ~70% of reflux events. Hiatal hernia creates an acid pocket above the diaphragm. Esophageal mucosal damage depends on: duration of acid contact, pepsin activity, and mucosal resistance. Baclofen (GABA-B agonist) reduces TLESRs but limited by CNS side effects.

**Q:** What are the red flag symptoms (VBAD) in dyspepsia that warrant urgent investigation?

**A:** VBAD: Vomiting, Bleeding/anemia, Abdominal mass, Dysphagia. Also: weight loss, persistent vomiting, GI bleeding (hematemesis, hematochezia, melena).

Red flag features in dyspepsia suggest organic pathology including malignancy, peptic ulcer disease with complications, or obstructing lesions. These warrant prompt upper endoscopy regardless of age.

**Q:** How should new-onset dyspepsia be investigated based on patient age?

**A:** <60 years without high-risk features: test for H. pylori using urea breath test, stool antigen test, or serology. ≥60 years: undergo upper endoscopy to rule out organic pathology.

The age cutoff of 60 reflects increased prevalence of gastric cancer in older patients. H. pylori testing is appropriate in younger patients as H. pylori is the most common cause of PUD-related dyspepsia.

**Q:** What are the two H. pylori eradication regimens used in Canada?

**A:** 1. Bismuth Quadruple Therapy (PBMT): PPI standard dose BID + Bismuth + Tetracycline 500 mg + Metronidazole 500 mg. 2. Concomitant Non-Bismuth Quadruple (PAMC): PPI standard dose BID + Amoxicillin 1000 mg BID + Clarithromycin 500 mg BID + Metronidazole 500 mg BID.

Bismuth quadruple therapy is preferred in areas with high clarithromycin resistance. Concomitant therapy is an alternative. Triple therapy (PPI + clarithromycin + amoxicillin) is no longer recommended due to high resistance rates.

**Q:** What is the Canadian Choosing Wisely recommendation regarding PPIs in dyspepsia?

**A:** Do not keep patients on PPI without at least 1 trial off the medication per year. (https://choosingwiselycanada.org/perspective/ppi-toolkit/)

Chronic PPI use is associated with risks including C. difficile infection, hypomagnesemia, B12 deficiency, and increased fracture risk. Periodic reassessment of PPI necessity is part of good stewardship.

**Q:** What is the definition of functional dyspepsia?

**A:** Functional dyspepsia is defined as dyspepsia (epigastric pain or discomfort) with a negative upper endoscopy.

Functional dyspepsia accounts for the majority of dyspepsia cases. Treatment options include gastric acid suppression (H2 blockers, PPIs), TCAs, or prokinetics (e.g., metoclopramide). Lifestyle modifications are first-line.

**Q:** What are the 5 anatomical parts of the stomach?

**A:** Cardia, Fundus, Body, Antrum, Pylorus.

The stomach receives food from the esophagus via the cardia, stores it in the fundus and body, and processes it in the antrum before passage through the pylorus to the duodenum. Parietal cells in the body/fundus secrete HCl and intrinsic factor; G cells in the antrum secrete gastrin.

**Q:** What are the key anatomical differences between the jejunum and ileum?

**A:** Jejunum: deep red, 2–4 cm thick, heavy walls, large/tall closely packed plicae circulares, long vasa recta, scant mesenteric fat, few Peyer's patches. Ileum: paler pink, 2–3 cm thick, thin/light walls, small/sparse plicae circulares, more mesenteric fat, many Peyer's patches.

These anatomical features reflect functional differences: the jejunum is the primary site of nutrient absorption, while the ileum absorbs bile salts and vitamin B12 (the only site for B12 absorption). Peyer's patches in the ileum are important for immune surveillance.

**Q:** What is the only site of vitamin B12 absorption in the GI tract?

**A:** The terminal ileum is the only site of vitamin B12 absorption (requires intrinsic factor secreted by gastric parietal cells).

Intrinsic factor (IF) binds to B12 in the stomach/proximal small bowel. The IF-B12 complex is absorbed at specific receptors in the terminal ileum. Conditions affecting the terminal ileum (Crohn's disease, ileal resection) or gastric parietal cells (pernicious anemia, gastrectomy) cause B12 deficiency.

**Q:** What hormone does the duodenum release in response to low duodenal pH, and what is its effect?

**A:** Secretin is released in response to low duodenal pH (acidic chyme). It reduces gastric acid secretion and increases bicarbonate secretion from the pancreas to neutralize duodenal pH.

Secretin is a key negative feedback hormone for gastric acid. Cholecystokinin (CCK) is also released from the duodenum to stimulate gallbladder contraction and pancreatic enzyme secretion. Both are released in response to duodenal luminal contents.

**Q:** What is the preferred initial imaging modality for dysphagia before endoscopy, and why?

**A:** Barium swallow should be considered first if there is dysphagia, decreased level of consciousness (risk of aspiration), inability to cooperate (risk of pharyngeal trauma), or possible fistulae before performing EGD.

EGD provides the best mucosal visualization and allows therapeutic intervention, but carries risk of aspiration and trauma in certain circumstances. Barium swallow is safer in these scenarios and can identify structural causes prior to endoscopy.

**Q:** What are absolute indications for gastroscopy (EGD) in suspected GERD?

**A:** 1. Heartburn with red flags (bleeding, weight loss, dysphagia, persistent vomiting, family history of GI cancer). 2. Persistent reflux symptoms or prior severe erosive esophagitis after 4–8 weeks of PPI BID trial. 3. History suggesting esophageal stricture (especially dysphagia). 4. High-risk for Barrett's esophagus (male, age >50, obese, white, tobacco use, long symptom history).

EGD is essential to rule out complications of GERD such as Barrett's esophagus, esophageal adenocarcinoma, and severe esophagitis. Red-flag symptoms should prompt urgent endoscopy.

**Q:** What is the definition of GERD and its most common pathophysiologic cause?

**A:** GERD is a condition that develops when the reflux of gastric content causes troublesome symptoms or complications. The most common cause is increased number of transient lower esophageal sphincter (LES) relaxations.

Other contributing mechanisms include low basal LES tone (especially in scleroderma), delayed esophageal clearance, delayed gastric emptying, obesity, pregnancy, and acid hypersecretion (rare—Zollinger-Ellison syndrome). Hiatal hernia worsens but does not cause GERD.

**Q:** What are the typical and atypical esophageal symptoms of GERD?

**A:** Typical: heartburn (pyrosis) and acid regurgitation (together 80% sensitive and specific). Atypical esophageal: chest pain, dysphagia (late), odynophagia (rare). Non-esophageal: chronic cough, wheezing, aspiration pneumonia (respiratory); sore throat, hoarseness, dental erosions (non-respiratory).

Heartburn and regurgitation are the cardinal symptoms. Non-esophageal manifestations (ENT, respiratory) can make diagnosis challenging. These extraesophageal symptoms are poor predictors of underlying reflux and should not be the sole diagnostic criteria.

**Q:** What are the two management goals based on GERD classification at gastroscopy?

**A:** Non-erosive reflux disease (NERD): aim for symptom relief only (PPI PRN or surgical fundoplication). Esophagitis: aim to heal inflammation (PPI indefinitely).

Gastroscopy classifies GERD into NERD and erosive esophagitis. Treatment intensity differs: NERD requires only symptom control, while esophagitis requires sustained PPI therapy to prevent complications (stricture, Barrett's esophagus).

**Q:** What are the established side effects of long-term PPI use?

**A:** Established associations: C. difficile diarrhea, hypomagnesemia, vitamin B12 deficiency, small bowel bacterial overgrowth. Uncertain/no clear pathophysiology: pneumonia, fractures, chronic kidney disease, dementia. Stopping PPIs can cause rebound acid hypersecretion. May increase IBD risk with prolonged use.

Long-term PPI safety is an important clinical consideration. While some side effects (C. diff, hypomagnesemia, B12 deficiency) have clear mechanisms related to acid suppression, other associations (CKD, dementia) lack established pathophysiology. Risk-benefit assessment is essential for each patient.

**Q:** What are the mechanical and motility causes of dysphagia, and how do they differ clinically?

**A:** Mechanical (solids only): peptic stricture/cancer, eosinophilic esophagitis (with stricture), extrinsic compression, Schatzki ring/web, Zenker's diverticulum. Motility (solids AND liquids): achalasia, distal esophageal spasm (DES), scleroderma, eosinophilic esophagitis (without stricture).

The distinction between solids-only vs. solids-and-liquids dysphagia is clinically important. Mechanical obstruction affects solids first (progressively worsening suggests malignancy). Motility disorders affect both liquids and solids from the outset. Achalasia is the classic motility disorder (failure of LES relaxation).

**Q:** What is the definition of dyspepsia and how does it differ from GERD?

**A:** Dyspepsia is defined as postprandial fullness, early satiety, or epigastric pain or burning. It may overlap with GERD but is a broader symptom complex that includes functional dyspepsia, PUD, gastric cancer, and GERD.

Dyspepsia is distinct from GERD (which emphasizes heartburn and regurgitation due to reflux). Functional dyspepsia has no identifiable cause after investigation. Alarm features (weight loss, bleeding, dysphagia) warrant urgent investigation for malignancy.

**Q:** What foods/substances may aggravate GERD symptoms?

**A:** Alcohol (EtOH), caffeine, tobacco, fatty/fried foods, peppermint, spicy foods, citrus fruit juices. Note: These aggravate symptoms but do NOT change the underlying disease process.

Dietary modification provides symptomatic benefit in GERD but does not alter disease course. The LES is transiently relaxed by many of these substances. Lifestyle modifications (dietary changes, weight loss, avoiding recumbency after meals, elevating head of bed) are first-line adjuncts to pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What are the four parts of the duodenum and what makes them anatomically distinct?

**A:** Superior (5 cm), Descending (7–10 cm), Horizontal (6–8 cm), Ascending (5 cm). The first (superior) part is intraperitoneal; the rest are retroperitoneal.

The duodenum is the first and shortest part of the small intestine. The major duodenal papilla (ampulla of Vater) opens into the descending (second) part, where bile and pancreatic juice enter. The retroperitoneal location of D2-D4 is relevant to trauma classification.

## Pancreatitis

**Q:** A 45-year-old presents with severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, and vomiting after a heavy meal. Lipase is 5x upper limit of normal. Diagnosis and initial management?

**A:** Acute pancreatitis; aggressive IV fluid resuscitation (lactated Ringer preferred), NPO initially then early enteral nutrition, analgesia, monitor for complications

Ranson criteria and BISAP score predict severity. CGA guidelines recommend LR over NS in acute pancreatitis (reduces SIRS). Early enteral nutrition (within 24-48h) preferred over TPN. Antibiotics not routinely given unless infected necrosis suspected.

**Q:** What are the two most common causes of acute pancreatitis in Canada?

**A:** Gallstones (40-50%) and alcohol (25-35%) — mnemonic GET SMASHED for all causes: Gallstones, Ethanol, Trauma, Steroids, Mumps, Autoimmune, Scorpion, Hypercalcemia/Hypertriglyceridemia, ERCP/Drugs

Gallstone pancreatitis: early cholecystectomy (same admission) prevents recurrence. Alcoholic pancreatitis: abstinence education. Hypertriglyceridemia pancreatitis: >11 mmol/L is risk; treat with insulin infusion + fibrates long-term.

**Q:** What criteria define severe acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Atlanta revised criteria: persistent organ failure >48h (respiratory, cardiovascular, or renal); BISAP score ≥3 or Ranson ≥3 predicts severity; CT severity index (CTSI) for pancreatic necrosis extent

Severe acute pancreatitis has 20-30% mortality. Organ failure definition: Pa02/FiO2 <300, creatinine >170 μmol/L, systolic BP <90 after resuscitation. CT chest/abdomen 48-72h after admission for patients not improving.

**Q:** What is infected pancreatic necrosis and how is it managed?

**A:** Infected necrosis: secondary infection of pancreatic/peripancreatic necrosis (usually >2 weeks after onset); diagnose with CT (gas in necrosis) or FNA; treat with antibiotics (imipenem or meropenem) and drainage

Sterile necrosis: manage conservatively. Infected necrosis: step-up approach — antibiotics, then EUS-guided or CT-guided drainage, then minimally invasive necrosectomy (VARD or percutaneous). Open necrosectomy has been largely replaced.

**Q:** What is chronic pancreatitis and what are its main complications?

**A:** Chronic irreversible pancreatic inflammation causing exocrine insufficiency (malabsorption, steatorrhea), endocrine insufficiency (diabetes), chronic pain, and pancreatic ductal stones/pseudocysts

Most common cause: alcohol (70-80%). Treat: pain management (analgesia, nerve block, endoscopic/surgical decompression), pancreatic enzyme replacement (PERT), diabetes management. Increased risk of pancreatic adenocarcinoma.

**Q:** What is the role of ERCP in acute pancreatitis?

**A:** ERCP within 24h for cholangitis complicating gallstone pancreatitis; ERCP within 72h for ongoing biliary obstruction (CBD stone, dilated CBD) without cholangitis — NOT recommended routinely

CGA guidelines limit ERCP to confirmed or strongly suspected bile duct stones with obstruction or cholangitis. Routine ERCP without biliary obstruction does not improve outcomes. MRCP or EUS less invasive for diagnosis.

**Q:** A 60-year-old with history of alcohol use has a pancreatic pseudocyst found on CT. When should it be drained?

**A:** Drain if: symptomatic (pain, vomiting), infected, growing, compressing adjacent structures — asymptomatic pseudocysts can be observed regardless of size

Pancreatic pseudocysts develop 4-6 weeks after acute pancreatitis. Most resolve spontaneously. EUS-guided drainage is preferred over surgical or percutaneous drainage. Walled-off necrosis requires step-up approach.

**Q:** What features on imaging suggest pancreatic adenocarcinoma vs. chronic pancreatitis?

**A:** Pancreatic cancer: hypoechoic/hypodense mass, upstream ductal dilation (double duct sign: dilated CBD + PD), vascular invasion; chronic pancreatitis: ductal stones, parenchymal calcifications, irregular ductal dilation

Double duct sign (dilated CBD and pancreatic duct) is classic for pancreatic head adenocarcinoma. CA 19-9 elevated but not specific. EUS + biopsy for tissue diagnosis. Most cases are unresectable at diagnosis.

**Q:** What is autoimmune pancreatitis (AIP) and how does it differ from pancreatic cancer?

**A:** AIP Type 1 (IgG4-related): diffuse sausage-shaped pancreas, elevated IgG4, responds dramatically to steroids; Type 2: UC-associated, IgG4 normal

AIP mimics pancreatic cancer on imaging. Key distinguishing features: diffuse pancreatic enlargement, halo/capsule-like rim, elevated serum IgG4 (>2x ULN in Type 1). Steroid trial appropriate when AIP suspected (avoid unnecessary Whipple).

**Q:** What is the management of pancreatic exocrine insufficiency?

**A:** Pancreatic enzyme replacement therapy (PERT) with meals: lipase ≥40,000 units with main meals, 20,000 units with snacks; monitor fat-soluble vitamin levels

PERT taken with first bite of meal and immediately before eating fat-containing foods. Dose titrated to reduce steatorrhea. Fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) supplementation required. Proton pump inhibitors may improve PERT efficacy.

**Q:** What are Ranson criteria at admission for pancreatitis?

**A:** Age>55, WBC>16k, glucose>11, LDH>350, AST>250. >=3 criteria = severe (15-40% mortality)

BISAP simpler alternative. CT severity index assesses necrosis. Atlanta: mild/moderate/severe.

**Q:** What is initial management of acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Aggressive IV fluids (LR), analgesics, early oral feeding when tolerated, ERCP if cholangitis

Early enteral preferred. No prophylactic antibiotics for sterile necrosis. Cholecystectomy before discharge for gallstone pancreatitis.

**Q:** What are the Ranson criteria for acute pancreatitis severity (on admission and at 48h)?

**A:** Admission: Age >55, WBC >16, Glucose >11 mmol/L, LDH >350, AST >250. At 48h: Hct drop >10%, BUN rise >1.8 mmol/L, Ca <2.0, PaO2 <60, base deficit >4, fluid sequestration >6L. ≥3 criteria = severe.

Ranson criteria are historical but still tested. APACHE II ≥8 at 48h or BISAP score are alternative severity scores. Revised Atlanta classification defines severity: mild (no organ failure/complications), moderate (transient organ failure <48h), severe (persistent organ failure >48h).

**Q:** What is the initial management of acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation (lactated Ringer's preferred, goal-directed 1.5 mL/kg/h initially), pain control (IV hydromorphone or fentanyl, avoid morphine historically though evidence weak), NPO initially then early oral feeding as tolerated (within 24h if mild), no prophylactic antibiotics.

Early aggressive fluid resuscitation reduces organ failure and necrosis. LR preferred over NS (less SIRS, lower CRP). Early enteral nutrition (within 24-48h) reduces infections and mortality compared to prolonged NPO. ERCP only indicated for cholangitis or persistent biliary obstruction.

**Q:** When are antibiotics indicated in acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Only for: infected pancreatic necrosis (confirmed by CT-guided FNA or clinical deterioration with gas in necrosis on CT), concurrent cholangitis, or other documented infections. NOT for sterile necrosis prophylaxis.

Prophylactic antibiotics do NOT reduce mortality or infected necrosis in RCTs and increase fungal infections. If infected necrosis suspected: carbapenems (meropenem) have best pancreatic tissue penetration. Step-up approach: percutaneous drainage → endoscopic necrosectomy → surgical necrosectomy.

**Q:** What is the role and timing of ERCP in gallstone pancreatitis?

**A:** Urgent ERCP (<24h) ONLY if concurrent cholangitis or persistent biliary obstruction (rising bilirubin, dilated CBD). NOT indicated for predicted mild gallstone pancreatitis without obstruction. Cholecystectomy during same admission (within 48h of resolution) for mild disease.

The AGA/CAG recommend against routine early ERCP for gallstone pancreatitis without cholangitis. MRCP or EUS can assess for retained CBD stones. Same-admission cholecystectomy for mild pancreatitis reduces recurrence (interval cholecystectomy if severe, after 6 weeks).

**Q:** What are the local complications of acute pancreatitis and when do they occur?

**A:** Early (<4 weeks): acute peripancreatic fluid collection (APFC), acute necrotic collection (ANC). Late (>4 weeks): pseudocyst (walled-off fluid without solid debris), walled-off necrosis (WON, encapsulated necrosis). Intervene only if symptomatic or infected.

Revised Atlanta classification defines these by timing and content. Pseudocysts are rare and usually resolve spontaneously. WON may require drainage if infected or causing symptoms — endoscopic (transmural) drainage preferred over surgical. CT with IV contrast best performed after 72-96h to assess necrosis extent.

**Q:** What are the common etiologies of acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Gallstones (~40%) and alcohol (~30%) account for ~70%. Others: hypertriglyceridemia (>11.3 mmol/L), post-ERCP, drugs (azathioprine, valproic acid, didanosine, GLP-1 agonists), autoimmune, pancreas divisum, malignancy, hypercalcemia, trauma, scorpion sting.

If no clear etiology after initial workup (RUQ US, lipids, alcohol history): consider EUS (microlithiasis, small tumors), MRCP (anatomic variants), IgG4 (autoimmune pancreatitis). Idiopathic pancreatitis requires thorough investigation as 10-20% have occult biliary cause. Genetic testing (PRSS1, SPINK1, CFTR) for recurrent pancreatitis.

**Q:** What are the features of autoimmune pancreatitis (AIP) Type 1 vs Type 2?

**A:** Type 1: older males, elevated IgG4, sausage-shaped pancreas on CT, associated with IgG4-related disease (sclerosing cholangitis, retroperitoneal fibrosis). Type 2: younger, no IgG4 elevation, associated with IBD (UC), granulocytic epithelial lesions (GELs). Both respond dramatically to steroids.

AIP Type 1 can mimic pancreatic cancer. Diagnosis: HISORt criteria (Histology, Imaging, Serology, Other organ involvement, Response to steroids). Elevated IgG4 >2x ULN is suggestive but not pathognomonic. Trial of steroids with response monitoring can help differentiate from malignancy.

**Q:** What are the key features and management of chronic pancreatitis?

**A:** Features: chronic abdominal pain, exocrine insufficiency (steatorrhea, weight loss), endocrine insufficiency (diabetes). Diagnosis: CT (calcifications, duct dilation), MRCP/EUS. Management: pain control, pancreatic enzyme replacement (PERT) with meals, fat-soluble vitamin supplementation (ADEK), diabetes management, alcohol/smoking cessation.

PERT dose: lipase 40,000-50,000 units with meals, 25,000 with snacks. Add PPI if poor response (acid degrades enzymes). Fecal elastase <200 μg/g confirms exocrine insufficiency. Endoscopic therapy or surgery (Frey/Puestow) for ductal obstruction. Increased pancreatic cancer risk (4-8%).

**Q:** What is the BISAP score for acute pancreatitis?

**A:** B=BUN >25 mg/dL, I=Impaired mental status, S=SIRS (≥2 criteria), A=Age >60, P=Pleural effusion. Score ≥3 predicts severe pancreatitis, organ failure, and increased mortality.

BISAP can be calculated within 24h of admission (advantage over Ranson which requires 48h). Simpler than APACHE II. SIRS criteria: HR >90, RR >20, Temp >38 or <36, WBC >12 or <4. Used alongside clinical assessment for disposition decisions (ICU vs ward).

**Q:** What are the systemic complications of severe acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Organ failure: respiratory (ARDS, most common), renal (AKI), cardiovascular (shock). Also: DIC, abdominal compartment syndrome, splanchnic vein thrombosis (splenic/portal/SMV), pseudoaneurysm with hemorrhage.

Persistent organ failure (>48h) defines severe pancreatitis per Revised Atlanta and carries 30-50% mortality. Marshall scoring system (Cr, PaO2/FiO2, SBP) used for organ failure assessment. Splanchnic vein thrombosis occurs in 10-15% — anticoagulate only if symptomatic or extensive. Pseudoaneurysm bleeding: angiographic embolization first-line.

**Q:** What are the endocrine and exocrine functions of the pancreas?

**A:** Endocrine (islets of Langerhans): alpha cells → glucagon; beta cells → insulin; delta cells → somatostatin. Exocrine: digestive enzymes including amylase, lipase, trypsin, chymotrypsin, and carboxypeptidase.

The pancreas has dual function. Endocrine products regulate blood glucose homeostasis. Exocrine products are secreted into the duodenum via the pancreatic duct to digest carbohydrates (amylase), fats (lipase), and proteins (trypsin, chymotrypsin, carboxypeptidase).

**Q:** What are the 4 parts of the pancreas?

**A:** Head (includes uncinate process), Neck, Body, and Tail. The major pancreatic duct connects to the common bile duct just before the ampulla of Vater.

The head of the pancreas lies within the C-loop of the duodenum. Pancreatic cancer in the head causes obstructive jaundice (Courvoisier's sign). The tail of the pancreas is near the hilum of the spleen, explaining why splenic injury can accompany pancreatitis.

**Q:** What is the role of ERCP versus MRCP in the evaluation of pancreatic/biliary duct disease?
