**A:** MRCP is nearly as sensitive as ERCP for determining bile duct obstruction but less accurate in determining the cause (tumour vs. stone vs. stricture). ERCP is indicated when therapeutic intervention is likely required (strong suspicion of stone, stenting, tissue sampling). EUS provides detailed anatomy with potential for sampling/intervention.

MRCP is the non-invasive choice for diagnostic imaging of the biliary tree. ERCP carries procedural risks (post-ERCP pancreatitis ~3–5%) but offers therapeutic capability. EUS is valuable for characterizing pancreatic masses and biliary anatomy without radiation.

**Q:** What are Ranson's criteria for acute pancreatitis, and how are they interpreted?

**A:** At admission: age >55, WBC >16×10⁹/L, glucose >11 mmol/L, LDH >350 U/L, AST >250 U/L. During initial 48h: Hct drop >10%, BUN rise >1.8 mmol/L, Ca <2 mmol/L, base deficit >4 mmol/L, fluid sequestration >6 L. Score <3 = severe pancreatitis unlikely (2% mortality); score ≥3 = severe pancreatitis likely.

Ranson's criteria help predict prognosis in acute pancreatitis. APACHE II score is used for severe acute pancreatitis. Gallstone pancreatitis accounts for ~45% of acute pancreatitis cases.

**Q:** What is the most sensitive and specific test for acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Serum lipase (more sensitive and specific than amylase). Elevated ALT >150 U/L or AST strongly suggests gallstone etiology.

Amylase is elevated in gallstone pancreatitis but to a lesser degree than in alcoholic pancreatitis. Lipase remains elevated longer. Imaging with CT is reserved for severe cases to evaluate for complications.

**Q:** When is cholecystectomy recommended after an episode of gallstone pancreatitis?

**A:** During the same admission. Recurrence is 25-60% if cholecystectomy is not performed.

Most stones pass spontaneously (~90%). Urgent ERCP + sphincterotomy is indicated for impacted CBD stones or coexistent cholangitis. Antibiotics are not indicated for uncomplicated acute pancreatitis.

**Q:** What are the local and systemic complications of acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Local: acute fluid collections, walled-off pancreatic pseudocyst (>4 wk), abscess/infection, necrosis. Systemic: splenic/mesenteric/portal vein thrombosis, pancreatic ascites/pleural effusion, DM, ARDS/sepsis/multiorgan failure, coagulopathy/DIC, severe hypocalcemia.

Necrotizing pancreatitis is managed with drain placement and debridement if refractory to medical management, septic, or without other sources of sepsis. Pancreatic pseudocysts develop a non-epithelialized fibrous wall and up to 40% resolve spontaneously.

**Q:** What are the indications for surgical management of chronic pancreatitis?

**A:** Failure of medical treatment (debilitating pain), pseudocyst complications (persistence, hemorrhage, infection, rupture), CBD/duodenal obstruction, pancreatic fistula, variceal hemorrhage from splenic vein obstruction, anatomical abnormality causing recurrent pancreatitis, rule out pancreatic cancer (present in 15%).

Preoperative CT and/or ERCP are mandatory to delineate anatomy. The Puestow procedure (lateral pancreaticojejunostomy) improves pain in 80% of patients when the ductal system is dilated.

**Q:** What is a pancreatic pseudocyst, and when is drainage contraindicated?

**A:** A localized fluid collection rich in pancreatic enzymes with a non-epithelialized fibrous/granulation wall, complicating acute or chronic pancreatitis. Pseudoaneurysm is an absolute contraindication to endoscopic drainage; must embolize first.

The cyst wall must be mature (4-6 weeks) before drainage. Asymptomatic pseudocysts: expectant management with CT/MRI in 3-6 months. Symptomatic options: endoscopic drainage (transmural or transpapillary), surgical drainage (cystogastrostomy, cystoduodenostomy, cystojejunostomy), or percutaneous drainage.

**Q:** What is the most clearly established risk factor for pancreatic cancer?

**A:** Smoking (2-5x increased risk).

Pancreatic cancer is the 4th most common cause of cancer-related mortality in both men and women in Canada. Other risk factors include DM, chronic pancreatitis, obesity, heavy alcohol use, high fat/low fibre diet, partial gastrectomy, cholecystectomy, and chemical exposure (p-naphthylamine, benzidine).

**Q:** Which location of pancreatic cancer tends to present later and is more often unresectable at diagnosis?

**A:** Body or tail of the pancreas (30% of cases); ~80% are unresectable at diagnosis.

Pancreatic head tumours (70%) present earlier with jaundice, steatorrhea, and weight loss. Body/tail tumours present with vague mid-epigastric pain and weight loss but without early jaundice, leading to later detection. Vague abdominal pain with weight loss in a patient >50 is pancreatic cancer until proven otherwise.

**Q:** What is the most useful serum tumour marker for pancreatic cancer?

**A:** CA 19-9.

CA 19-9 is used for monitoring response to treatment and detecting recurrence. It is not sensitive enough for screening. CEA may also be elevated. LFTs may show obstructive jaundice pattern (elevated ALP and bilirubin) in head-of-pancreas tumours.

**Q:** What are the criteria for resectable pancreatic cancer and what surgical procedure is performed?

**A:** Resectable: no involvement of liver, peritoneum, or major vasculature (hepatic artery, SMA, SMV, portal vein, IVC, aorta), no distant metastasis. Surgery: Whipple procedure (pancreaticoduodenectomy) for head tumours; distal pancreatectomy + splenectomy for body/tail tumours. Adjuvant chemotherapy recommended (gemcitabine + capecitabine or 5-FU/leucovorin).

Only 10-20% of pancreatic cancers are resectable. The Whipple procedure carries <5% mortality. Five-year survival after surgical resection is 20% vs. 1% overall. Palliative chemotherapy options include gemcitabine + nab-paclitaxel or FOLFIRINOX.

**Q:** What are the most important poor prognostic indicators for pancreatic cancer?

**A:** Lymph node status, margin status (R1), tumour size >3 cm, and perineural invasion.

Median survival for unresectable disease is 3-6 months if metastatic and 8-12 months if locally advanced. Overall 5-year survival for all patients is only 1%. Neoadjuvant chemoradiation improves survival in borderline resectable pancreatic cancer.

**Q:** A 60-year-old man presents with weight loss, vague mid-epigastric pain, and new onset diabetes. CT shows a 3 cm mass in the body of the pancreas. What percentage is likely unresectable and what is the expected 5-year survival?

**A:** ~80% of body/tail tumours are unresectable at diagnosis. Overall 5-year survival for all pancreatic cancer patients is 1%; following surgical resection it rises to 20%.

Body/tail pancreatic tumours present insidiously without early jaundice, leading to late detection. New onset diabetes in a patient >50 with weight loss and abdominal pain should raise suspicion for pancreatic cancer until proven otherwise.

## Colorectal Cancer & Screening

**Q:** What is the recommended colorectal cancer screening for average-risk individuals in Canada?

**A:** Start at age 50 (45 per some provincial guidelines): FIT (fecal immunochemical test) every 1-2 years OR flexible sigmoidoscopy every 10 years OR colonoscopy every 10 years

Canadian Cancer Society and CTFPHC recommend CRC screening starting at age 50 for average-risk individuals. FIT is the preferred non-invasive screening modality. Abnormal FIT requires colonoscopy. High-risk groups (IBD, FAP, Lynch, family history) require earlier and more frequent surveillance.

**Q:** What genetic syndromes are associated with hereditary colorectal cancer?

**A:** Lynch syndrome (HNPCC): MSH2, MLH1, MSH6, PMS2 mutations — 80% lifetime CRC risk; FAP: APC mutation — thousands of polyps, near 100% CRC risk

Lynch syndrome is the most common hereditary CRC syndrome. Colonoscopy every 1-2 years starting at age 20-25. FAP: prophylactic colectomy in teens. MUTYH-associated polyposis (MAP) is autosomal recessive. All CRC patients <70 should have MMR testing.

**Q:** What is the clinical presentation of colorectal cancer and how does it differ by location?

**A:** Right colon: iron deficiency anemia, weight loss, occult bleeding (large tumor, delayed obstruction); Left colon: change in bowel habits, hematochezia, obstruction (narrower lumen)

CRC is the second most common cancer in Canada. Left-sided CRC typically presents with altered bowel habits and rectal bleeding. Right-sided CRC often presents with anemia and systemic symptoms. All patients >50 with rectal bleeding require colonoscopy.

**Q:** What is the TNM staging of colorectal cancer and its associated 5-year survival?

**A:** T1-2 N0 M0 (Stage I): 90%; T3-4 N0 M0 (Stage II): 70-85%; any T N1-2 M0 (Stage III): 40-70%; any T any N M1 (Stage IV): 5-15%

Stage determines treatment. Stage I: resection alone. Stage III: resection + adjuvant FOLFOX chemotherapy (oxaliplatin + 5-FU + leucovorin). Stage IV: palliative chemotherapy (FOLFOX, FOLFIRI) ± biologic agents (bevacizumab, cetuximab for KRAS wild-type).

**Q:** What are adenomatous polyps and which features increase colorectal cancer risk?

**A:** High-risk adenomas: size ≥10mm, 3 or more adenomas, villous histology, or high-grade dysplasia — require 3-year surveillance colonoscopy

Low-risk adenomas (1-2 tubular adenomas <10mm, no HGD): 5-7 year colonoscopy surveillance. Serrated polyps: sessile serrated lesions ≥10mm or with dysplasia also require 3-year surveillance. CCA/CAG guidelines updated 2020.

**Q:** A 65-year-old has a 2cm adenomatous polyp removed at colonoscopy. First surveillance colonoscopy is when?

**A:** 3 years — high-risk adenoma (≥10mm) requires 3-year follow-up colonoscopy

CAG surveillance guidelines: 1-2 small tubular adenomas → 5-7 years; 3-4 small adenomas or 1-2 adenomas with any HGD or villous histology → 3 years; ≥5 small adenomas or ≥3 with at least one large → 1-3 years. Return to average risk after normal surveillance.

**Q:** What is the role of carcinoembryonic antigen (CEA) in colorectal cancer management?

**A:** CEA is not used for diagnosis or screening; used post-resection for surveillance — elevated preoperative CEA has prognostic value; rising post-op CEA suggests recurrence

CEA should be measured preoperatively (baseline) and every 3 months for 2 years post-resection, then every 6 months for 3 years. Rising CEA mandates imaging to look for recurrence. CEA has poor sensitivity for early CRC.

**Q:** What is microsatellite instability (MSI) and why is it tested in colorectal cancer?

**A:** MSI: deficient DNA mismatch repair (dMMR) leading to accumulation of errors in repetitive DNA sequences; MSI-H tumors respond to immune checkpoint inhibitors (pembrolizumab) and have better prognosis in Stage II

MSI/MMR testing is universal in CRC to screen for Lynch syndrome (germline mutation) and guide treatment. MSI-H Stage II CRC: do NOT benefit from 5-FU adjuvant chemotherapy. MSI-H metastatic CRC: first-line pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-177).

**Q:** When should colonoscopy be repeated after normal screening colonoscopy?

**A:** 10 years for average-risk individuals with completely normal colonoscopy (no polyps found)

CAG guidelines recommend 10-year intervals for normal colonoscopy. Quality indicators: adequate bowel prep, cecal intubation with photo documentation, adequate withdrawal time (≥6 minutes), adenoma detection rate. Poor prep requires repeat in 1 year.

**Q:** What is the hereditary cancer surveillance strategy for Lynch syndrome patients?

**A:** Annual colonoscopy starting at age 20-25 (or 2-5 years before youngest family member diagnosis); consider annual endometrial sampling; aspirin 600mg/day reduces CRC risk (CAPP2 trial)

Lynch syndrome also increases risk of endometrial, ovarian, urologic, gastric, and small bowel cancers. CCA/Canadian guidelines recommend comprehensive cancer surveillance. Prophylactic surgery (hysterectomy, BSO) discussed with patients who have completed childbearing.

**Q:** What is CRC screening in Canada per CTFPHC?

**A:** FIT every 2 years OR flex sig every 10 years, ages 50-74. Colonoscopy NOT primary screening

High-risk: colonoscopy q1-5y from age 40. Lynch: q1-2y. CTFPHC differs from US guidelines.

**Q:** What is the CRC screening protocol for average-risk Canadians per CTFPHC?

**A:** FIT (fecal immunochemical test) every 2 years for ages 50-74 (CTFPHC). Flexible sigmoidoscopy every 10 years is alternative. Colonoscopy is NOT recommended as primary screening for average risk by CTFPHC (though CCS and some provinces use it).

CTFPHC prioritizes FIT over colonoscopy for population screening due to non-invasive nature and similar mortality reduction. Positive FIT → diagnostic colonoscopy. Provincial programs vary: Ontario uses FIT, BC uses FIT, some allow screening colonoscopy. CTFPHC recommends against screening <50 and >74.

**Q:** What are the colonoscopy surveillance intervals after polypectomy?

**A:** Low-risk adenoma (1-2 tubular <10mm): repeat in 5-10 years. High-risk adenoma (≥10mm, villous, HGD, ≥3 adenomas, or serrated): repeat in 3 years. Piecemeal resection of large polyp: 6 months. Normal colonoscopy: 10 years.

CAG 2020 guidelines simplified surveillance. Sessile serrated lesions ≥10mm or with dysplasia are high-risk. Hyperplastic polyps <10mm in rectosigmoid are not adenomas — standard 10-year interval. Risk stratification at each colonoscopy determines next interval.

**Q:** What defines Lynch syndrome and what is the screening approach?

**A:** Autosomal dominant DNA mismatch repair (MMR) gene mutation (MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2). Lifetime CRC risk 40-80%. Screen with colonoscopy every 1-2 years starting age 20-25 (or 5 years before youngest family CRC diagnosis). Also screen for endometrial, ovarian, urinary tract cancers.

Suspect Lynch if: Amsterdam II criteria or revised Bethesda guidelines. Universal tumor testing (MMR IHC/MSI) on all new CRC. Germline genetic testing if tumor shows MMR deficiency. Prophylactic hysterectomy/BSO considered after childbearing. ASA may reduce CRC risk (CAPP2 trial).

**Q:** What is the TNM staging and 5-year survival for CRC?

**A:** Stage I: T1-2 N0 (>90% survival). Stage II: T3-4 N0 (70-85%). Stage III: any T, N1-2 (50-70%). Stage IV: distant metastases (5-15%). Treatment: Stage I-III curative resection ± adjuvant chemo. Stage IV: palliative chemo ± surgery if resectable metastases.

Adjuvant chemotherapy standard for Stage III (FOLFOX: 5-FU + leucovorin + oxaliplatin x 3-6 months). Stage II with high-risk features (T4, perforation, <12 LN sampled, LVI, poorly differentiated) may benefit from adjuvant chemo. Stage IV: test for RAS/BRAF/MSI for targeted therapy selection.

**Q:** What is the adenoma-carcinoma sequence in CRC development?

**A:** Normal mucosa → aberrant crypt foci → small adenoma (tubular) → large adenoma (villous features, high-grade dysplasia) → carcinoma. Key mutations: APC (initiation) → KRAS → DCC/SMAD4 → p53 (progression). Takes 10-15 years.

This Vogelstein model explains ~80% of sporadic CRC. An alternative pathway (serrated/BRAF/CpG island methylation) accounts for 15-20% of CRC, especially right-sided. Understanding this sequence justifies colonoscopic polypectomy (interrupting the sequence) and 10-year screening intervals.

**Q:** What are the presenting features of left-sided vs right-sided CRC?

**A:** Left-sided (descending/sigmoid/rectum): change in bowel habits, narrow stools, obstruction, hematochezia, tenesmus. Right-sided (cecum/ascending): insidious iron deficiency anemia, weight loss, vague abdominal pain, palpable mass. Obstruction is rare on right side due to wider lumen.

Right-sided CRC presents later due to liquid stool and distensible colon. Right-sided tumors are more often MSI-high, BRAF-mutated, mucinous, and respond to immunotherapy. Left-sided tumors respond better to anti-EGFR therapy (cetuximab) if RAS wild-type. Sidedness is a prognostic and predictive factor.

**Q:** What is the management of acute large bowel obstruction from CRC?

**A:** Resuscitation, NG decompression, CT abdomen/pelvis. Left-sided obstruction: emergent surgery (Hartmann procedure — resection with end-colostomy) or colonic stenting as bridge to elective surgery. Right-sided: right hemicolectomy with primary anastomosis.

Self-expanding metal stents (SEMS) as bridge to surgery in left-sided obstruction allow bowel decompression, optimization, and single-stage surgery with primary anastomosis. Concerns about stenting: perforation risk, potential tumor seeding. Not recommended if perforation suspected. Hartmann reversal in 3-6 months.

**Q:** What is the role of CEA in CRC management?

**A:** NOT a screening test (low sensitivity/specificity). Use for: pre-op baseline (prognosis — elevated CEA = worse), post-op surveillance for recurrence (check q3-6 months x 3 years, then q6 months x 2 years). Rising CEA post-resection: workup for recurrence with CT chest/abdomen/pelvis.

CEA >5 μg/L preoperatively associated with worse prognosis. Post-op, CEA should normalize within 4-6 weeks. Persistently elevated suggests residual/metastatic disease. CEA can be elevated in smokers, liver disease, IBD, and other cancers — not specific for CRC.

**Q:** What biomarkers guide targeted therapy selection in metastatic CRC?

**A:** RAS (KRAS/NRAS) wild-type + left-sided: anti-EGFR (cetuximab, panitumumab) + chemo. RAS mutant: bevacizumab (anti-VEGF) + chemo. BRAF V600E mutant: encorafenib + cetuximab. MSI-high/dMMR: pembrolizumab (first-line immunotherapy per KEYNOTE-177).

All metastatic CRC should be tested for RAS, BRAF, MSI/MMR, and HER2. MSI-H tumors (~15% of CRC) have excellent response to immunotherapy, now first-line. BRAF V600E (5-10%) has poor prognosis with standard chemo. HER2 amplification (3%): trastuzumab + pertuzumab or tucatinib combinations.

**Q:** What is the post-CRC resection surveillance protocol?

**A:** CEA q3-6 months x 3 years, then q6 months x 2 years. CT chest/abdomen/pelvis annually x 3 years (Stage II-III). Colonoscopy at 1 year post-op, then at 3 years, then every 5 years. PET not routine.

Goal: detect resectable recurrence early (especially liver/lung metastases). 80% of recurrences occur within 3 years. Isolated liver metastases: potentially curable with hepatic resection (5-year survival 40-50%). CCS/ASCO guidelines recommend structured follow-up for at least 5 years.

**Q:** A 70-year-old male presents with 10 kg unintentional weight loss over 6 months, fatigue, and early satiety. What is the most important initial workup?

**A:** Malignancy screen is the priority. Initial workup: CBC (cytopenias → hematologic malignancy), liver enzymes/LDH (metastatic disease), CXR (lung cancer), CT chest/abdomen/pelvis (solid organ malignancy), fecal occult blood test (GI malignancy), age-appropriate cancer screening (colonoscopy, PSA). Also check TSH (hyperthyroidism), HbA1c (diabetes), and HIV.

Unintentional weight loss >5% of body weight over 6-12 months is clinically significant. Malignancy is the leading cause in the elderly (up to 36%). The combination of early satiety, weight loss, and fatigue in an older male should prompt investigation for gastric, pancreatic, and colon cancer as top differentials.

**Q:** What are the main diagnostic categories for unintentional weight loss, and what key screening tests differentiate them?

**A:** 1) Malignancy (36%): CBC, LDH, CT, cancer screening. 2) Non-malignant GI (17%): celiac screen (tTG-IgA), stool studies, endoscopy. 3) Psychiatric (10%): depression screen (PHQ-9), eating disorders. 4) Endocrine: TSH (hyperthyroidism), HbA1c (uncontrolled diabetes), cortisol (adrenal insufficiency). 5) Chronic infection: HIV, TB. 6) Inflammatory: ESR/CRP (vasculitis, polymyalgia rheumatica).

A thorough history is essential — increased appetite with weight loss suggests hyperthyroidism or diabetes, while decreased appetite narrows to malignancy, depression, or medication side effects. In up to 25% of cases, no cause is found despite workup, and these patients generally have a good prognosis.

**Q:** A 35-year-old woman presents with unintentional weight loss, heat intolerance, tremor, palpitations, and exophthalmos. What is the diagnosis and confirmatory workup?

**A:** Graves' disease (autoimmune hyperthyroidism). Confirm with TSH (suppressed/low), free T4 and free T3 (elevated), and TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb, positive in Graves'). Radioactive iodine uptake scan shows diffuse homogeneous uptake. Management options: antithyroid drugs (methimazole), radioactive iodine ablation, or thyroidectomy.

Graves' disease is the most common cause of hyperthyroidism and the only cause with ophthalmopathy (exophthalmos). Weight loss occurs despite increased appetite due to elevated metabolic rate. Methimazole is first-line medical therapy (propylthiouracil preferred only in first trimester pregnancy and thyroid storm).

**Q:** What is the key finding from the DINAMO trial regarding acute diverticulitis?

**A:** Uncomplicated mild acute diverticulitis can be effectively treated as an outpatient WITHOUT antibiotics. In an RCT of 480 patients, hospital admission rates were similar: 14/238 with antibiotics vs. 8/242 without antibiotics.

This landmark trial challenges the traditional management of uncomplicated diverticulitis with routine antibiotics. Most uncomplicated diverticulitis is self-limiting and resolves with bowel rest and analgesia. Antibiotics are still indicated for complicated diverticulitis (abscess, perforation, fistula, obstruction).

**Q:** What are the common causes of constipation, including organic and functional categories?

**A:** Organic: colorectal cancer, stricture, extrinsic compression, anal disease, rectocele. Medications: narcotics, antidepressants, calcium channel blockers. Metabolic: diabetes mellitus, hypothyroidism, hypercalcemia. Neurologic: Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis, stroke. Collagen vascular disease: scleroderma, dermatomyositis. Most often: no cause found → chronic idiopathic constipation, slow transit constipation, or defecatory disorder.

Constipation in the absence of alarm features (weight loss, rectal bleeding, family history of colon cancer) can be managed empirically. New onset constipation in adults >50 warrants colorectal cancer screening.

**Q:** What are the common causes of lower GI bleeding (LGIB)?

**A:** Common: diverticulosis, ischemia, angiodysplasia (elderly), infectious colitis, IBD, anorectal disease (hemorrhoids, fissure, ulcer). Uncommon: brisk UGIB, post-polypectomy, radiation colitis. Rare: intussusception, vasculitides, stercoral ulcer, coagulopathies.

Diverticular bleeding is the most common cause of LGIB in adults >40 years. Angiodysplasia is more common in elderly. Anorectal disease (hemorrhoids) is common but benign. Colonoscopy is the primary diagnostic and therapeutic tool for LGIB.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of ischemic colitis compared with other causes of lower GI bleeding?

**A:** Ischemic colitis: acute onset crampy left lower abdominal pain + rectal bleeding + possible abdominal tenderness. Affects watershed areas (splenic flexure and rectosigmoid junction). Risk factors: atherosclerotic risk factors, vasoconstricting medications (e.g., cocaine, vasopressors), history of low flow state (shock, cardiac surgery).

The splenic flexure and rectosigmoid are watershed areas with dual/marginal blood supply and are most susceptible to ischemia. The clinical picture of left-sided abdominal pain with rectal bleeding in an elderly patient with vascular risk factors should prompt consideration of ischemic colitis.

## General

**Q:** What medication is used for variceal bleeding?

**A:** Octreotide

Octreotide reduces splanchnic blood flow and portal pressure to control variceal hemorrhage

**Q:** What is the most common cause of GI bleed in chronic alcohol users?

**A:** Gastritis

Alcohol is a direct irritant to the gastric mucosa and impairs mucosal defense, making gastritis the most frequent cause of GI bleeding in chronic drinkers.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for variceal GI bleeding?

**A:** Octreotide

Octreotide reduces portal pressure by splanchnic vasoconstriction and is the first-line pharmacologic agent for variceal hemorrhage.

**Q:** What is first-line treatment for variceal GI bleed?

**A:** Octreotide

Octreotide is the first-line pharmacological agent for variceal GI bleeding as it reduces portal pressure by splanchnic vasoconstriction.

**Q:** What is the recommended colorectal cancer screening modality for adults ages 60-74 at average risk?

**A:** FOBT (gFOBT or FIT) every 2 years OR flexible sigmoidoscopy every 10 years

The 2016 Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care recommends these screening options with strong recommendation and moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** What is the recommended colorectal cancer screening modality for average-risk adults ages 60-74 according to 2016 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** FOBT (either gFOBT or FIT) every 2 years OR flexible sigmoidoscopy every 10 years (strong recommendation)

The 2016 Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care recommends screening adults ages 60-74 with FOBT q2yr or flexible sigmoidoscopy q10yr as a strong recommendation with moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** What are the GI complications of problem drinking?

**A:** Gastritis, dyspepsia, pancreatitis, liver disease, bleeds, diarrhea

Alcohol is directly irritating to the GI tract and causes pancreatic and hepatic injury.

**Q:** List gastrointestinal complications of problem drinking

**A:** Gastritis, dyspepsia, pancreatitis, liver disease, bleeds, diarrhea, oral esophageal issues

Alcohol directly irritates the GI tract and causes significant hepatic and pancreatic damage.

**Q:** What are the GI complications of alcohol use disorder?

**A:** Gastritis, dyspepsia, pancreatitis, liver disease, bleeds, diarrhea

Alcohol directly irritates the GI tract and pancreas, and chronic use leads to alcoholic hepatitis and cirrhosis.

**Q:** What are GI complications of problem drinking?

**A:** Gastritis, dyspepsia, pancreatitis, liver disease, bleeds, diarrhea

Alcohol irritates the GI mucosa and is a common cause of pancreatitis and alcoholic liver disease.

**Q:** What is the appropriate management for non-erosive esophagitis (NERD)?

**A:** On-demand therapy with antacids (Mg(OH)2, Al(OH)3), alginate, H2-blockers, or PPIs

NERD patients may not require continuous PPI therapy and can use on-demand treatment for symptom control, as disease progression is less likely.

**Q:** What is the most common bacterial cause of diarrhea in Canada?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter is the most common bacterial cause of diarrhea in Canada, often associated with consuming undercooked poultry.

**Q:** Which pathogen causes emetic syndrome with a short incubation of 1-6 hours?

**A:** Bacillus cereus (Type A)

B. cereus Type A causes emetic syndrome from preformed exotoxin in rice dishes with a very short 1-6 hour incubation.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC produces heat-labile and heat-stable toxins and accounts for the majority of traveler's diarrhea cases.

**Q:** Which pathogen is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni infection is a common trigger for Guillain-Barré syndrome due to molecular mimicry.

**Q:** Which pathogen causes massive watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day)?

**A:** Vibrio cholerae

Vibrio cholerae causes cholera, characterized by massive watery diarrhea; mortality is <1% with proper treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Vibrio cholerae?

**A:** Tetracycline or fluoroquinolones (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Antibiotics reduce duration and fluid requirements; tetracycline and ciprofloxacin are effective treatments.

**Q:** What pathogen has a very low infectious dose (very small inoculum needed)?

**A:** Shigella

Shigella requires a very small inoculum for infection; it causes bloody diarrhea and can lead to toxic megacolon.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Shigella?

**A:** Fluoroquinolones

Fluoroquinolones are the treatment of choice for Shigella dysentery; antidiarrheals may increase toxic megacolon risk.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Salmonella typhi (typhoid fever)?

**A:** Ceftriaxone, ciprofloxacin, or azithromycin

Empiric treatment for enteric fever includes ceftriaxone, ciprofloxacin, or azithromycin; fluoroquinolone resistance is increasing.

**Q:** Which protozoan causes 'beaver fever' and is associated with daycare and untreated water?

**A:** Giardia lamblia

Giardia is transmitted fecal-oral via contaminated food/water; treatment is metronidazole or nitazoxanide.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Giardia lamblia?

**A:** Metronidazole or nitazoxanide

Treatment of symptomatic Giardia infection; treatment of asymptomatic carriers is NOT recommended.

**Q:** Which parasite can cause liver abscess if untreated?

**A:** Entamoeba histolytica

Entamoeba histolytica can cause amoebic liver abscess if untreated; treated with metronidazole followed by iodoquinol or paromomycin.

**Q:** What is the most common viral cause of gastroenteritis in adults?

**A:** Norovirus

Norovirus (including Norwalk virus) is the most common viral cause of gastroenteritis, with 24-hour incubation and 24-hour duration.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Rehydration - sealed beverages or oral rehydration solutions

Rehydration is the mainstay of therapy; oral rehydration solutions (1 packet in 1L water) are used for severe fluid loss.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of Dukoral vaccine against cholera and ETEC?

**A:** ~80% against cholera, ~50-67% against ETEC

Dukoral requires 2 doses taken 2 weeks prior to travel; effect lasts up to 3 months.

**Q:** What is the definition of chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Passage of frequent unformed stool for >4 weeks

Chronic diarrhea is defined as >4 weeks duration, compared to persistent diarrhea lasting 14-30 days.

**Q:** What percentage of travelers to developing countries get traveler's diarrhea in the first 2 weeks?

**A:** Up to 50%

Traveler's diarrhea is the most common illness affecting travelers; 10-20% get it after returning home.

**Q:** What is the approximate proportion of bacterial vs. viral vs. protozoal causes of traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Bacterial 80-90%, viral 5-8%, protozoal ~10% in long-term travelers

E. coli (ETEC) is the most common bacterial cause; protozoal causes are rare except in long-term travelers.

**Q:** What is the recommended prevention strategy for traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Proper hygiene: avoid street vendor food, untreated water, raw fruits/vegetables without peel; bismuth subsalicylate 60% effective

Antibiotic prophylaxis is NOT recommended due to increased resistance risk; Dukoral offers partial protection.

**Q:** What investigations are needed for chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Guided by history; stool analysis for C. difficile toxin, C&S

Most chronic diarrhea cases are non-infectious; investigations should be guided by the clinical history.

**Q:** Which bacteria produces heat-stable preformed exotoxin causing rapid onset (2-4 hours)?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus

S. aureus produces heat-stable preformed exotoxin in unrefrigerated meat and dairy, causing symptoms within 2-4 hours.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Clostridium perfringens?

**A:** None (supportive care only)

C. perfringens causes self-limiting diarrhea <24 hours; spores are heat-resistant and secondary enterotoxin is produced.

**Q:** What is the most common bacterial cause of infectious diarrhea in Canada?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter is the most common bacterial cause of diarrhea in Canada, typically associated with consumption of uncooked poultry.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of B. cereus Type A (emetic) food poisoning?

**A:** Incubation 1-6 hours, duration <12 hours, associated with rice dishes, caused by preformed exotoxin

B. cereus emetic syndrome is caused by a heat-stable preformed exotoxin in rice dishes, with rapid onset and short duration.

**Q:** What pathogen causes C. perfringens food poisoning and what is its key feature?

**A:** Clostridium perfringens; spores are heat-resistant

C. perfringens food poisoning is caused by contaminated meat and poultry; the spores are heat-resistant and can survive cooking.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC produces heat-labile and heat-stable toxins and is the leading cause of traveller's diarrhea worldwide.

**Q:** What pathogen causes S. aureus food poisoning?

**A:** Heat-stable preformed exotoxin in unrefrigerated meat and dairy

S. aureus produces a heat-stable toxin in improperly refrigerated foods like meat, dairy, and salads.

**Q:** What is the mortality of cholera with proper treatment?

**A:** Less than 1%

With appropriate fluid replacement and antibiotics, cholera mortality is less than 1%.

**Q:** What is the treatment for symptomatic Entamoeba histolytica?

**A:** Metronidazole followed by iodoquinol or paromomycin

Metronidazole treats invasive tissue infection, then iodoquinol or paromomycin eliminates intestinal cysts.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of viral gastroenteritis in adults?

**A:** Norovirus

Norovirus is the most common cause of viral gastroenteritis in adults, causing brief but intense symptoms.

**Q:** What is the etiology breakdown of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Bacterial 80-90% (E. coli most common), viral 5-8%, protozoal ~10% in long-term travellers

Bacterial pathogens, especially ETEC, dominate traveller's diarrhea; protozoal causes are more common in long-term travelers.

**Q:** What is the mainstay of traveller's diarrhea treatment?

**A:** Rehydration

Fluid replacement is the cornerstone of traveller's diarrhea treatment, using sealed beverages or oral rehydration solutions.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of Dukoral vaccine for cholera and ETEC?

**A:** ~80% efficacy for cholera, 50-67% for ETEC

Dukoral provides moderate protection against cholera and ETEC, lasting up to 3 months with two doses.

**Q:** What pathogen causes 'beaver fever'?

**A:** Giardia lamblia

Giardia lamblia is also known as 'beaver fever' and is transmitted via contaminated food/water, commonly affecting daycare children and those who drink untreated water.

**Q:** What is the recommended treatment for Giardia lamblia?

**A:** Metronidazole or nitazoxanide

Giardia lamblia is treated with metronidazole or nitazoxanide; treatment of asymptomatic carriers is NOT recommended.

**Q:** At what age is the rotavirus oral vaccine given?

**A:** 2 and 4 months of age

The rotavirus oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months of age. Virtually all children are infected by age 3, and rotavirus can cause severe dehydration.

**Q:** What is the mainstay of treatment for traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Rehydration

Rehydration is the mainstay of therapy for traveller's diarrhea. Use sealed beverages for mild cases and oral rehydration solutions (1 package in 1L water) for severe fluid loss.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of Dukoral vaccine against cholera?

**A:** ~80%

Dukoral vaccine offers approximately 80% protection against V. cholerae and 50-67% against ETEC. Two doses should be taken 2 weeks prior to traveling.

**Q:** What pathogen is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni infection is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome, an autoimmune demyelinating condition.

**Q:** What is the characteristic stool volume in Vibrio cholerae?

**A:** Massive watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day)

Vibrio cholerae causes massive watery diarrhea of 1-3 liters per day, which can be fatal without treatment. Mortality is <1% with proper therapy.

**Q:** What type of toxin does B. cereus Type A (emetic) produce?

**A:** Preformed exotoxin

B. cereus Type A (emetic) produces a preformed exotoxin found in rice dishes, with rapid onset of 1-6 hours and duration <12 hours.

**Q:** What dietary intervention is recommended for IBS with pain, bloating, and diarrhea?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols)

Low FODMAP diet reduces fermentable carbohydrates that cause gas, bloating, and diarrhea in IBS patients.

**Q:** What probiotics have evidence for IBS symptom improvement?

**A:** Lactobacillus or Bifidobacterium species

Probiotics can improve abdominal pain, bloating, and flatulence in IBS patients, particularly certain bacterial strains.

**Q:** What is recommended for IBS-D regarding fiber?

**A:** Increase soluble fiber (bran or psyllium) to improve stool consistency; avoid insoluble fiber which may worsen gas/bloating

Soluble fiber helps form stool while insoluble fiber may worsen abdominal discomfort in IBS-D.

**Q:** What medications are used for IBS-C?

**A:** Soluble fiber, PEG 3350, osmotic laxatives, prokinetics (prucalopride), secretagogues (linaclotide, plecanatide), tenapanor

IBS-C treatment progresses from fiber to osmotic agents to prokinetics and secretagogues. PEG improves stool form but not abdominal pain.

**Q:** How is constipation defined?

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Normal stool frequency is 3/day to 3/week. Constipation involves difficulty with passage or infrequency.

**Q:** What are the three types of constipation?

**A:** 1) Normal transit constipation, 2) Delayed transit constipation, 3) Outlet dysfunction/dyssynergic defecation

These types can be found in combination. Dyssynergic defecation involves inability to coordinate anorectum during defecation.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of constipation?

**A:** Functional, idiopathic constipation

Most constipation is functional with no identifiable organic cause. Organic causes should be considered only if other symptoms exist.

**Q:** What are stimulant cathartics for constipation?

**A:** Senna and bisacodyl - effective within 24 hours

Stimulants are more potent laxatives that work by stimulating intestinal motility. Used when osmotic agents insufficient.

**Q:** What are linaclotide and plecanatide?

**A:** Secretagogues that increase water secretion into intestinal lumen

These medications mimic guanylate cyclase-C agonists to increase intestinal fluid secretion and accelerate transit.

**Q:** What is tenapanor?

**A:** An NHE3 (sodium-proton) inhibitor used for IBS-C and constipation

Tenapanor reduces phosphate absorption and increases water secretion in the gut by inhibiting sodium-hydrogen exchange.

**Q:** What is eluxadoline used for?

**A:** IBS with diarrhea (IBS-D)

Eluxadoline is a mixed mu-opioid receptor agonist and delta-opioid receptor antagonist that reduces GI motility and secretion.

**Q:** What neurologic conditions can cause constipation?

**A:** Intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis

Neurologic diseases can affect autonomic innervation of the gut, leading to impaired motility and constipation.

**Q:** What is the definition of constipation?

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Constipation is defined by stool frequency (<3/week), hard stool consistency, and difficult passage.

**Q:** What defines upper gastrointestinal bleeding?

**A:** Bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz (suspensory ligament where the fourth portion of duodenum transitions to jejunum).

The ligament of Treitz marks the boundary between upper and lower GI bleeding, with 75% of all GI bleeds being upper GI in origin.

**Q:** What is the clinical presentation of outlet dysfunction (dyssynergic defecation)?

**A:** Inability to coordinate anorectal muscles during defecation - patients strain excessively but have difficulty expelling stool, often requiring digital assistance.

This is a functional disorder where the puborectalis fails to relax or actually contracts during defecation, creating a functional obstruction. Biofeedback therapy is often effective.

**Q:** What are stimulant cathartics used for constipation and examples?

**A:** Senna and bisacodyl - effective within 24 hours. Used for short-term relief of constipation.

Stimulants directly irritate the colonic mucosa and increase peristalsis. They are more effective than surface-acting agents but should not be used long-term.

**Q:** What dietary intervention is recommended for IBS symptoms?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols) for pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea

FODMAPs are poorly absorbed carbohydrates that ferment in the gut, causing IBS symptoms. This diet reduces osmotic and fermentative load.

**Q:** What is the treatment for IBS with constipation (IBS-C)?

**A:** Increase soluble fiber (bran or psyllium), PEG 3350, linaclotide, plecanatide, tenapanor, or osmotic laxatives

These treatments work by increasing water content in the intestine, softening stool, or promoting bowel movements. Soluble fiber increases stool consistency.

**Q:** What is the recommended dietary fiber intake for constipation?

**A:** Aim for 30 grams daily, increase dose slowly

Gradual increase helps minimize side effects like bloating and gas. Fiber adds bulk and promotes regular bowel movements.

**Q:** What is the definition of upper gastrointestinal bleeding?

**A:** Bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz (suspensory ligament where fourth portion of duodenum transitions to jejunum)

The ligament of Treitz marks the boundary between upper and lower GI bleeding. Upper GI bleeds account for 75% of all GI bleeds.

**Q:** Define constipation according to clinical criteria

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Normal stool frequency is considered 3/day to 3/week.

**Q:** List neurologic causes of constipation

**A:** Intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis

Neurologic conditions can impair the autonomic innervation of the bowel.

**Q:** What is the location of the ligament of Treitz?

**A:** The suspensory ligament where the fourth portion of the duodenum transitions to the jejunum

The ligament of Treitz marks the boundary between upper and lower GI bleeding.

**Q:** What is aortoenteric fistula and why is it concerning?

**A:** A rare and lethal cause of GI bleed, most common in patients with history of aortic graft surgery

This is a surgical emergency with a narrow window of opportunity for intervention.

**Q:** What pathogen is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome following diarrhea?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni infection is a common trigger for Guillain-Barré syndrome due to molecular mimicry.

**Q:** Which E. coli pathotype is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC produces heat-labile and heat-stable toxins and is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea.

**Q:** What toxin does Staphylococcus aureus produce causing food poisoning?

**A:** Heat-stable preformed exotoxin (emetic toxin)

S. aureus produces a heat-stable exotoxin in unrefrigerated food, causing rapid onset vomiting.

**Q:** What is the classic presentation of Vibrio cholerae diarrhea?

**A:** Massive watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day)

Vibrio cholerae causes voluminous rice-water diarrhea that can rapidly cause dehydration and death.

**Q:** What is the classic food source for B. cereus emetic syndrome?

**A:** Rice dishes (particularly leftover rice)

B. cereus type A (emetic) is associated with rice dishes where spores survive improper storage.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Entamoeba histolytica?

**A:** Metronidazole followed by iodoquinol or paromomycin

Metronidazole treats invasive disease, then iodoquinol/paromomycin eliminates cysts.

**Q:** When is the rotavirus vaccine given?

**A:** At 2 and 4 months of age

Oral rotavirus vaccine is administered at 2 and 4 months of age to prevent severe rotavirus gastroenteritis.

**Q:** What percentage of travellers to developing countries get traveller's diarrhea in the first 2 weeks?

**A:** Up to 50%

Traveller's diarrhea affects up to 50% of travellers to developing countries in the first 2 weeks.

**Q:** What is the most common bacterial cause of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC is the most common cause, accounting for 80-90% of bacterial traveller's diarrhea cases.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) for preventing traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** 60% effective

Bismuth subsalicylate is 60% effective for prevention when taken as 2 tablets QID.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of bacterial food poisoning with short incubation (1-6 hours)?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus (2-4 hours) or B. cereus type A (1-6 hours)

S. aureus has 2-4h incubation; B. cereus emetic type has 1-6h incubation from preformed toxin.

**Q:** What pathogen causes diarrhea with incubation of 8-12 hours and is associated with meat/poultry?

**A:** Clostridium perfringens

C. perfringens has 8-12h incubation, associated with contaminated meat and poultry, spores are heat-resistant.

**Q:** What is the treatment for severe Campylobacter jejuni diarrhea?

**A:** Macrolide (e.g., azithromycin) or fluoroquinolone if susceptible

Macrolides are first-line; fluoroquinolones are used if susceptibility confirmed, noting increasing resistance.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Shigella dysentery?

**A:** Fluoroquinolone (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Fluoroquinolones are first-line for Shigella; antidiarrheals may increase toxic megacolon risk.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Yersinia enterocolitica severe illness?

**A:** Fluoroquinolone (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Fluoroquinolones are first-line for severe Yersinia infection; most cases in children <4 years are self-limited.

**Q:** What is the pathogen distribution in traveller's diarrhea by type?

**A:** Bacterial 80-90%, viral 5-8%, protozoal ~10% (in long-term travellers)

Bacterial causes dominate (ETEC most common), with norovirus/rotavirus for viral and Giardia/Cryptosporidium for protozoal.

**Q:** What foods should be avoided to prevent traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Foods from unhygienic establishments, raw fruits/vegetables without peel, raw/undercooked meat/seafood, untreated water, beer on tap

Avoid high-risk foods and beverages to prevent traveller's diarrhea; proper hygiene practices are essential.

**Q:** What investigations are used for chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Guided by history; stool analysis for C. difficile toxin, C&S

Chronic diarrhea workup is guided by history; stool studies including C. difficile toxin and culture are initial tests.

**Q:** A 25-year-old returning traveler from Southeast Asia presents with 3 days of watery diarrhea. Which bacterium is the most common cause of traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC is the most common cause of traveler's diarrhea, producing heat-labile and heat-stable toxins that cause secretory diarrhea.

**Q:** A patient with raw poultry consumption develops diarrhea with Guillain-Barré syndrome. Which organism is responsible?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni is the most common bacterial cause of diarrhea in Canada and is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome due to molecular mimicry.

**Q:** A traveler develops profuse watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day). What organism causes this and what is the mortality with treatment?

**A:** Vibrio cholerae; mortality <1% with treatment

Vibrio cholerae causes cholera, characterized by massive rice-water diarrhea. Proper rehydration and antibiotics (tetracycline or fluoroquinolones) reduce mortality to less than 1%.

**Q:** A patient with AIDS develops watery diarrhea. Oocysts are seen on acid-fast stain. What is the treatment?

**A:** Paromomycin + nitazoxanide (with immune reconstitution)

Cryptosporidium causes severe diarrhea in immunocompromised patients. Treatment requires paromomycin plus nitazoxanide and immune reconstitution.

**Q:** A patient with dysentery develops toxic megacolon. Which organism is associated and what should NOT be given?

**A:** Shigella; antidiarrheals should be avoided

Shigella can cause toxic megacolon as a complication. Antidiarrheals increase the risk of toxic megacolon and should be avoided.

**Q:** A 35-year-old man has diarrhea 2 weeks after returning from India. He reports 'beaver fever' from camping. What parasite?

**A:** Giardia lamblia

Giardia lamblia is transmitted via contaminated water (often from beavers - 'beaver fever') and causes foul-smelling, fatty diarrhea.

**Q:** What is the defining feature of enteroinvasive E. coli (EIEC)?

**A:** Invades colonic mucosa causing dysentery-like illness

EIEC invades colonic epithelial cells, similar to Shigella, causing inflammatory dysentery with fever, abdominal pain, and bloody diarrhea.

**Q:** A patient with diarrhea has a history of recent clindamycin use. What is the most likely organism?

**A:** Clostridioides difficile

Clindamycin, along with fluoroquinolones, penicillins, and cephalosporins, is a major risk factor for C. difficile infection.

**Q:** What is the most common protozoal cause of traveler's diarrhea?

**A:** Giardia lamblia

While protozoa account for only ~10% of traveler's diarrhea in long-term travelers, Giardia is the most common protozoal cause.

**Q:** A patient with dysentery develops a liver abscess. What is the organism and complication?

**A:** Entamoeba histolytica; amebic liver abscess

Without treatment, E. histolytica can spread hematogenously to the liver, causing amebic liver abscess - the most common extraintestinal complication.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of bacterial diarrhea in Canada?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni is the most common bacterial cause of infectious diarrhea in Canada, typically from undercooked poultry.

**Q:** A patient with watery diarrhea has a history of recent camping and untreated water ingestion. What parasite is associated with this?

**A:** Giardia lamblia (also called 'beaver fever')

Giardia is commonly acquired from contaminated water sources during camping or drinking untreated water ('beaver fever').

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Clostridium perfringens food poisoning?

**A:** None - supportive care only

C. perfringens food poisoning is self-limited, lasting less than 24 hours. Treatment is supportive; antibiotics are not indicated.

**Q:** What is unique about the spores of Clostridium perfringens?

**A:** They are heat-resistant

C. perfringens spores are heat-resistant, allowing them to survive cooking and germinate in cooked meat held at improper temperatures.

**Q:** What type of fiber should be increased in IBS-D and what caution applies?

**A:** Soluble fiber (bran or psyllium) to increase stool consistency

Soluble fiber can improve stool consistency in IBS-D but may worsen abdominal gas and bloating in some patients - this is controversial.

**Q:** What treatments are recommended for IBS-C (IBS with constipation)?

**A:** Increase soluble fiber, PEG 3350, linaclotide, plecanatide, tenapanor, or osmotic/other laxatives

Soluble fiber may increase bloating. PEG 3350 improves stool form. Linaclotide and plecanatide are secretagogues; tenapanor is an NHE3 inhibitor.

**Q:** Name 3 neurologic causes of constipation

**A:** Intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis

Neurologic conditions can affect the autonomic innervation of the gut, leading to impaired motility.

**Q:** What is the only test indicated for constipation if it is the only presenting symptom (per 2013 AGA recommendation)?

**A:** Complete blood count (CBC)

The American Gastroenterology Association recommends only CBC as initial test when constipation is the sole presenting symptom.

**Q:** What is the recommended daily dietary fiber intake for constipation?

**A:** 30 grams daily, with dose increased slowly

Dietary fiber is useful for mild to moderate constipation but not severe cases. Gradual increase helps minimize bloating.

**Q:** Name 2 stimulant/cathartic laxatives used for constipation and when are they effective?

**A:** Senna and bisacodyl - effective in 24 hours

Stimulant laxatives promote intestinal motility and are used when osmotic agents are insufficient.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of linaclotide and plecanatide?

**A:** Secretagogues that increase water secretion into the intestinal lumen

These medications activate guanylate cyclase-C receptors to increase chloride and water secretion, softening stool.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of tenapanor?

**A:** NHE3 (sodium-proton) inhibitor - reduces sodium absorption in the intestine

Tenapanor inhibits NHE3 to increase water retention in the lumen, improving stool consistency.

**Q:** Define upper gastrointestinal bleeding

**A:** Bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz (accounts for 75% of GI bleeds)

The ligament of Treitz is the suspensory ligament where the fourth portion of duodenum transitions to jejunum.

**Q:** What is the ligament of Treitz?

**A:** Suspensory ligament where the fourth portion of the duodenum transitions to jejunum

This landmark marks the anatomical division between upper and lower GI bleeding.

**Q:** What dietary intervention has evidence for treating IBS symptoms?

**A:** Low FODMAP (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols) diet

The low FODMAP diet is effective for pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea in IBS. It should be symptom-guided and implemented with proper education.

**Q:** What medications are used specifically for IBS-C (IBS with constipation)?

**A:** Soluble fiber, PEG 3350, osmotic or other laxatives, linaclotide, plecanatide, and tenapanor

PEG 3350 can alter stool form but has no evidence for helping abdominal pain. Linaclotide and plecanatide are secretagogues that increase water secretion into the intestinal lumen.

**Q:** What is the definition of constipation according to Toronto Notes?

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Constipation is characterized by infrequent bowel movements (<3/week), hard stools, and difficult evacuation often requiring straining.

**Q:** What is the recommended dietary fiber intake for constipation treatment and how should it be implemented?

**A:** Aim for 30 grams daily, increase dose slowly

Dietary fiber is useful for mild to moderate constipation but not for severe cases. Gradual dose increases help minimize bloating and gas.

**Q:** What stimulant cathartics are used for constipation and how quickly do they work?

**A:** Senna and bisacodyl - effective within 24 hours

Stimulant cathartics directly stimulate intestinal motility and are used for more acute constipation relief.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for constipation in order of increasing potency?

**A:** Dietary fiber → surface-acting agents (softeners) → osmotic agents → stimulants → enemas/suppositories → prokinetics → secretagogues → NHE3 inhibitors

Treatment follows a stepwise approach starting with the least invasive interventions and escalating based on response.

**Q:** What is eluxadoline and what is its indication?

**A:** Eluxadoline is a medication used for IBS with diarrhea (IBS-D)

Eluxadoline is a mixed mu-opioid receptor agonist and delta-opioid receptor antagonist that reduces bowel motility and secretion in IBS-D.

**Q:** What is tenesmus in the context of constipation?

**A:** The sensation of incomplete evacuation after defecation, often with straining

Tenesmus is a common symptom of constipation and can be associated with outlet dysfunction or dyssynergic defecation.

**Q:** What are the organic causes of constipation that should be considered?

**A:** Intestinal obstruction, left-sided colon cancer (consider in older patients), fecal impaction, and painful anal conditions (e.g., fissures)

Organic causes should be suspected when constipation is accompanied by other symptoms beyond just infrequent bowel movements.

**Q:** Define constipation according to standard criteria.

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Constipation is defined by stool frequency (<3/week), consistency (hard/lumpy), and evacuation difficulty.

**Q:** Define upper gastrointestinal bleeding (UGIB).

**A:** Bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz (where fourth portion of duodenum transitions to jejunum)

The ligament of Treitz is the anatomic landmark distinguishing upper from lower GI bleeding.

**Q:** What is a characteristic feature of an enteric fistula as a cause of GI bleed?

**A:** Enteric fistula is a rare and lethal cause of GI bleed, most common in patients with a history of aortic graft surgery.

Aortoenteric fistulas present with GI bleeding and abdominal pain; they require urgent surgical evaluation.

**Q:** What pathogen is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea, producing heat-labile and heat-stable enterotoxins.

**Q:** What food is associated with Bacillus cereus emetic syndrome, and what is the toxin type?

**A:** Rice dishes; preformed heat-stable exotoxin

B. cereus emetic syndrome is associated with reheated rice dishes and is caused by a preformed heat-stable exotoxin (cereulide).

**Q:** What type of toxin does Clostridium perfringens produce, and what foods are associated?

**A:** Secondary enterotoxin; heat-resistant spores in meat and poultry

C. perfringens produces a secondary enterotoxin and is associated with contaminated meat and poultry; spores are heat-resistant.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of Staphylococcus aureus food poisoning?

**A:** Heat-stable preformed exotoxin in unrefrigerated meat and dairy

S. aureus produces a heat-stable preformed exotoxin in unrefrigerated meat and dairy products (custard, pudding, potato salad, mayonnaise).

**Q:** At what ages is the rotavirus oral vaccine typically given?

**A:** 2 and 4 months of age

The rotavirus oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months of age; virtually all children are infected by age 3 years.

**Q:** What percentage of traveller's diarrhea cases are caused by bacteria?

**A:** 80-90%

Bacterial pathogens cause 80-90% of traveller's diarrhea cases, with ETEC being the most common overall cause.

**Q:** In which region is fluoroquinolone resistance particularly high in traveller's diarrhea pathogens?

**A:** South and Southeast Asia

Fluoroquinolone resistance is particularly high in South and Southeast Asia, limiting the use of ciprofloxacin for empiric treatment in these regions.

**Q:** How effective is bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) for preventing traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** ~60% effective (2 tablets QID)

Bismuth subsalicylate is approximately 60% effective for preventing traveller's diarrhea when taken as 2 tablets four times daily.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of Dukoral vaccine against V. cholerae and ETEC?

**A:** ~80% against cholera, ~50-67% against ETEC

Dukoral provides ~80% protection against cholera and ~50-67% protection against ETEC, lasting up to 3 months.

**Q:** What is the mainstay of therapy for traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Rehydration

Rehydration is the mainstay of therapy for traveller's diarrhea; oral rehydration solutions (1 package in 1L water) are used for severe fluid loss.

**Q:** How is chronic diarrhea defined in terms of duration?

**A:** Passage of frequent unformed stool for >4 weeks

Chronic diarrhea is defined as passage of frequent unformed stool for more than 4 weeks, distinguishing it from persistent diarrhea (14-30 days).

**Q:** What is the majority etiology of chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Non-infectious causes

The majority of chronic diarrhea cases are non-infectious in etiology, requiring investigation for other causes like IBD, IBS, malabsorption, etc.

**Q:** What is the estimated effectiveness of Salmonella Typhi vaccines?

**A:** 50-70%

Two vaccines against Salmonella Typhi are available with an estimated effectiveness of 50-70%.

**Q:** Define constipation according to the text.

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage)

Constipation is defined by stool frequency (<3/week), consistency (hard/lumpy), and ease of passage.

**Q:** What neurological conditions can cause constipation?

**A:** Intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis

Neurological diseases affect the autonomic innervation of the GI tract, impairing motility.

**Q:** What is the recommended daily dietary fibre intake for constipation?

**A:** 30 grams daily, increase dose slowly

Dietary fibre is useful for mild-moderate constipation but not severe. Slow titration reduces gas/bloating side effects.

**Q:** What are stimulant/cathartic laxatives for constipation and examples?

**A:** Effective in 24 hours. Examples: senna, bisacodyl

Stimulant laxatives directly stimulate colonic motility and are used when faster relief is needed.

**Q:** What is eluxadoline and what is it used for?

**A:** Medication for IBS-D (irritable bowel syndrome with diarrhea)

Eluxadoline is a mixed opioid receptor agonist/antagonist that reduces GI motility and secretion.

**Q:** What are enema and suppository options for constipation?

**A:** Saline enema, phosphate enema, glycerin suppository, bisacodyl suppository

Rectal interventions provide rapid relief and are useful for fecal impaction or when oral laxatives are insufficient.

**Q:** What is the first-line dietary intervention for IBS?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols) - helps with pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea.

FODMAPs are poorly absorbed carbohydrates that ferment in the colon, causing IBS symptoms. This is a first-line dietary therapy with good evidence.

**Q:** Define constipation according to clinical criteria.

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage).

Normal stool frequency is 3-21 per week; constipation is typically <3 BMs/week with hard stools.

**Q:** What is the recommended dietary intervention for IBS symptoms?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols) for pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea

FODMAPs are poorly absorbed carbohydrates that ferment in the gut, worsening IBS symptoms. This diet is supported by evidence for symptom relief.

**Q:** What probiotic species have evidence for improving IBS symptoms?

**A:** Lactobacillus or Bifidobacterium species

Probiotics can improve abdominal pain, bloating, and flatulence in IBS, though evidence is variable across strains.

**Q:** What are the two types of Bacillus cereus food poisoning and their respective incubations?

**A:** Type A (emetic) has 1-6h incubation from rice dishes; Type B (diarrheal) has 8-16h incubation from meats and vegetables

B. cereus produces two distinct syndromes: emetic from preformed exotoxin in rice dishes (short incubation), and diarrheal from secondary endotoxin in meats and vegetables (longer incubation).

**Q:** What pathogen causes massive watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day)?

**A:** Vibrio cholerae

Vibrio cholerae causes cholera, characterized by massive watery diarrhea of 1-3 liters per day, with mortality less than 1% with proper treatment.

**Q:** What percentage of travellers to developing countries are affected by traveller's diarrhea in the first 2 weeks?

**A:** Up to 50%

Traveller's diarrhea is the most common illness affecting travellers, with up to 50% affected in the first 2 weeks and 10-20% after returning home.

**Q:** What is the efficacy of bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) in preventing traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** 60% effective (2 tablets QID)

Bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) is 60% effective for preventing traveller's diarrhea when taken as 2 tablets four times daily.

**Q:** What does Dukoral vaccine protect against and with what efficacy?

**A:** Vibrio cholerae (~80% efficacy) and ETEC (~50-67% efficacy)

Dukoral oral vaccine provides ~80% protection against cholera and 50-67% protection against ETEC, lasting up to 3 months with 2 doses taken 2 weeks prior to travel.

**Q:** What is the most common etiology of traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Bacterial (80-90%), with E. coli (ETEC) being the most common bacterial cause

Bacterial pathogens account for 80-90% of traveller's diarrhea, with ETEC being the most common; viral causes account for 5-8%, and protozoal causes (~10%) are more common in long-term travellers.

**Q:** What is unique about the pathophysiology of Staph aureus food poisoning?

**A:** Heat-stable preformed exotoxin

Staphylococcus aureus produces a heat-stable preformed exotoxin that causes vomiting within 2-4 hours after eating contaminated unrefrigerated meat and dairy products.

**Q:** What is the hallmark of Clostridium perfringens food poisoning?

**A:** Heat-resistant spores

Clostridium perfringens food poisoning results from heat-resistant spores in contaminated meat and poultry, producing a secondary enterotoxin causing diarrhea within 8-12 hours.

**Q:** What is the typical duration of norovirus gastroenteritis?

**A:** 24 hours

Norovirus (including Norwalk virus) causes a short-lived gastroenteritis lasting approximately 24 hours with nausea and vomiting predominating.

**Q:** What is Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC)?

**A:** Chronic inflammation and fibrous obliteration of intrahepatic bile ductules

PBC is an autoimmune disease affecting mainly middle-aged women (M:F=1:9), often associated with Sjogren's syndrome, scleroderma, CREST syndrome, RA, and thyroiditis.

**Q:** What are the extrahepatic manifestations of PBC?

**A:** Pruritus, fatigue, jaundice, melanosis (dark skin), xanthelasma, osteoporosis

End-stage disease presents with hepatocellular failure, portal hypertension, and ascites

**Q:** What is the prognosis of PBC with UDCA treatment?

**A:** Treatment improves prognosis; normalization or near-normalization of ALP with UDCA is associated with improved survival comparable to age- and sex-matched controls

Not all asymptomatic patients show progression, but disease can be fatal if untreated

**Q:** When is serum amylase >5x normal almost always due to?

**A:** Pancreatitis or renal insufficiency

This degree of elevation strongly suggests pancreatic origin rather than non-pancreatic causes

**Q:** What pathogen is most commonly associated with traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC)

ETEC produces heat-labile and heat-stable toxins and is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea, accounting for 80-90% of bacterial cases.

**Q:** What is the key feature distinguishing B. cereus emetic vs diarrheal syndrome?

**A:** Incubation period - emetic (1-6h) from preformed toxin, diarrheal (8-16h) from secondary endotoxin

B. cereus type A (emetic) has short incubation from preformed exotoxin in rice dishes, while type B (diarrheal) has longer incubation from secondary endotoxin.

**Q:** Which pathogen causes pseudomembranous colitis?

**A:** Clostridium difficile

C. difficile is normally present in small numbers but overgrowth occurs after antibiotic exposure, causing pseudomembranous colitis.

**Q:** What is the treatment for cholera?

**A:** Tetracycline or fluoroquinolones (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Cholera causes massive watery diarrhea (1-3 L/day) and mortality is less than 1% with proper treatment including antibiotics.

**Q:** Which parasite is associated with 'beaver fever'?

**A:** Giardia lamblia

Giardia is transmitted fecal-orally through contaminated water (historically from beaver ponds) and causes prolonged diarrhea with malodorous stools.

**Q:** Which virus is the most common cause of viral gastroenteritis in adults?

**A:** Norovirus

Norovirus (including Norwalk virus) is the most common cause of viral gastroenteritis in adults, with 24-hour incubation and self-limited 24-hour course.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of B. cereus emetic syndrome?

**A:** Preformed exotoxin in rice dishes

B. cereus type A (emetic) produces a preformed heat-stable exotoxin in reheated rice, causing symptoms within 1-6 hours.

**Q:** Which protozoal parasites account for ~10% of traveller's diarrhea in long-term travellers?

**A:** Giardia, Entamoeba histolytica, Cryptosporidium, and Cyclospora

Protozoal causes are rare (~10%) in short-term travellers but more significant in long-term travellers to endemic areas.

**Q:** What is the incubation period for C. perfringens?

**A:** 8-12 hours

C. perfringens has an 8-12 hour incubation period, causes diarrhea without fever, and is self-limited within 24 hours.

**Q:** What is the recommended prevention strategy for traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Proper hygiene: avoid street vendor food, raw fruits/vegetables without peel, undercooked meat/seafood, untreated water

Prevention includes avoiding risky foods/beverages. Bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) is 60% effective. Antibiotic prophylaxis is NOT recommended.

**Q:** What is the usual duration of non-typhoidal salmonellosis?

**A:** 3-7 days

Non-typhoidal salmonellosis typically lasts 3-7 days and usually does not require antibiotics except in severe illness, extremes of age, or specific comorbidities.

**Q:** What is the key investigation finding in Entamoeba histolytica sigmoidoscopy?

**A:** Flat ulcers with yellow exudates

Sigmoidoscopy in amebic dysentery may show flat ulcers with yellow exudates, distinguishing it from other causes of dysentery.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Giardia lamblia?

**A:** Metronidazole or nitazoxanide

Metronidazole is first-line for Giardia, with nitazoxanide as an alternative. Treatment of asymptomatic carriers is NOT recommended.

**Q:** What is the typical incubation period for Giardia?

**A:** 1-4 weeks

Giardia lamblia has an incubation period of 1-4 weeks, transmitted fecal-orally through contaminated food/water.

**Q:** What is the first-line dietary intervention for IBS symptoms?

**A:** Low FODMAP diet (Fermentable Oligo-, Di-, Monosaccharides And Polyols) for pain, bloating, gas, and diarrhea.

Low FODMAP diet reduces fermentable carbohydrate absorption, decreasing symptoms in many IBS patients.

**Q:** How is constipation defined according to Toronto Notes?

**A:** Passage of infrequent/hard stools and/or difficult stool evacuation (e.g., straining, sensation of anorectal blockage).

Constipation is defined by stool frequency (<3/week), form (hard/lumpy), and/or difficult passage.

## Gi Upper

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for classic GERD symptoms?

**A:** Empiric 8-week trial of PPIs

PPIs are the most effective therapy for GERD. After 8 weeks, discontinue if no Barrett's or erosive esophagitis, but symptoms may recur.

**Q:** What are the only beneficial lifestyle changes for GERD?

**A:** Weight loss (if obese/significant weight gain) and elevating head of bed (for nocturnal symptoms)

Diet helps symptoms but not the disease. Avoid EtOH, coffee, spices, tomatoes, and citrus juices - these reduce symptoms but don't alter disease progression.

**Q:** What are the complications of GERD?

**A:** Esophageal stricture disease (scarring causing dysphagia to solids), esophagitis (ulceration/bleeding), Barrett's esophagus, and esophageal adenocarcinoma

Complications range from benign strictures to premalignant Barrett's and最终的 adenocarcinoma. Chronic GERD is the key risk factor.

**Q:** What is Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Metaplasia of normal squamous esophageal epithelium to intestinal columnar epithelium

Acquired via long-standing GERD and consequent damage to squamous epithelium. Endoscopy shows salmon pink mucosa in distal esophagus; diagnosis requires biopsy showing specialized intestinal epithelium.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Male sex, age >50, White individuals, smokers, overweight, hiatus hernia, and long history of reflux symptoms

In North America and Western Europe, 0.5-20% of adults have BE. Up to 10% of GERD patients have developed BE by time of presentation.

**Q:** What is the malignant transformation rate of Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Approximately 0.12% per year for all BE patients prior to dysplasia; 32-59% over 5-8 years in high-grade dysplasia

Risk is very low in non-dysplastic BE but significantly higher once high-grade dysplasia develops, warranting intensive surveillance or intervention.

**Q:** How is Barrett's esophagus managed?

**A:** High-dose PPI indefinitely (or fundoplication) to reduce transformation; surveillance gastroscopy every 3-5 years if no dysplasia

High-grade dysplasia requires intensive biopsy surveillance, endoscopic ablation/resection, or esophagectomy. Evidence increasingly favors endoscopic ablation with mucosal resection or radiofrequency ablation.

**Q:** What is the role of esophageal manometry in GERD?

**A:** Indicated in patients with non-cardiac chest pain and/or dysphagia with normal gastroscopy; diagnoses peristalsis abnormalities and decreased LES tone

Cannot detect presence of reflux but is essential before surgical fundoplication to ensure intact esophageal function; excludes alternative diagnoses like scleroderma and achalasia.

**Q:** When is 24-hour pH monitoring indicated in GERD?

**A:** Most accurate test for reflux; most useful if PPIs do not improve symptoms

Not required or performed in most cases, but valuable for confirming reflux in PPI-refractory symptoms before considering surgical intervention.

**Q:** What key questions differentiate oropharyngeal from esophageal dysphagia?

**A:** Oropharyngeal: difficulty initiating swallowing, choking, coughing, nasal regurgitation. Esophageal: inability to move food down the esophagus

Also assess: associated symptoms (regurgitation, chest pain, voice change, weight loss), solid vs liquid vs both, intermittent vs progressive, and presence of GERD.

**Q:** What are the causes of oropharyngeal dysphagia?

**A:** Neurological: cortical, bulbar. Muscular: muscular dystrophy, polymyositis, myasthenia gravis. Structural: Zenker's diverticulum, cricopharyngeal, thyromegaly

Requires careful neurological exam. Key distinguishing features include difficulty initiating swallow, choking, coughing, and nasal regurgitation.

**Q:** What is the approach to a patient <50 with dysphagia and no alarm features but concomitant GERD?

**A:** Trial of PPIs first before proceeding to endoscopy

If no alarm features (dysphagia, odynophagia, weight loss, anemia, GI bleeding) and patient has GERD symptoms, empiric PPI trial is appropriate first step.

**Q:** What is the difference between dysphagia and odynophagia?

**A:** Dysphagia = difficulty in swallowing. Odynophagia = pain on swallowing

These are distinct symptoms with different etiologies. Dysphagia suggests mechanical or motility issue; odynophagia suggests inflammatory or ulcerative process.

**Q:** What is the recommended surveillance protocol for non-dysplastic Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Surveillance gastroscopy every 3-5 years

If low-grade dysplasia: every 6 months for 1 year then annually. High-grade dysplasia requires intensive surveillance with biopsy, endoscopic ablation/resection, or esophagectomy.

**Q:** What are alarm features in GERD that warrant endoscopy?

**A:** Anorexia, weight loss, bleeding, dysphagia (suggesting complicated disease)

Chronic GERD or symptoms suggestive of complicated disease warrant EGD. Also: chronic GERD patients should be evaluated for Barrett's risk factors.

**Q:** What is the most accurate test for diagnosing gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)?

**A:** 24-hour pH monitoring

24-hour pH monitoring is the most accurate test for reflux, though not required in most cases. It is most useful when PPIs do not improve symptoms.

**Q:** What are the only beneficial lifestyle changes for GERD symptoms?

**A:** Weight loss (if obese or significant weight gain) and elevating the head of bed (if nocturnal symptoms)

While diet may help symptoms, it does not change the underlying disease. Only weight loss and head-of-bed elevation have proven benefit.

**Q:** What is the gold standard treatment for GERD?

**A:** Proton pump inhibitors (PPIs)

PPIs are the most effective therapy for GERD. An empiric 8-week trial is recommended for patients with classic symptoms.

**Q:** What are complications of untreated/complicated GERD?

**A:** Esophageal stricture (scarring causing dysphagia to solids), esophagitis (ulceration, bleeding), and Barrett's esophagus leading to adenocarcinoma

Chronic GERD can lead to scarring causing strictures, mucosal damage causing esophagitis, and metaplasia (Barrett's) that can progress to cancer.

**Q:** What is Barrett's esophagus defined as histologically?

**A:** Replacement of normal squamous esophageal epithelium with intestinal columnar epithelium (specialized intestinal metaplasia)

Diagnosis requires biopsy demonstrating specialized intestinal epithelium of any length within the esophagus, seen endoscopically as salmon pink mucosa.

**Q:** What are risk factors for Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Male sex, age >50, White race, smokers, overweight/obesity, hiatus hernia, and long history of reflux symptoms

Barrett's is more common in males over 50 with chronic GERD, particularly those with obesity, hiatus hernia, and smoking history.

**Q:** What is the annual malignant transformation rate for Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia?

**A:** Approximately 0.12% per year

The rate of malignant transformation is very low (0.12%/year) for all BE patients prior to dysplasia, but significantly higher (32-59% over 5-8 years) in high-grade dysplasia.

**Q:** How is Barrett's esophagus with high-grade dysplasia managed?

**A:** Regular intensive biopsy surveillance, endoscopic ablation/mucosal resection, or esophagectomy

Evidence increasingly favours endoscopic ablation (RFA) with mucosal resection over esophagectomy for high-grade dysplasia in BE.

**Q:** How do you distinguish oropharyngeal from esophageal dysphagia on history?

**A:** Oropharyngeal: difficulty initiating swallowing, choking, coughing, nasal regurgitation. Esophageal: inability to move food down the esophagus

Oropharyngeal dysphagia presents with problems starting to swallow (transfer problem), while esophageal dysphagia is a passage problem.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of esophageal stricture?

**A:** Peptic stricture (from chronic GERD)

Peptic strictures from chronic GERD are the most common cause of esophageal structural narrowing, causing dysphagia to solids.

**Q:** What is the recommended surveillance interval for Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia?

**A:** Every 3-5 years

Patients with BE and no dysplasia should undergo surveillance gastroscopy every 3-5 years to monitor for dysplasia progression.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment approach for a patient <50 years with dysphagia and no alarm features who also has GERD symptoms?

**A:** Empiric 8-week PPI trial before proceeding to endoscopy

In younger patients with dysphagia and GERD symptoms without alarm features, an empiric PPI trial is recommended as first-line before endoscopy.

**Q:** What lifestyle modifications have been shown to benefit GERD symptoms?
