**A:** Weight loss (if obese or significant weight gain) and elevating the head of bed (for nocturnal symptoms)

While diet may help symptoms, it doesn't change the disease. Only weight loss and head-of-bed elevation are evidence-based lifestyle changes.

**Q:** What are the alarm features in GERD that warrant endoscopy?

**A:** Dysphagia, odynophagia, weight loss, anorexia, anemia, GI bleeding

EGD is recommended for patients with chronic GERD or symptoms suggestive of complicated disease including anorexia, weight loss, bleeding, or dysphagia.

**Q:** What is the annual malignant transformation rate for Barrett's esophagus patients without dysplasia?

**A:** Approximately 0.12% per year

The rate of malignant transformation is approximately 0.12% per year for all BE patients prior to dysplasia, but is significantly higher in high-grade dysplasia (32-59% over 5-8 years).

**Q:** How often should surveillance endoscopy be performed in Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia?

**A:** Every 3-5 years

For Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia, surveillance gastroscopy is recommended every 3-5 years, with more intensive surveillance for high-grade dysplasia.

**Q:** What is the preferred treatment for high-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Endoscopic ablation with mucosal resection or radiofrequency ablation (RFA)

Evidence increasingly favors endoscopic ablation with mucosal resection or radiofrequency ablation over esophagectomy for high-grade dysplasia.

**Q:** What type of dysphagia (solid foods only vs. solids and liquids) suggests a mechanical obstruction versus a neuromuscular disorder?

**A:** Solid food only suggests mechanical obstruction; solids, liquids, or both suggests neuromuscular disorder

In the approach to dysphagia, solid food alone points to mechanical obstruction, while involvement of both solids and liquids indicates a neuromuscular disorder.

**Q:** What is the key distinguishing feature of oropharyngeal dysphagia?

**A:** Difficulty initiating swallowing, choking, coughing, nasal regurgitation

Oropharyngeal dysphagia presents with difficulty initiating the act of swallowing, choking, coughing, and nasal regurgitation, as opposed to esophageal dysphagia.

**Q:** In a patient <50 years old with dysphagia and no alarm features but with GERD, what is the initial management before endoscopy?

**A:** Empiric 8-week PPI trial

If concomitant GERD symptoms in patients <50 with no alarm features, a trial of PPI first before proceeding to endoscopy is recommended.

**Q:** What are the complications of untreated/advanced GERD?

**A:** Esophageal stricture (leading to dysphagia), esophagitis (ulceration, bleeding), Barrett's esophagus, and esophageal adenocarcinoma

GERD can lead to scarring causing stricture with dysphagia to solids, esophagitis with ulceration and bleeding, Barrett's esophagus, and progression to adenocarcinoma.

**Q:** What finding on endoscopy suggests Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Salmon pink mucosa in the distal esophagus

Endoscopy shows salmon pink mucosa in the distal esophagus, and diagnosis is confirmed by biopsy showing specialized intestinal epithelium.

**Q:** For low-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus, what are the management options?

**A:** Surveillance every 6 months for 1 year then annually, OR endoscopic ablation/resection

Both surveillance and endoscopic ablation/resection are satisfactory options for low-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus.

**Q:** What foods/substances should GERD patients avoid that may worsen symptoms?

**A:** Alcohol, coffee, spices, tomatoes, and citrus juices

While diet helps symptoms but not the disease, patients are advised to avoid alcohol, coffee, spices, tomatoes, and citrus juices.

**Q:** What is the recommended management for PPI-refractory GERD with reflux-related symptoms?

**A:** Consider surgical treatment (laparoscopic Nissen fundoplication) - showed 67% success vs 28% in medical therapy in RCT

A 2019 NEJM randomized trial showed surgical treatment achieved significantly greater success (67%) than medical therapy (28%) for PPI-refractory reflux-related heartburn.

**A:** 24-hour pH monitoring

While esophageal manometry diagnoses motility abnormalities and endoscopy visualizes structural issues, 24h pH monitoring is most accurate for detecting reflux.

**Q:** Which lifestyle modifications have been shown to improve GERD symptoms?

While diet may help symptoms, it does not change the underlying disease. Only weight loss and head-of-bed elevation are truly beneficial.

Endoscopic appearance alone is insufficient - biopsy demonstrating intestinal metaplasia is required for definitive diagnosis.

The risk of progression to adenocarcinoma is low in non-dysplastic BE but significantly higher in high-grade dysplasia (32-59% over 5-8 years).

Non-dysplastic Barrett's requires periodic surveillance, though the exact interval depends on segment length and other risk factors.

**Q:** How is high-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus managed?

**A:** Intensive surveillance with frequent biopsies, endoscopic ablation (radiofrequency ablation), endoscopic mucosal resection, or esophagectomy

Evidence increasingly favors endoscopic ablation with mucosal resection or radiofrequency ablation over surgical esophagectomy.

**Q:** What is the treatment for low-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Surveillance every 6 months for 1 year then annually, OR endoscopic ablation/resection - both are acceptable options

Both surveillance and endoscopic intervention are satisfactory approaches for low-grade dysplasia.

Dysphagia refers to difficulty initiating swallowing (oropharyngeal) or moving food down the esophagus (esophageal).

**Q:** In a patient <50 years old with dysphagia and no alarm features, what is the initial management?

**A:** Empiric PPI trial before proceeding to endoscopy

**Q:** What are the clinical features of esophageal motor disorders?

**A:** Dysphagia with solids or liquids (or both) and chest pain

Motor disorders present with variable dysphagia and sometimes chest pain, depending on the specific disorder.

Maintenance PPI is indicated for complicated GERD, but can be discontinued in recovered patients without BE or erosive esophagitis.

**Q:** What is on-demand therapy appropriate for in GERD?

**A:** Non-erosive esophagitis (NERD)

Chronic GERD leads to repeated injury and healing, resulting in fibrosis and stricture formation that causes dysphagia to solids.

**A:** Inability to move food down the esophagus

**Q:** In a patient <50 with dysphagia and no alarm features but with GERD, what is the initial management?

**A:** Empiric 8-week PPI trial before proceeding to endoscopy

**Q:** What is the first-line surgical treatment for GERD?

**A:** Laparoscopic Nissen fundoplication (wrapping gastric fundus around the lower esophagus)

**Q:** Can diet cure GERD?

**A:** No, diet helps symptoms but does not cure the disease

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of Barrett's esophagus in Western populations?

**A:** 0.5-20% of adults; up to 10% of GERD patients have developed BE by time of presentation

**Q:** What is the relationship between GERD and Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Barrett's is thought to be acquired via long-standing GERD and consequent damage to squamous epithelium

**Q:** What are causes of oropharyngeal (transfer) dysphagia?

**A:** Neurological: cortical, bulbar; Muscular: dystrophy, polymyositis, myasthenia gravis; Structural: Zenker's diverticulum, thyromegaly, cervical spur

**Q:** What are causes of esophageal dysphagia?

**A:** Mechanical obstruction (strictures, rings, carcinoma), neuromuscular disorders (achalasia, scleroderma, DES), and motility disorders

**Q:** What is the characteristic endoscopy finding in Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Salmon pink mucosa in the distal esophagus

**Q:** What are the red flag features in chronic GERD that warrant EGD?

**A:** Anorexia, weight loss, bleeding, and dysphagia

**Q:** In which esophageal motor disorder do patients present with dysphagia to both solids and liquids?

**A:** Achalasia (due to failure of LES relaxation)

**Q:** What are the indications for esophageal manometry?

**A:** Patients with non-cardiac chest pain and/or dysphagia with normal gastroscopy; to diagnose peristalsis abnormalities and decreased LES tone; to exclude scleroderma and achalasia; before surgical fundoplication

Esophageal manometry studies motility but cannot detect presence of reflux. It is essential before fundoplication to ensure intact esophageal function.

**Q:** What lifestyle modifications have been shown to benefit GERD?

**A:** Weight loss (if obese or significant weight gain) and elevating the head of bed (if nocturnal symptoms)

Only these two lifestyle changes are beneficial. Diet helps symptoms but not the disease - avoid EtOH, coffee, spices, tomatoes, and citrus juices.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** 0.5-20% of adults in North America/Western Europe; up to 10% of GERD patients; more common in males, ages >50, White individuals, smokers, overweight, with hiatus hernia and long reflux history

Barrett's is a common condition strongly associated with chronic GERD. Key risk factors include male sex, age over 50, and white race.

**Q:** What is the annual malignant transformation rate of Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Approximately 0.12% per year for all BE patients prior to dysplasia

The risk is relatively low but increases significantly in high-grade dysplasia, with studies reporting 32-59% transformation rate over 5-8 years.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia?

**A:** Acid suppressive therapy with high-dose PPI indefinitely (or surgical fundoplication) to reduce transformation, plus surveillance gastroscopy every 3-5 years

Even without dysplasia, indefinite acid suppression is recommended to potentially reduce progression to dysplasia.

**Q:** What is the treatment for high-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Regular intensive biopsy surveillance, endoscopic ablation/mucosal resection, or esophagectomy - with increasing evidence favoring endoscopic ablation (RFA or EMR)

High-grade dysplasia has high transformation risk (32-59% over 5-8 years) and requires aggressive intervention.

**Q:** What are the key distinguishing features between oropharyngeal and esophageal dysphagia?

**A:** Oropharyngeal: difficulty initiating swallowing, choking, coughing, nasal regurgitation; Esophageal: inability to move food down esophagus, associated with reflux symptoms

Oropharyngeal dysphagia originates from neuromuscular disorders (cortical, bulbar, peripheral), while esophageal dysphagia can be mechanical or neuromuscular.

**Q:** In a patient with dysphagia, what features suggest proceeding directly to endoscopy vs. trial of PPI?

**A:** If age >50 with alarm features (weight loss, dysphagia progression) → endoscopy; If age <50 with no alarm features and concomitant GERD → trial PPI first

Age and alarm features guide the approach. Progressive symptoms, weight loss, and older age warrant earlier endoscopic evaluation.

**Q:** What is the endoscopic appearance of Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Salmon pink mucosa in the distal esophagus

The characteristic salmon-pink (versus pink) color difference between gastric and esophageal mucosa helps identify Barrett's during endoscopy.

**Q:** What is the management approach for low-grade dysplasia in Barrett's esophagus?

**A:** Either surveillance (every 6 months for 1 year then annually) OR endoscopic ablation/resection are satisfactory options

Low-grade dysplasia has more treatment options than high-grade, with both surveillance and intervention being acceptable approaches.

**Q:** What are alarm features in GERD that warrant EGD?

**A:** Anorexia, weight loss, bleeding, dysphagia - especially in chronic GERD or symptoms suggestive of complicated disease

Alarm features suggest possible complications or malignancy and warrant endoscopic evaluation regardless of duration of symptoms.

**Q:** What CNS manifestations are seen in Wilson's disease?

**A:** Basal ganglia (wing flapping tremor, Parkinsonism), cerebellum (dysarthria, dysphagia, incoordination, ataxia), cerebrum (psychosis, affective disorder)

Neurological involvement results from basal ganglia degeneration, with patients presenting with movement disorders, cerebellar signs, and psychiatric symptoms.

**Q:** What proportion of GI bleeds are upper GI?

**A:** 75% of GI bleeds are upper gastrointestinal

Most GI bleeding originates from the upper GI tract proximal to the ligament of Treitz.

**Q:** What is the transfusion threshold for acute upper GI bleeding?

**A:** Transfuse at hemoglobin <70-80 g/L (or <100 g/L in patients with cardiovascular disease)

Restrictive transfusion strategy (targeting 70-80 g/L) is associated with better outcomes in UGIB.

**Q:** What are the neurological manifestations of Wilson's disease?

**A:** Basal ganglia (wing-flapping tremor, Parkinsonism), cerebellum (dysarthria, dysphagia, incoordination, ataxia), cerebrum (psychosis, affective disorder)

Neurological involvement typically presents in younger patients with movement disorders, psychiatric symptoms, and cognitive decline due to copper deposition in the brain.

**Q:** What CNS manifestations can occur in Wilson's disease?

**A:** Basal ganglia (wing-flapping tremor, Parkinsonism), cerebellum (dysarthria, dysphagia, incoordination, ataxia), cerebrum (psychosis, affective disorder)

Neurological involvement in Wilson's disease results from copper deposition in the basal ganglia, cerebellum, and cerebral cortex, causing movement disorders, psychiatric symptoms, and cognitive changes.

**Q:** What are the key components of the Canadian consensus for H. pylori infection management in adults?

**A:** The Canadian Helicobacter pylori consensus conference provides updated recommendations for diagnosis, treatment, and eradication in adult patients

The Canadian consensus (J Gastroenterol 1999) addresses screening, treatment regimens, and follow-up for H. pylori infection in Canada

**Q:** When is IVIG indicated in dermatomyositis?

**A:** Severe or refractory muscle weakness, particularly with dysphagia and/or dyspnea

IVIG provides rapid improvement in severe cases with significant weakness or respiratory involvement.

## Gastroenterology

**Q:** What is the most common cause of transient relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter, and what is the most effective therapy for GERD?

**A:** Increased transient relaxations of the LES are the most common cause of GERD. PPIs are the most effective therapy (symptom relief is 80% sensitive for reflux diagnosis).

GERD diagnosis is usually clinical based on heartburn and regurgitation (80% sensitive and specific). An empiric 8-week PPI trial is first-line. Gastroscopy is indicated for red flags (bleeding, weight loss, dysphagia) or refractory symptoms.

**Q:** A 60-year-old male with long-standing GERD undergoes endoscopy showing salmon-pink mucosa in the distal esophagus. Biopsy shows intestinal metaplasia. What is the diagnosis, the rate of malignant transformation, and the recommended surveillance?

**A:** Barrett's esophagus (BE). Malignant transformation rate is ~0.12%/year (prior to dysplasia). Surveillance: gastroscopy every 3-5 years if no dysplasia.

BE is more common in males, age >50, White individuals, smokers, and those with long-standing GERD. High-grade dysplasia has a 32-59% transformation rate over 5-8 years. Treatment includes high-dose PPI indefinitely and endoscopic ablation for high-grade dysplasia.

**Q:** What percentage of duodenal ulcers are caused by H. pylori, and what is the recommended first-line eradication regimen?

**A:** 90% of duodenal ulcers are caused by H. pylori. First-line: concomitant quadruple therapy - PPI BID + amoxicillin 1000 mg BID + clarithromycin 500 mg BID + metronidazole 500 mg BID for 14 days.

An alternative is bismuth quadruple therapy (PPI + bismuth subsalicylate + metronidazole + tetracycline). H. pylori is also responsible for 60% of gastric ulcers. NSAIDs account for the remaining major etiology.

**Q:** A 30-year-old woman presents with chronic diarrhea, iron deficiency anemia, and bloating. She has a family history of celiac disease. What is the initial screening test and the diagnostic gold standard?

**A:** Initial screening: serum anti-tissue transglutaminase (anti-tTG) IgA antibody (90-98% sensitive, 94-97% specific) with concomitant serum IgA level. Gold standard: small bowel (duodenal) biopsy showing villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes.

Serum IgA must be measured simultaneously since IgA deficiency causes false-negative anti-tTG. Celiac disease is associated with HLA-DQ2 (90%) and HLA-DQ8. Treatment is a strict gluten-free diet. Refractory cases should prompt evaluation for lymphoma.

**Q:** What are the key differences between Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis in terms of distribution, depth of involvement, and common complications?

**A:** Crohn's: any GI tract location (mouth to anus), skip lesions, transmural inflammation, fistulas/strictures/abscesses. UC: continuous from rectum proximally, limited to colon, mucosal/submucosal only, higher risk of colorectal cancer and toxic megacolon.

UC has more liver complications (especially PSC) and greater colorectal cancer risk (5% at 10 years, 15% at 20 years for pancolitis). Crohn's uniquely causes fistulas due to transmural involvement. Colectomy is curative for UC but not for Crohn's.

**Q:** A patient with IBS asks about the diagnosis. What criteria are used and what are the four subtypes?

**A:** Rome IV criteria: recurrent abdominal pain associated with defecation or change in stool frequency/form. Four subtypes: IBS-D (diarrhea predominant), IBS-C (constipation predominant), IBS-M (mixed), IBS-untyped.

IBS is a disorder of gut-brain interaction with visceral hypersensitivity as the hallmark. Worldwide prevalence is 5-7%. Psychological factors (anxiety, depression, early life trauma) are risk factors. Diagnosis is primarily clinical with no specific diagnostic test.

**Q:** A patient presents with acute bloody diarrhea. Using the CHESS mnemonic, what organisms cause acute bloody diarrhea?

**A:** CHESS: Campylobacter, Hemorrhagic E. coli (O157:H7), Entamoeba histolytica, Salmonella, Shigella.

Bloody diarrhea suggests inflammatory (mucosal damage) diarrhea. Antimotility agents like loperamide are contraindicated in bloody diarrhea due to risk of toxic megacolon. Enterohemorrhagic E. coli O157:H7 can cause HUS, and antibiotics may worsen outcomes.

**Q:** What is the earliest laboratory finding in cirrhosis, and what is the sequence of subsequent abnormalities?

**A:** Earliest: fall in platelet count <150 (from hypersplenism). Sequence: platelets fall first, then WBC decreases, then hemoglobin drops, then INR rises, then albumin falls, and finally bilirubin rises in severe disease.

Pancytopenia in cirrhosis results from portal hypertension causing hypersplenism. Thrombocytopenia is often the first clue. The Child-Pugh score (bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, encephalopathy) and MELD score quantify severity and guide transplant decisions.

**Q:** What is Gilbert's syndrome, how common is it, and how is it differentiated from more serious causes of jaundice?

**A:** Gilbert's syndrome is a hereditary mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia due to deficiency of glucuronyl transferase. Affects 7% of population. Differentiation: isolated elevated unconjugated bilirubin that worsens with fasting/illness, normal liver enzymes, no other clinical implications.

Gilbert's is autosomal dominant, presents in teens-20s, and is entirely benign requiring only reassurance. It differs from Crigler-Najjar syndrome which has severe or complete deficiency of glucuronyltransferase and can be life-threatening.

**Q:** A 40-year-old man with ulcerative colitis presents with fatigue and pruritus. ALP is markedly elevated. MRCP shows beading of intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts. What is the diagnosis and key associated risks?

**A:** Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC). Key risks: cholangiocarcinoma (10-15%), 5-fold increased risk of colon cancer (vs. UC alone), secondary biliary cirrhosis from repeated cholangitis episodes.

PSC is associated with UC in 70-80% of cases (usually males). Surveillance includes annual colonoscopy for CRC, MRCP at least annually for cholangiocarcinoma, and 6-monthly imaging for HCC if cirrhosis. Liver transplantation is the definitive treatment (>90% 1-year survival).

**Q:** A 45-year-old man presents with epigastric pain, and new-onset dyspepsia. He is H. pylori negative and does not take NSAIDs. Endoscopy shows no ulcer. What is the most likely diagnosis and management?

**A:** Functional dyspepsia (negative endoscopy in setting of dyspeptic symptoms). Management: lifestyle modifications (decrease caffeine/alcohol, avoid citrus, smaller meals, smoking cessation), PPI or H2 blocker trial, consider TCAs or prokinetics (metoclopramide).

Functional dyspepsia is the most common cause of dyspepsia. Only 5% of patients with dyspepsia have ulcers. Red flags (VBAD: vomiting, bleeding/anemia, abdominal mass, dysphagia) warrant endoscopy. Patients <60 without red flags should be tested for H. pylori first.

**Q:** What is autoimmune hepatitis, what are the characteristic serological findings, and what is the first-line treatment?

**A:** Chronic inflammatory liver disease from loss of tolerance to liver antigens. Serological findings: hypergammaglobulinemia (elevated IgG), positive ANA and/or anti-smooth muscle antibody. Treatment: corticosteroids (80% respond) + azathioprine.

Autoimmune hepatitis can be severe with 40% mortality at 6 months without treatment. Liver biopsy shows periportal (zone 1) interface hepatitis. It can overlap with PSC and PBC. Most patients relapse when corticosteroids are withdrawn without azathioprine.

**Q:** What is the Child-Pugh classification system for cirrhosis, and what are the survival implications of each class?

**A:** Child-Pugh uses 5 parameters (bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, encephalopathy), each scored 1-3. Class A (5-6 points): life expectancy 15-20 years, 0% perioperative mortality. Class B (7-9): transplant candidate, 30% perioperative mortality. Class C (10-15): ~3 months life expectancy, ~82% perioperative mortality.

The Child-Pugh score quantifies cirrhosis severity and guides management decisions including transplant evaluation. The MELD score (based on bilirubin, INR, creatinine) is preferred for transplant allocation as it more accurately predicts 3-month mortality.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for H. pylori eradication?

**A:** Bismuth quadruple therapy (PPI BID + bismuth subsalicylate QID + metronidazole QID + tetracycline 500 mg QID) x 7-14 days, OR concomitant non-bismuth quadruple therapy (PPI BID + clarithromycin 500 mg BID + amoxicillin 1000 mg BID + metronidazole 500 mg BID).

For dyspepsia in patients <60 without high-risk features, test for H. pylori first. Patients ≥60 should undergo upper endoscopy. PPIs should have at least 1 trial off medication per year.

**Q:** Why should antibiotics be avoided in E. coli O157:H7 (EHEC) infection?

**A:** Antibiotics increase the risk of hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). Antidiarrheals also increase HUS risk.

EHEC produces Shiga toxin, contamination from hamburger or raw milk. 10% of patients develop HUS. Renal function must be monitored. The incubation period is 3-8 days and illness lasts 5-10 days.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea and what is the empiric treatment?

**A:** ETEC (enterotoxigenic E. coli) is most common (80-90% are bacterial). Empiric treatment for moderate-severe illness: ciprofloxacin or azithromycin (preferred in SE Asia due to fluoroquinolone resistance).

Up to 50% of travelers to developing countries are affected. Rehydration is the mainstay. Bismuth subsalicylate provides 60% prophylactic efficacy. Antibiotic prophylaxis is not recommended routinely.

**Q:** What serologic test is most sensitive and specific for celiac disease, and what is the gold standard?

**A:** Anti-tTG IgA antibody (90-98% sensitive, 94-97% specific). Measure serum IgA simultaneously due to false negatives with IgA deficiency. Gold standard is small bowel mucosal biopsy showing villous atrophy.

Celiac disease is associated with HLA-DQ2 (90%) and HLA-DQ8 (5%). Earliest biopsy finding is increased intraepithelial lymphocytes, followed by crypt hyperplasia, then villous atrophy. Gluten-free diet should not start before biopsy.

**Q:** How do you differentiate Crohn's disease from ulcerative colitis clinically?

**A:** CD: any GI tract location ('gum to bum'), skip lesions, transmural inflammation, granulomas, fistulae/strictures, less rectal bleeding. UC: rectum always involved, continuous mucosal inflammation, very common rectal bleeding, no granulomas, toxic megacolon risk.

CD commonly presents with RLQ pain (ileal disease) mimicking appendicitis. UC presents with bloody diarrhea as the hallmark. Smoking increases CD risk but is protective in UC. Both carry increased colon cancer risk.

**Q:** What are the extraintestinal manifestations of IBD?

**A:** Dermatologic (erythema nodosum, pyoderma gangrenosum), rheumatologic (peripheral arthritis, ankylosing spondylitis, sacroiliitis), ocular (uveitis, episcleritis), hepatobiliary (PSC, cholelithiasis), urologic (oxalate stones), thromboembolism, osteoporosis.

PSC is associated with UC in 70-80% of cases and increases colon cancer risk 5-fold. Cholelithiasis occurs in 15-35% of ileal CD. Erythema nodosum is more common in CD; PSC is more common with UC.

**Q:** What are the Rome IV criteria for irritable bowel syndrome?

**A:** Recurrent abdominal pain for >6 months, at least 1 day/week in last 3 months, associated with ≥2 of: (1) related to defecation, (2) change in stool frequency, (3) change in stool form.

IBS subtypes are determined by Bristol Stool Chart: IBS-D (diarrhea), IBS-C (constipation), IBS-M (mixed). Investigations can be limited to CBC and celiac serology. Low FODMAP diet helps pain, bloating, and gas.

**Q:** What is the serum-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG) and how is it interpreted?

**A:** SAAG = serum albumin - ascitic fluid albumin. SAAG ≥11 g/L indicates portal hypertension (cirrhosis, heart failure, Budd-Chiari). SAAG <11 g/L indicates non-portal HTN causes (peritoneal carcinomatosis, TB, pancreatic disease).

Ascitic fluid total protein >25 g/L suggests cardiac portal hypertension; <25 g/L suggests cirrhosis. Treatment of ascites includes Na restriction (<2g/day), diuretics (spironolactone + furosemide), and therapeutic paracentesis for refractory cases.

**Q:** How is spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) diagnosed and treated?

**A:** Diagnosis: absolute neutrophil count in ascitic fluid >250 cells/mm³. Treatment: IV cefotaxime 2g q8h or ceftriaxone 2g daily x 5 days, PLUS IV albumin (1.5 g/kg at diagnosis, 1 g/kg on day 3).

SBP complicates 10% of cirrhotic ascites with higher risk during GI bleeds. One-third of patients are asymptomatic. E. coli is the most common pathogen. Long-term prophylaxis with norfloxacin or TMP-SMX after the first episode.

**Q:** What is the hepatitis B serology pattern for chronic inactive (immune control) phase?

**A:** HBsAg+, anti-HBs−, HBeAg−, anti-HBe+, anti-HBc IgG+, low HBV-DNA (<2000 IU/mL), normal ALT/AST.

The four phases of chronic HBV are: immune tolerance, immune clearance, chronic inactive (immune control), and HBeAg-negative chronic hepatitis. Risk of reactivation to phase 2 exists, especially with immunosuppression.

**Q:** What distinguishes the hepatitis B serology of resolved infection from immunization?

**A:** Resolved infection: anti-HBs+, anti-HBe+, anti-HBc IgG+. Immunization: anti-HBs+ only (all other markers negative).

Anti-HBc IgG is the key differentiator — it is present in resolved natural infection but absent after vaccination alone. Anti-HBc IgM indicates acute infection.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chronic hepatitis C?

**A:** Direct acting antiviral (DAA) tablets: Maviret (glecaprevir/pibrentasvir) or Epclusa (sofosbuvir/velpatasvir). Pan-genotypic, oral, interferon-free regimens. Check for hepatitis B before starting (risk of reactivation).

70-80% of acute HCV becomes chronic; of these, 20% develop cirrhosis within 20 years. The major risk factor is injection drug use. HCV can cause cryoglobulinemia and membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Wilson's disease (ABCD mnemonic)?

**A:** Asterixis; Basal ganglia degeneration (parkinsonian features in the young); Ceruloplasmin decreased, Corneal copper deposits (Kayser-Fleischer rings); Dementia.

Wilson's disease is autosomal recessive (ATP7B gene). Suspect if liver enzymes elevated with neuropsychiatric symptoms at age <40. Screening: low ceruloplasmin, Kayser-Fleischer rings, increased urinary copper. Treatment: penicillamine, trientine, zinc.

**Q:** What is the clinical presentation of hereditary hemochromatosis (mnemonic)?

**A:** Arthralgia, Bronze skin, Cardiomyopathy/Cirrhosis, Diabetes (pancreatic damage), hypogonadism (anterior pituitary damage). Also known as 'bronze diabetes.'

HFE gene mutations (C282Y most common) cause decreased hepcidin and increased GI iron absorption. Screen with transferrin saturation >45% and ferritin >400 ng/mL. Treatment is phlebotomy. Normal life expectancy if treated before cirrhosis.

**Q:** What is the earliest laboratory finding in cirrhosis?

**A:** Fall in platelet count below 150,000 (from hypersplenism). Later: INR rises, albumin falls, bilirubin rises.

The progression follows the 'W' pattern of liver dysfunction tests. Compensated cirrhosis can last 10-20 years. Decompensation presents with ascites (most common), variceal bleeding, or encephalopathy.

**Q:** What is the Child-Pugh scoring system and what does each class predict?

**A:** Based on bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, and encephalopathy (5 criteria, 1-3 points each). Class A (5-6): 15-50 yr survival, 0% perioperative mortality. Class B (7-9): transplant candidate, 30% mortality. Class C (10-15): 1-3 mo survival, 82% mortality.

MELD score (based on creatinine, INR, bilirubin, sodium) is used for transplant prioritization. MELD >15 generally warrants transplant evaluation. Usual causes of death: renal failure (hepatorenal syndrome), sepsis, GI hemorrhage.

**Q:** What are the precipitating factors for hepatic encephalopathy (HEPATICS)?

**A:** Hemorrhage in GI tract/Hypokalemia, Excess dietary protein, Paracentesis, Alkalosis/Anemia, Trauma, Infection, Colon surgery, Sedatives.

Treatment includes lactulose (titrated to 2-3 soft stools/day to lower colonic pH and convert NH3 to non-diffusible NH4+) and oral rifaximin. Protein restriction is no longer recommended given concurrent malnutrition risk.

**Q:** How do you differentiate primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC) from primary biliary cholangitis (PBC)?

**A:** PSC: male predominant, associated with IBD (especially UC), affects both intra/extrahepatic ducts, MRCP shows beading. PBC: female predominant (M:F=1:9), associated with other autoimmune diseases, affects intrahepatic ducts only, positive anti-mitochondrial antibodies.

PSC increases risk of cholangiocarcinoma (10-15%) and colon cancer (5x in those with UC). PBC is treated with ursodeoxycholic acid. Both may progress to cirrhosis requiring liver transplant.

**Q:** What is the etiology of acute pancreatitis using the mnemonic I GET SMASHED?

**A:** Idiopathic, Gallstones (45%), Ethanol (35%), Tumours, Scorpion stings, Microbiological, Autoimmune, Surgery/trauma, Hyperlipidemia/Hypercalcemia/Hypothermia, Emboli/ischemia, Drugs.

Gallstones and alcohol account for 80% of cases. ALT >150 is specific for biliary cause. CT with IV contrast is most useful when done >1 day after presentation. Necrotic pancreas does not take up contrast dye.

**Q:** What are Cullen's sign and Grey-Turner's sign?

**A:** Cullen's sign: periumbilical ecchymosis (sensitive, not specific for acute pancreatitis). Grey-Turner's sign: flank ecchymosis (specific, not sensitive for acute pancreatitis).

Both signs indicate retroperitoneal hemorrhage. In pancreatitis, they suggest severe necrotizing disease. Inglefinger's sign (pain worse supine, better leaning forward) is also characteristic of pancreatitis.

**Q:** What are the Ranson criteria for acute pancreatitis prognosis?

**A:** At admission: Age >55, WBC >16, Glucose >11 mmol/L, AST >250, LDH >350. During initial 48h: Hct drop >10%, BUN rise >1.8 mmol/L, Calcium <2 mmol/L, Base deficit >4, Fluid sequestration >6L. Score <3: 2% mortality; ≥3: severe pancreatitis.

Treatment is supportive: hemodynamic stability, analgesia, oxygen. IV fluids with careful monitoring for third spacing. Antibiotics only for documented infection, not prophylaxis. Enteral nutrition is preferred over TPN.

**Q:** What is Gilbert's syndrome?

**A:** Hereditary predominantly unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia due to deficiency of glucuronyl transferase. Affects 7% of population, especially males. Autosomal dominant (70% UGT gene mutation). Entirely benign, requires only reassurance.

Presents in teens-20s with intermittent jaundice during fasting or acute illness. All other liver function is normal. Distinguished from Crigler-Najjar syndrome which involves severe or complete deficiency of glucuronyl transferase.

**Q:** What is Charcot's triad and Reynolds' pentad in ascending cholangitis?

**A:** Charcot's triad: fever, RUQ pain, jaundice (present in 50-70%). Reynolds' pentad adds: hypotension and altered mental status (suppurative cholangitis).

Cholangitis requires biliary drainage, ideally via ERCP. Common pathogens include E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, and Enterococcus. Reynolds' pentad carries approximately 50% mortality. Broad-spectrum antibiotics are required.

**Q:** What is the treatment for bleeding esophageal varices?

**A:** 1) Assess hemodynamic stability and resuscitate. 2) IV octreotide (splanchnic vasoconstriction). 3) Endoscopic variceal ligation (EVL) or sclerotherapy. 4) IV ceftriaxone (antibiotics reduce mortality). TIPS if refractory.

Variceal bleeding has 30% risk in first year, 50-70% rebleeding rate, and 20% mortality at 6 weeks. Non-selective beta-blockers (propranolol, nadolol, carvedilol) reduce bleeding risk as prophylaxis. Restrictive transfusion strategy (Hb <70) is preferred.

**Q:** What are the complications of ileal resection in Crohn's disease based on length resected?

**A:** <100 cm resected: watery diarrhea/cholorrhea (impaired bile salt absorption) — treat with cholestyramine. >100 cm resected: steatorrhea (reduced surface area, bile salt deficiency) — treat with fat restriction and medium-chain triglycerides.

This distinction is clinically important because cholestyramine (bile salt binder) would worsen steatorrhea in patients with >100 cm resection. At least 50% of CD patients have clinical recurrence within 5 years of surgery; 85% within 15 years.

**Q:** What is the MASLD (formerly NAFLD) and its key treatment?

**A:** Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease: hepatic steatosis with ≥1 cardiometabolic risk factor and no other cause. Key treatment: gradual weight loss (0.5-1 kg/week); ≥7-10% body weight loss improves disease; >10% loss may reverse steatohepatitis.

Most common cause of liver disease in North America. Component of metabolic syndrome. AST/ALT ratio <1 (unlike alcoholic liver disease where AST:ALT >2:1). Main causes of death are cardiovascular disease and malignancy, not liver disease itself.

**Q:** What is the AST:ALT ratio significance in liver disease?

**A:** ALT > AST = most causes of hepatitis. AST > ALT = alcoholic liver disease or other causes that have progressed to advanced cirrhosis. AST:ALT >2:1 is characteristic of alcoholic hepatitis.

In alcoholic hepatitis, both are usually <300. Associated findings include elevated MCV, elevated WBC (neutrophils), and elevated GGT. Maddrey's discriminant function >32 or MELD >21 indicates severe disease warranting corticosteroid consideration.

**Q:** What distinguishes hepatocellular from cholestatic liver enzyme patterns?

**A:** Hepatocellular: elevated AST/ALT with normal or mildly elevated ALP. Cholestatic: elevated ALP/GGT with normal or mildly elevated AST/ALT. If ALP elevated alone, rule out bone disease by fractionating ALP or checking GGT.

Cholestatic pattern suggests bile duct obstruction (stones, cancer, PSC), destruction of microscopic ducts (PBC), bile acid transporter defects, or liver infiltration (metastases, granulomas). A predominant AST/ALT rise can occur acutely with sudden biliary obstruction.

**Q:** What is the Forrest classification for peptic ulcer bleeding and risk of rebleed?

**A:** Ia: arterial bleeding (55-100% rebleed), Ib: oozing (55-100%), IIa: visible vessel (43%), IIb: sentinel clot (22%), IIc: hematin-covered flat spot (10%), III: no stigmata/clean base (5%).

Dual endoscopic therapy (epinephrine injection + thermal hemostasis or clips) is standard of care for high-risk lesions. IV PPI decreases rebleed risk. Restrictive transfusion (Hb <80 g/L without cardiovascular disease) is recommended.

**Q:** What is the hallmark of chronic pancreatitis and its most common cause?

**A:** Hallmark: abdominal pain. Most common cause: alcohol (>90% of chronic pancreatitis). Late features (when >90% function lost): steatorrhea, diabetes, calcification, weight loss.

Serum amylase and lipase are usually normal in chronic pancreatitis. EUS is the most sensitive test. Fecal elastase is low in steatorrhea. Treatment includes EtOH abstinence, pancreatic enzyme replacement, analgesics, and surgical drainage if duct is dilated.

**Q:** Which bacterial pathogen is the MOST COMMON cause of bacterial diarrhea in Canada?

**A:** Campylobacter jejuni

Campylobacter jejuni, transmitted primarily via uncooked poultry, is the most common bacterial cause of diarrhea in Canada. It is also associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome as a post-infectious complication. Treatment with macrolides or fluoroquinolones is indicated if diarrhea lasts >1 week, is bloody, or occurs in the immunocompromised.

**Q:** A patient develops bloody diarrhea after eating undercooked hamburger. Antibiotics are withheld. Why?

**A:** EHEC (E. coli O157:H7) is suspected; antibiotics increase the risk of hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). Antidiarrheal agents also increase HUS risk.

Enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC/STEC, O157:H7) produces Shiga toxin and is associated with HUS in ~10% of cases. Both antibiotics and antidiarrheal agents increase the risk of HUS. Management is supportive with monitoring of renal function.

**Q:** What is the recommended first-line empiric treatment for Clostridium difficile infection? What is used for fulminant (previously termed 'severe') infection?

**A:** Empiric treatment: vancomycin (oral) OR fidaxomicin. For fulminant C. difficile infection: oral vancomycin; add IV metronidazole if ileus is present. Metronidazole may be used if access to empiric treatment is limited.

C. difficile colitis typically follows antibiotic use (especially clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, cephalosporins) or recent hospitalization. The culprit antibiotic should be stopped if possible. For fulminant disease, oral vancomycin is the drug of choice, with IV metronidazole added if ileus prevents drug reaching the colon.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of Bacillus cereus Type A (emetic) vs. Type B (diarrheal) food poisoning?

**A:** Type A (emetic): preformed exotoxin in rice dishes; onset 1–6h, nausea/vomiting, <12h duration. Type B (diarrheal): secondary endotoxin from meats/vegetables/cereals; onset 8–16h, diarrhea, <24h duration. Neither requires antimicrobial therapy.

B. cereus produces two distinct toxin syndromes. The emetic form (preformed heat-stable toxin) has a very short incubation, while the diarrheal form (enterotoxin produced in vivo) has a longer onset. Both are self-limited and require only supportive care.

**Q:** Which organism causes 'traveller's diarrhea' most commonly, and what is the empiric antibiotic regimen?

**A:** ETEC (Enterotoxigenic E. coli) is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea. Empiric antibiotics for moderate-to-severe disease: ciprofloxacin, azithromycin, or rifaximin. Note increasing fluoroquinolone resistance, especially in South/Southeast Asia.

Bacterial pathogens cause 80–90% of traveller's diarrhea, with ETEC being the leading cause. ETEC produces heat-labile and heat-stable toxins. Rehydration is the mainstay of therapy. Antibiotic prophylaxis is NOT recommended due to risk of resistant organism infection.

**Q:** A child presents with RLQ pain, fever, and diarrhea mimicking appendicitis. Stool cultures are pending. What pathogen should be considered?

**A:** Yersinia enterocolitica. It can cause mesenteric adenitis and terminal ileitis that mimic appendicitis, and can also mimic Crohn's disease of the terminal ileum. Most cases occur in children <4 years old.

Yersinia can present without diarrhea as isolated mesenteric adenitis or terminal ileitis, closely mimicking acute appendicitis. It is transmitted via contaminated food and unpasteurized milk. Fluoroquinolones are used only for severe illness.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Giardia lamblia infection ('beaver fever')? How is it treated?

**A:** Risk factors: daycare children, intake of untreated water, MSM (men who have sex with men), immunodeficiency (decreased IgA). Treatment: metronidazole or nitazoxanide. Treatment of asymptomatic carriers is NOT recommended. May require duodenal biopsy for diagnosis.

Giardia is a protozoan that causes fecal-oral diarrhea with a 1–4 week incubation. It causes abdominal pain and nausea/vomiting but not fever or bloody stool. IgA deficiency increases susceptibility, which is also relevant because IgA deficiency is more prevalent in celiac disease patients.

**Q:** What is the treatment of Entamoeba histolytica infection? What complication can occur if untreated?

**A:** Symptomatic infection: metronidazole + iodoquinol OR paromomycin. Asymptomatic cyst passage: iodoquinol or paromomycin alone. If untreated: potential for liver abscess. Sigmoidoscopy may show flat ulcers with yellow exudates.

E. histolytica is a worldwide parasite transmitted fecal-orally, causing bloody diarrhea with fever and abdominal pain. The key complication is amebic liver abscess. Treatment requires both a luminal agent (iodoquinol/paromomycin) and a tissue agent (metronidazole) for symptomatic disease.

**Q:** What is the Dukoral® vaccine? What does it protect against and when is it recommended by the Public Health Agency of Canada?

**A:** Oral vaccine protecting against Vibrio cholerae (~80% efficacy) and ETEC (~50–67% efficacy). Requires 2 doses taken 2 weeks prior to travel; effect lasts up to 3 months. Recommended (not routine) for: gastric hypochlorhydria, young children >2yr, high-risk travellers with chronic illness (chronic renal failure, CHF, T1DM, IBD), immunosuppression, history of recurrent traveller's diarrhea, and travellers to cholera-endemic countries.

Dukoral is an oral killed whole-cell V. cholerae vaccine with recombinant cholera toxin B subunit. It provides cross-protection against ETEC due to shared B subunit homology. Antibiotic prophylaxis for traveller's diarrhea is not recommended.

**Q:** Define chronic diarrhea. What investigations are recommended?

**A:** Passage of frequent unformed stool for >4 weeks (persistent diarrhea lasts 14–30 days). Investigations: stool (C. difficile toxin, C&S, O&P, fecal fat, WBC, fecal calprotectin); blood (CBC, electrolytes, CRP, TSH, celiac serology: IgA anti-tTG + serum immunoglobulin quantitation); colonoscopy/ileoscopy with biopsy; upper GI endoscopy with duodenal biopsy.

Majority of chronic diarrhea is non-infectious. Celiac serology (anti-tTG IgA) should be paired with serum IgA quantitation to detect IgA deficiency, which can cause false-negative tTG results. A trial of lactose-free diet should be used with caution as it may delay diagnosis of IBD or celiac disease.

**Q:** Distinguish maldigestion from malabsorption.

**A:** Maldigestion: inability to break down large molecules in the intestinal lumen into component small molecules (e.g., pancreatic exocrine deficiency, bile salt deficiency, enzyme deficiencies). Malabsorption: inability to transport molecules across the intestinal mucosa into circulation (e.g., celiac disease, Giardia, Whipple's disease, small bowel resection).

Maldigestion occurs in the intestinal lumen, while malabsorption occurs at the mucosal level. Common causes of maldigestion include cystic fibrosis (pancreatic exocrine insufficiency), pancreatitis, and bile salt deficiency from terminal ileal disease. Both lead to similar clinical manifestations including steatorrhea and nutritional deficiencies.

**Q:** Where in the GI tract are iron, calcium, folic acid, and vitamin B12 absorbed?

**A:** Iron: duodenum and upper jejunum. Calcium: duodenum and upper jejunum. Folic acid: jejunum. Vitamin B12: ileum (after complex formation with intrinsic factor from stomach; pancreatic proteases cleave R-protein in basic medium to allow B12-IF complex formation).

Proximal small bowel absorbs iron, calcium, and folate. This means celiac disease (predominantly proximal) causes iron, calcium, and folate deficiency more commonly than B12 deficiency. B12 absorption requires intrinsic factor (from parietal cells), gastric acid, and a functional terminal ileum.

**Q:** What are the clinical manifestations of fat-soluble vitamin deficiencies (ADEK)?

**A:** Vitamin A: night blindness, dry skin, keratomalacia. Vitamin D: osteomalacia (adults), rickets (children). Vitamin E: retinopathy, neurological problems. Vitamin K: prolonged INR causing bleeding (risk increased after prolonged broad-spectrum antibiotics or starvation).

Fat-soluble vitamins (ADEK) require adequate fat digestion and absorption via micelles. Steatorrhea from any cause can lead to fat-soluble vitamin deficiencies. Vitamin K is also synthesized by intestinal flora, making prolonged antibiotic use a risk factor for deficiency.

**Q:** What is the gold standard investigation for steatorrhea, and what are the initial screening tests?

**A:** Gold standard: 72-hour stool collection (weight and fat content). Initial qualitative screening: Sudan stain of stool for fat globules. Other useful tests: fecal elastase (screens for pancreatic insufficiency), serum tTG-IgA + abdominal imaging (most useful—celiac disease and chronic pancreatitis are the two most common causes of steatorrhea).

Steatorrhea (excessive fat in stool) is caused by fat maldigestion or malabsorption. Quantitative fecal fat >7% is diagnostic. The two most common causes are celiac disease and chronic pancreatitis, so anti-tTG IgA serology and imaging of the pancreas are first-line investigations.

**Q:** What are the consequences of ileal resection <100 cm vs. >100 cm?

**A:** <100 cm resected: impaired bile salt absorption → watery diarrhea (cholorrhea/choleretic diarrhea). Treatment: cholestyramine or loperamide. >100 cm resected: reduced mucosal surface + bile salt deficiency → steatorrhea/fatty diarrhea. Treatment: fat restriction, medium chain triglycerides.

The pivotal number is 100 cm of terminal ileum. With smaller resections, the remaining bile salts enter the colon and cause secretory diarrhea. With larger resections, bile salt pool is depleted and fat cannot be absorbed, causing steatorrhea. Cholestyramine is a bile-salt binding resin used for the smaller resection scenario.

**Q:** What is the pathogenesis of celiac disease? What are the HLA associations?

**A:** Autoimmune disease triggered by gluten (specifically gliadin, the toxic protein). HLA-DQ2 (chromosome 6): found in ~90% of celiac patients vs. 20% of general population. HLA-DQ8: found in ~5% of celiac patients. HLA-DQ2/8 are necessary permissive genes but not sufficient—up to 40% of White individuals carry these alleles without developing celiac disease. The auto-antigen is tissue transglutaminase (tTG).

Celiac disease is unique in that the genetics (HLA-DQ2/8), the auto-antigen (tTG), and the environmental trigger (gluten from wheat, barley, rye) are all known. It is associated with other autoimmune diseases including Sjögren's syndrome, T1DM, and thyroid disease.

**Q:** What is the correct sequence of pathological changes in celiac disease, from earliest to latest?

**A:** 1. Increased intraepithelial lymphocytes (earliest finding) → 2. Crypt hyperplasia → 3. Villous atrophy (last stage). Diagnosis requires small bowel mucosal biopsy (usually duodenum).

Villous atrophy is the hallmark histologic finding but is the LAST stage to develop. The differential diagnosis for villous atrophy is broad and includes small bowel overgrowth, Crohn's disease, lymphoma, Giardia, and HIV. Biopsy should not be deferred for a trial of gluten-free diet, as this could normalize histology and falsely exclude celiac disease.

**Q:** How is celiac disease diagnosed serologically? What is the important caveat regarding IgA deficiency?

**A:** Serum anti-tTG IgA: 90–98% sensitive, 94–97% specific. CAVEAT: Selective IgA deficiency (more prevalent in celiac patients) causes false-negative anti-tTG IgA. Therefore, serum immunoglobulin quantitation (IgA level) must be measured simultaneously. If IgA deficient, use tTG IgG and/or DGP (deamidated gliadin peptide) IgG.

IgA deficiency is more frequent in individuals with celiac disease than in the general population. Measuring only anti-tTG IgA without checking total IgA can miss celiac disease in IgA-deficient patients. A gluten-free diet should not be started before serological tests and biopsy are completed.

**Q:** What extraintestinal manifestations are associated with celiac disease?

**A:** Dermatitis herpetiformis, bone fractures/metabolic bone disease (osteoporosis), infertility, epilepsy, myopathy, depression, paranoia, and cerebellar ataxia. Also increased risk of lymphoma (enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma), adenocarcinoma (small bowel, colon), and autoimmune diseases.

Celiac disease has numerous extraintestinal manifestations. Dermatitis herpetiformis is the classic skin finding. The risk of lymphoma may be reduced by strict adherence to a gluten-free diet. Poor response to gluten-free diet should prompt investigation for non-adherence, concurrent diseases (microscopic colitis, pancreatic insufficiency), or development of T-cell lymphoma.

**Q:** What is the dietary treatment for celiac disease? Which grains are permitted and which must be avoided?

**A:** Avoid: wheat, barley, rye (and by default any grain with gliadin-like toxic proteins). Permitted: rice flour, corn flour. Oats: allowed ONLY if certified gluten-free (grown without cross-contamination). Also supplement: iron, folate, and other vitamins as needed.

Gluten-free diet is the cornerstone of treatment for celiac disease. Inadvertent or advertent non-adherence is the most common cause of poor response to treatment. Oats contain avenin, a protein that is immunologically distinct from gliadin; certified gluten-free oats may be acceptable in a subgroup of patients.

**Q:** What gene mutation is associated with Crohn's disease, especially in Ashkenazi Jews? What does its gene product do?

**A:** CARD15/NOD2 gene mutation. The CARD15 gene product modulates NF-κB, which is required for the innate immune response to microbial pathogens (primarily expressed in monocytes-macrophages). Heterozygous mutation: relative risk 3; Homozygous: relative risk 40. Associated with ileal involvement, fistulizing, fibrostenotic, and stricturing disease.

CARD15/NOD2 is the most important genetic locus in Crohn's disease. It is associated particularly with ileal CD, early onset, and fibrostenotic/fistulizing disease phenotypes. IBD has a polygenomic pattern with over 200 associated gene loci.

**Q:** Compare the GI location, rectal bleeding, diarrhea type, and histology of Crohn's disease (CD) vs. Ulcerative Colitis (UC).

**A:** CD: Any part of GI tract ('gum to bum'); small bowel + colon 50%, small bowel only 30%, colon only 20%; rectal bleeding uncommon; transmural distribution with skip lesions, non-caseating granulomas, deep fissures, glands intact. UC: Isolated to large bowel; ALWAYS involves rectum; rectal bleeding very common (90%); mucosal distribution, continuous disease (no skip lesions), crypt abscesses, granulomas absent.

The key differentiating histologic features are: CD = transmural + skip lesions + granulomas; UC = mucosal only + continuous + no granulomas. Endoscopically, CD shows cobblestoning and segmental inflammation while UC shows continuous diffuse erythema and friability.

**Q:** What are the most common and most serious surgical complications unique to Crohn's disease vs. Ulcerative Colitis?

**A:** Crohn's disease: strictures, fistulae (entero-cutaneous, entero-vesical, entero-vaginal, entero-enteric), perianal disease, abscesses, perforation. Ulcerative Colitis: toxic megacolon (transverse colon >6 cm on abdominal X-ray), greater risk of colorectal cancer (8-fold relative risk overall; 5% at 10 yr, 15% at 20 yr for pancolitis).

Fistulae are characteristic of CD due to transmural inflammation with deep fissuring. UC has greater colorectal cancer risk; pancolitis >8 years warrants surveillance colonoscopy with biopsy and/or chromoendoscopy. Patients with UC + PSC have 5-fold increased colon cancer risk and need annual surveillance.

**Q:** What are the extraintestinal manifestations (EIMs) of IBD? Which are more common in CD vs. UC?

**A:** Dermatologic: erythema nodosum (CD > UC), pyoderma gangrenosum (CD > UC), perianal skin tags (CD >> UC), oral mucosal lesions (CD >> UC). Rheumatologic: peripheral arthritis (CD > UC), ankylosing spondylitis (CD > UC), sacroiliitis (equal). Ocular: uveitis and episcleritis (CD > UC). Hepatobiliary: PSC (1–5% IBD, more common UC in men), cholelithiasis (15–35% of ileal CD). Urologic: oxalate stones (CD with ileal disease and intact colon), uric acid stones (ileostomy).

Most EIMs are more common in CD, particularly those related to perianal and colonic involvement. PSC is more associated with UC and is a significant risk factor for colorectal cancer (5-fold increase). Oxalate stones in CD occur because ileal disease leads to unabsorbed fat binding calcium, leaving oxalate free for intestinal absorption.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of Crohn's disease in Canada?

**A:** 135,000 Canadians living with CD. Worldwide incidence 3–15 to 10–20 per 100,000. Bimodal age of onset: primary peak before age 30, second smaller peak at age 60. M = F. Incidence increasing, especially in young females. More common in White people and Ashkenazi Jews. Smoking incidence in CD patients is higher than general population.

Canada has one of the highest rates of IBD in the world. Unlike UC, smoking is a risk factor for CD (whereas smoking is protective in UC). The increasing incidence, especially in young females and in Asian immigrants adopting Western lifestyles, suggests environmental factors.

**Q:** What are the medical management options for Crohn's disease in order from mild to severe (traditional step-up approach)?

**A:** 1. Lifestyle/diet + antidiarrheal agents. 2. 5-ASA (sulfasalazine, mesalamine) — limited role, for mild ileitis only. 3. Antibiotics (metronidazole, ciprofloxacin) — particularly for perianal CD. 4. Corticosteroids (prednisone 40 mg/d; budesonide 9 mg/d for mild-moderate ileal/right colon CD). 5. Immunosuppressives (azathioprine, 6-MP, MTX) — steroid-sparing, maintain remission. 6. Biologics (anti-TNF: infliximab, adalimumab; ustekinumab; risankizumab; vedolizumab). 7. JAK inhibitors (tofacitinib). NOTE: Top-down management (early biologics + immunosuppressives) now widely accepted as best practice.

The CALM trial (Lancet 2017) demonstrated that tight control using biomarkers (CRP, fecal calprotectin) to guide treatment intensification led to better mucosal healing compared to clinical evaluation alone. Dual therapy (infliximab + azathioprine) is more effective than monotherapy. Corticosteroids have NO role in maintaining remission.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of vedolizumab? For which IBD conditions is it indicated?

**A:** Vedolizumab is a monoclonal antibody against integrin α4β7, reducing lymphocyte trafficking to the gut. It is indicated for ulcerative colitis and Crohn's disease (now primarily indicated for UC).

Vedolizumab has gut-selective anti-inflammatory action. Unlike systemic anti-TNF agents, it does not cause systemic immunosuppression. It is used for moderate-to-severe UC and CD that is steroid-unresponsive or steroid-dependent, or has failed other biologic agents.

**Q:** What are the indications for surgery in Ulcerative Colitis, and what curative procedure is performed?

**A:** Indications: failure of adequate medical therapy, toxic megacolon, uncontrollable bleeding, pre-cancerous changes (dysplasia on endoscopy/biopsy), inability to taper corticosteroids, overt malignancy. Curative procedure: colectomy with ileal pouch-anal anastomosis (IPAA — restorative). Permanent ileostomy eliminates risk of cancer recurrence post-colectomy.

UC can be cured by colectomy, unlike CD where surgery treats complications but cannot cure the disease. IPAA (J-pouch) allows bowel continuity without a permanent stoma. After permanent ileostomy, there is no residual colorectal cancer risk. The colectomy rate is ~1% per year after the first year; 20–25% eventually undergo colectomy.

**Q:** What is the risk of colorectal cancer in Ulcerative Colitis? What surveillance is recommended?

**A:** Risk: 5% at 10 years, 15% at 20 years for pancolitis; overall relative risk = 8-fold. Risk is higher with: longer duration, greater extent (pancolitis > left-sided > proctitis), active mucosal inflammation, concurrent PSC (5-fold additional risk). Surveillance: surveillance colonoscopy with biopsy and/or chromoendoscopy for pancolitis >8 years; annual surveillance for UC + PSC. Proctitis alone has the SAME risk as general population.

5-ASA derivatives may have chemopreventive effects against colorectal cancer in UC. Patients with both UC and PSC have the highest risk and require annual colonoscopy. Fecal calprotectin is a useful stool surrogate marker for monitoring mucosal inflammation activity.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and smoking relationship in Ulcerative Colitis?

**A:** 120,000 Canadians live with UC. Worldwide incidence 3–15 to 10–20 per 100,000. Two-thirds onset by age 30 (second peak after age 50). M = F. Small hereditary contribution (15% have 1st-degree relative with disease). REDUCED risk in smokers (protective effect — opposite to CD).

The inverse relationship between smoking and UC (and the positive relationship with CD) is well-established. Smoking appears to have protective effects on colonic mucus and immune regulation in UC. In contrast, smoking worsens CD. This distinction is tested on the MCCQE1.

**Q:** What is toxic megacolon? What are its radiologic criteria and which IBD condition is it most associated with?

**A:** Toxic megacolon: acute non-obstructive dilation of the colon with systemic toxicity and immediate risk of perforation. Radiologic criteria: transverse colon diameter >6 cm on abdominal X-ray. Most associated with Ulcerative Colitis. Also associated with Shigella and C. difficile. Antidiarrheal medications should be avoided in severe colitis to prevent precipitation.

Toxic megacolon is a life-threatening complication requiring urgent surgical consultation. It can complicate UC (more common), C. difficile colitis, and Shigella infection. The radiologic cutoff is >6 cm diameter of the transverse colon. Antidiarrheal agents (e.g., loperamide) and anticholinergics can precipitate it.

**Q:** What defines Irritable Bowel Syndrome (IBS) and what are its subtypes?

**A:** IBS is a disorder of gut-brain interaction characterized by abdominal pain and alteration in bowel habit. Diagnosis uses Rome IV Criteria. Subtypes (based on Bristol Stool Chart): IBS-D (predominant diarrhea), IBS-C (predominant constipation), IBS-M (mixed: both diarrhea and constipation each >25% of abnormal bowel movements), IBS-Untyped (insufficient abnormalities for C, D, or M).

IBS affects 5–7% of the population worldwide, with onset usually in young adulthood. It is a clinical diagnosis with no specific diagnostic test. Pathophysiology involves visceral hypersensitivity (hallmark), altered central processing, immune cell dysfunction, changes in intestinal permeability, altered serotonin signaling, and gut microbiome changes.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of IBS? What psychosocial factors are associated?

**A:** IBS is a disorder of the gut-brain axis. Hallmark: visceral hypersensitivity. Mechanisms: altered central processing of pain signals, immune cell dysfunction, increased intestinal permeability, altered gut serotonin signaling, altered gut microbiome, and altered GI motility (in a subset). Psychosocial risk factors: early life trauma/adversity, anxiety/depression, chronic stressors.

IBS is multifactorial and varies between patients. Unlike IBD, there is no gross or histologic mucosal inflammation. The biopsychosocial model is important—psychological factors contribute to severity. Post-infectious IBS can develop after acute gastroenteritis.

**Q:** What is the role of tofacitinib and upadacitinib in IBD treatment?

**A:** Tofacitinib: non-selective JAK (Janus Kinase) inhibitor; demonstrated efficacy in UC. Upadacitinib: gut-selective JAK inhibitor; used for outpatients with moderate-to-severe UC. Both used for patients with failure of other biologic agents, steroid-unresponsive or steroid-dependent disease.

JAK inhibitors are small molecule immunotherapy options. They are orally administered, unlike biologics (infliximab, adalimumab) which require IV or SC administration. Tofacitinib is also experimentally being studied for CD.

**Q:** What is ozanimod and for which condition is it used?

**A:** Ozanimod is an oral sphingosine-1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator used for treating moderate-to-severe Ulcerative Colitis. It does NOT require previous failure of immunosuppressants, glucocorticoids, biologics, or other small molecules. It increases clinical remission rates compared to placebo.

Ozanimod is a newer oral small molecule for UC. S1P receptor modulators work by trapping lymphocytes in lymph nodes, reducing gut inflammation. Its key feature is that it can be used without requiring prior step-up therapy failure.

**Q:** What are the side effects of azathioprine/6-mercaptopurine (6-MP) in IBD treatment?

**A:** Vomiting, pancreatitis, bone marrow suppression, increased risk of malignancy (e.g., lymphoma). Takes >3 months for beneficial effect; usually continued for several years. Acts as a steroid-sparing agent; used to maintain remission rather than treat active inflammation. Used in combination with biologics (dual therapy) to increase efficacy and lower risk of tolerance/anti-drug antibody development.

Azathioprine/6-MP are thiopurine immunosuppressants metabolized by TPMT (thiopurine methyltransferase). Testing TPMT activity before initiation can identify patients at risk for severe bone marrow suppression. Combining with anti-TNF therapy reduces immunogenicity and increases sustained drug efficacy.

**Q:** Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC) in IBD: what is the association, and what is its impact on colorectal cancer risk?

**A:** PSC occurs in 1–5% of IBD cases with colonic involvement and is more common in men with UC. Patients with UC AND PSC have a 5-fold increased risk of colorectal cancer compared to UC alone. Annual surveillance colonoscopy with biopsy and/or chromoendoscopy is required in this high-risk group.

PSC is a cholestatic liver disease that is strongly associated with UC. The mechanism linking PSC to increased colorectal cancer risk in UC is not fully elucidated but likely involves biliary acids and altered gut microbiome. Gallstones (pigment stones) are also associated with CD.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and indication for budesonide in IBD? Why is it preferred over prednisone in certain situations?

**A:** Budesonide is a first-pass metabolism corticosteroid (minimal systemic effects). Used at 9 mg/day for mild-to-moderate acute exacerbations of terminal ileum + right colon CD. In UC: oral budesonide CR (colonic extended release) can be used for mild-to-moderate disease. Preferred over prednisone because it has fewer systemic side effects due to high first-pass hepatic metabolism (~90% extraction).

Budesonide's topical anti-inflammatory effect is concentrated in the ileum and right colon (CD formulation) or colon (UC formulation). It does not have a proven role in maintaining remission (similar to prednisone). For severe/fulminant IBD, IV methylprednisolone is preferred.

**Q:** What is Vibrio cholerae infection? Describe its clinical features and treatment.

**A:** V. cholerae from contaminated food/water (especially shellfish). Incubation 1–3 days. Massive watery diarrhea (1–3 L/day). No fever, blood, or abdominal pain. Duration 3–7 days. Treatment: tetracycline or fluoroquinolones (ciprofloxacin). Mortality <1% with treatment. Vaccine (Dukoral) ~80% efficacy.

Cholera produces an enterotoxin that activates adenylyl cyclase, causing massive secretory diarrhea ('rice-water stools'). Rehydration is critical. Antibiotics shorten the disease course and duration of shedding. Dukoral vaccine is recommended for travellers to endemic areas at increased risk.

**Q:** What are the stool characteristics and treatment of Cryptosporidium infection? In whom is it most severe?

**A:** Cryptosporidium: fecal-oral transmission, 1-day incubation, watery diarrhea (no blood), fever, nausea/vomiting. Duration 1–20 days. Treatment: paromomycin + nitazoxanide. In immunosuppressed patients (e.g., HIV): immune reconstitution is key in addition to pharmacological treatment.

Cryptosporidium is an important cause of diarrhea in immunocompromised individuals, particularly those with HIV/AIDS. Unlike other parasites, it can cause severe, prolonged, life-threatening diarrhea in immunosuppressed patients. In immunocompetent individuals, it is typically self-limited.

**Q:** A patient with Shigella dysentery asks for loperamide (Imodium) for symptom relief. Should it be prescribed?

**A:** No. Antidiarrheal agents (e.g., loperamide) may increase the risk of toxic megacolon in Shigella dysentery and should be avoided. Treatment is with a fluoroquinolone. Only very small inocula are needed for Shigella infection (low infectious dose).

Shigella causes dysentery (bloody diarrhea with fever and tenesmus) with a 1–4 day incubation. Antidiarrheal agents are contraindicated because they delay pathogen clearance and increase the risk of toxic megacolon — same principle applies in severe UC. Complications include toxic megacolon and HUS.

**Q:** What is the CALM trial (Lancet 2017) and what were its conclusions for Crohn's disease management?

**A:** CALM was a multicenter RCT (224 patients, 22 countries). Patients with active CD were randomized to intensify treatment based on laboratory biomarkers (CRP + fecal calprotectin) vs. clinical evaluation alone (CD activity index + prednisone use). At 2 years, more patients in the biomarker-guided group achieved complete mucosal healing (no ulcers). Conclusion: tight control using biomarkers leads to better mucosal healing than symptom-based management alone.

The CALM trial provided evidence for a 'treat-to-target' strategy in CD using objective measures (CRP, fecal calprotectin) rather than symptoms alone, since symptoms correlate poorly with actual mucosal inflammation. This supports the top-down management strategy and early use of immunosuppressants/biologics.

**Q:** What is the prognosis and course of Ulcerative Colitis over time?

**A:** Chronic relapsing pattern in most patients. 10–15% have a chronic continuous pattern. Nearly all patients have >1 attack. More colonic involvement in the 1st year correlates with increased severity and higher colectomy rate. Colectomy rate: ~1% of all patients per year after the first year; 20–25% eventually undergo colectomy. Normal life expectancy. Proctitis alone: usually benign course, lifetime risk of extension is 15%.

UC is characterized by remissions and relapses. 5% of cases are fulminant. 5-ASA maintenance therapy decreases the yearly relapse rate from 60% to 15%. Fecal calprotectin is a useful non-invasive marker for monitoring disease activity.

**Q:** What is the non-typhoidal Salmonella infection? Who requires antibiotic treatment?

**A:** Non-typhoidal Salmonella (e.g., S. Typhimurium, S. Enteritidis): transmitted via contaminated animal food products (eggs, poultry, meat, milk). Incubation 12–72h. Fever, bloody stool, nausea/vomiting, abdominal pain. Duration 3–7 days. Ciprofloxacin only for: severe illness, extremes of age, joint prostheses, valvular heart disease, severe atherosclerosis, cancer, uremia.

Most non-typhoidal Salmonella infections are self-limited and do not require antibiotics in healthy adults. Antibiotics are reserved for patients with risk factors for complicated disease (bacteremia, endovascular infection). Antibiotic treatment may prolong fecal carriage in uncomplicated disease.

**Q:** How is vitamin B12 absorbed? What steps are required?

**A:** 1. B12 ingested and bound to R proteins (from salivary glands). 2. Stomach secretes intrinsic factor (IF) in acidic medium. 3. In basic (alkaline) medium of small intestine, pancreatic proteases cleave R protein. 4. B12 forms covalent bond with IF (protecting from protease attack). 5. B12-IF complex absorbed in the ileum. 6. B12 binds to transcobalamin (TC) in circulation. Deficiency causes: subacute combined degeneration of spinal cord, peripheral/optic neuropathy, dementia, megaloblastic anemia, glossitis.

The multi-step B12 absorption process means deficiency can result from: inadequate intake (vegans), lack of intrinsic factor (pernicious anemia, gastrectomy), pancreatic insufficiency (cannot cleave R protein), terminal ileal disease or resection. Anti-intrinsic factor antibodies and atrophic gastritis point to pernicious anemia.

**Q:** A patient undergoes ileal resection for Crohn's disease. What are the surgical complications and how do they depend on length resected?

**A:** < 100 cm resected: watery diarrhea (cholorrhea) due to bile salt malabsorption causing colonic secretion. Treatment: cholestyramine (bile-salt binding resin) or antidiarrheals (loperamide — more convenient and potent). > 100 cm resected: steatorrhea due to depleted bile salt pool + reduced absorptive surface. Treatment: fat restriction, medium-chain triglycerides. If <50% or <200 cm functional small intestine remains: short bowel syndrome risk.

The ileocecal valve and length/location of resection are key factors. Cholestyramine is specific for bile salt diarrhea but is non-specific; loperamide is often preferred for convenience. Fat restriction and MCTs bypass the need for bile salts (MCTs are absorbed directly without micelle formation).

**Q:** What is the genetic defect in Wilson's disease and what is the mode of inheritance?

**A:** Autosomal recessive defect in the ATP7B gene, causing impaired copper elimination (decreased biliary copper excretion and decreased incorporation of copper into ceruloplasmin).

Wilson's disease results from mutations in ATP7B, which encodes a copper-transporting ATPase. The consequence is copper accumulation in liver, brain, kidneys, and cornea. Worldwide incidence is 1 in 40,000.

**Q:** List the four major organ systems involved in Wilson's disease and their key manifestations.

**A:** 1) Liver: acute hepatitis, ALF, chronic active hepatitis, cirrhosis (low HCC risk). 2) Eyes: Kayser-Fleischer rings (copper in Descemet's membrane). 3) CNS: basal ganglia (wing-flapping tremor, Parkinsonism), cerebellum (dysarthria, ataxia), cerebrum (psychosis). 4) Kidneys: Fanconi syndrome (proximal tubule transport defects) and stones. Also blood (intravascular hemolysis) and joints.

KF rings are present in only 50% of isolated liver involvement but more commonly in CNS involvement. Suspect Wilson's in any young patient (<40) with combination of liver disease, dystonia, and/or psychiatric symptoms.

**Q:** What are the three screening tests and the gold standard investigations for Wilson's disease?

**A:** Screening: 1) Reduced serum ceruloplasmin (<50% of normal). 2) Kayser-Fleischer rings (slit-lamp examination). 3) Increased 24-hour urinary copper excretion. Gold standard: 1) Increased copper on liver biopsy by quantitative assay. 2) Genetic analysis (imperfect due to many ATP7B mutations).

Screen first-degree relatives. KF rings require slit-lamp examination. Genetic analysis is imperfect because many ATP7B mutations are possible, making quantitative liver copper the definitive test.

**Q:** What are the four drugs used to treat Wilson's disease and when is each preferred?

**A:** 1) Penicillamine: chelates copper, poorly tolerated. 2) Trientine: chelates copper. 3) Zinc: impairs copper excretion in stool and decreases gut absorption; used for maintenance or neurological presentations. 4) Tetrathiomolybdate: preferred if neurological involvement. Hepatic presentations are best treated with trientine + zinc combination. Liver transplant for severe liver failure.

Tetrathiomolybdate is preferred for neurological Wilson's because penicillamine can worsen neurological symptoms. Zinc acts by inducing intestinal metallothionein, which sequesters copper and prevents absorption.

**Q:** Define hereditary hemochromatosis: genetics, mechanism, and epidemiology.

**A:** Autosomal recessive (HFE gene mutations, most commonly C282Y/C282Y or C282Y/H63D). Decreased hepcidin production leads to increased GI iron absorption and tissue iron deposition despite adequate stores. Total body iron increases to 20–40 g (normal ~1 g). Most common in Northern European descent; homozygous prevalence 1/400 but incomplete penetrance.

C282Y/H63D compound heterozygote is only potentially causative. Non-HFE mutations (ferroportin, hemojuvelin, hepcidin, aceruloplasminemia) are rare. Secondary hemochromatosis occurs with transfusions, iron-loading anemias, and other liver diseases.

**Q:** What is the classic clinical pentad of hereditary hemochromatosis?

**A:** RECT mnemonic + Bronze skin: Arthralgia (especially MCP joints), Bronze/grey skin (melanin, not iron), Cardiomyopathy (diastolic dysfunction early, dilated cardiomyopathy late), Cirrhosis (with 200x increased HCC risk), Diabetes (pancreatic damage), plus Hypogonadism (anterior pituitary damage — hypogonadotropic hypogonadism).

Patients often present with trivially elevated transaminases found incidentally. Skin discoloration is due to melanin, not iron deposition. Chondrocalcinosis may be seen on imaging. Diastolic dysfunction and arrhythmias precede dilated cardiomyopathy.

**Q:** What screening tests are used for hemochromatosis and what are the diagnostic thresholds?

**A:** Transferrin saturation (free Fe²⁺/TIBC) >45%; serum ferritin >400 ng/mL (>1000 ng/mL indicates need for MRI or biopsy). HFE gene analysis: 90% of primary hemochromatosis involves C282Y allele. MRI is most sensitive and specific non-invasive modality. Liver biopsy is used to detect cirrhosis or if other causes of liver disease are suspected.

Liver biopsy generally needed if: age >40, elevated liver enzymes, or ferritin >1000 ng/mL (markers of advanced fibrosis). If C282Y/C282Y with no markers of advanced fibrosis, biopsy is generally not needed. Screen 25% chance siblings.

**Q:** What is the treatment for hereditary vs. secondary hemochromatosis and what is the prognosis?

**A:** Hereditary: weekly or q2-week phlebotomy, then lifelong maintenance q2–6 months targeting ferritin 50–100 ng/mL. Responds well to phlebotomy. Secondary: usually requires chelation therapy (e.g., deferoxamine) if phlebotomy is contraindicated (e.g., cardiomyopathy, anemia). Prognosis: normal life expectancy if treated before cirrhosis or DM develop.

Chelation therapy binds and sequesters iron for excretion. Ferritin may never normalize if other causes of high ferritin coexist (e.g., metabolic syndrome, alcohol). HCC screening is required if cirrhosis develops.

**Q:** Describe the pathophysiology of alcohol-related liver disease (ArLD) including the histological triad of alcoholic hepatitis.

**A:** Ethanol → acetaldehyde: reduces NAD⁺ to NADH, impairing lipolysis → fatty acid/TG accumulation. EtOH also increases gut permeability → bacterial translocation; causes relative hypoxia in zone III (central veins). Histological triad: 1) Ballooned hepatocytes with Mallory bodies (eosinophilic intracellular cytokeratin aggregates) surrounded by neutrophils. 2) Large fat globules. 3) Chicken-wire fibrosis (perivenular and space of Disse).

Zone III is near central veins and poorly oxygenated, making it most vulnerable to EtOH-induced injury. Mallory bodies are characteristic of alcoholic hepatitis but not pathognomonic. Neutrophilic infiltration distinguishes alcoholic from other causes of steatohepatitis.

**Q:** What is the blood test pattern in alcohol-related liver disease (ArLD) and how does it differ from MASLD?

**A:** ArLD: AST:ALT ratio >2:1 (both usually <300 U/L); increased GGT; increased MCV; leukocytosis (neutrophilia) with acute alcoholic hepatitis. MASLD (formerly NAFLD): AST:ALT ratio <1; elevated AST, ALT, and ALP; echogenic liver on ultrasound.

The AST:ALT >2:1 ratio in ArLD results partly from alcohol-induced mitochondrial injury preferentially raising AST, and pyridoxine (B6) deficiency reducing ALT synthesis. In MASLD, AST/ALT <1 is characteristic, reflecting different patterns of hepatocyte injury.

**Q:** What scores are used to assess severity of alcoholic hepatitis and guide treatment with corticosteroids?

**A:** Maddrey's discriminant function (based on PT and bilirubin): score >32 indicates severe disease, consider corticosteroids. MELD score >21 also indicates severe disease. Lille model (bilirubin response at day 7 of corticosteroids) guides continuation of full course. Pentoxifylline is less used — the most definitive trial did not demonstrate efficacy.

Corticosteroids (prednisone) are used for severe alcoholic hepatitis (Maddrey >32 or MELD >21). The Lille score at day 7 predicts response: poor responders may not benefit from continued steroids. 30-day mortality in severe disease is 30–60%.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of severe alcoholic hepatitis with and without continued drinking?

**A:** Immediate mortality (first 6 months if severe): 30–60%. With continued EtOH: 70% mortality at 5 years. With cessation: 30% mortality at 5 years. Hepatic steatosis: complete resolution with EtOH cessation. Chronic cirrhosis risk: 10–15% of individuals with EtOH use disorder.

The threshold for significant risk is >2–3 standard drinks/day in females and >3–6 standard drinks/day in males for >10 years; however only ~10–20% of heavy drinkers develop cirrhosis. Clinical findings do not accurately predict the type of liver involvement.

**Q:** Define MASLD (formerly NAFLD) and MetALD, and describe the key pathogenic mechanism.

**A:** MASLD: hepatic steatosis with ≥1 cardiometabolic risk factor, no other cause for steatotic liver disease.
