MetALD: hepatic steatosis with ≥1 cardiometabolic risk factor plus alcohol intake above threshold (>7 drinks/week women; >10 drinks/week men). Key pathogenesis: insulin resistance leads to hepatic steatosis. Histology is indistinguishable from alcoholic hepatitis.

MASLD is the most common cause of liver disease in North America. Risk factors include obesity, T2DM, hypertension, and hypertriglyceridemia (metabolic syndrome components). The renaming from NAFLD to MASLD emphasizes the metabolic underpinning.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for MASLD (formerly NAFLD) with level of evidence?

**A:** 1) Gradual weight loss 0.5–1 kg/week (mainstay); ≥7–10% body weight loss reverses MASH; rapid weight loss worsens disease. 2) Pioglitazone (evidence exists, but risk of weight gain and CHF). 3) Vitamin E 800 IU/day — evidence for non-diabetic, non-cirrhotic patients with hepatic inflammation. 4) Coffee 1–3 cups/day (some evidence). 5) Bariatric surgery. 6) GLP-1 analogues (e.g., semaglutide) — improve MASH via weight loss but not proven to improve liver fibrosis.

Rapid weight loss can mobilize free fatty acids quickly and worsen steatohepatitis. GLP-1 analogues are promising but currently their fibrosis benefit is not established. The main causes of death in non-cirrhotic MASLD are cardiovascular disease and malignancy.

**Q:** Define Acute Liver Failure (ALF) and list the most common causes.

**A:** ALF: severe decline in liver function with coagulation abnormality (INR >1.5) and encephalopathy, in the setting of previously normal liver, with rapid onset (<26 weeks duration). Common causes: drugs (especially acetaminophen — most common), hepatitis B (measure anti-HBc IgM), hepatitis A, ischemic hepatitis, autoimmune hepatitis, Wilson's disease, idiopathic. Hepatitis C causes ALF rarely.

Measure anti-HBc IgM in ALF suspected of HBV because HBV-DNA and HBsAg can rapidly become negative in fulminant HBV. Acetaminophen is the leading cause in Canada and should always be considered.

**Q:** What are the King's College criteria for liver transplantation in Acute Liver Failure?

**A:** Consider early transplant (especially) if: time from jaundice to encephalopathy >7 days (not extremely rapid onset); age <10 or >40 years; cause is drug or unknown; bilirubin >300 µmol/L; INR >3.5; creatinine >200 µmol/L. Chief value of liver biopsy is to exclude chronic disease; less helpful for prognosis.

The King's College criteria differ for acetaminophen vs. non-acetaminophen ALF. Early consideration for transplant is critical as the window is narrow. Biopsy should be considered before INR becomes too elevated to be safely performed.

**Q:** Define cirrhosis: compensated vs. decompensated, and what are the most common causes?

**A:** Cirrhosis: diffuse liver architecture distortion with fibrosis and regenerative nodules; biopsy is gold standard. Compensated: absence of complications, can last 10–20 years with near-normal life expectancy. Decompensated: development of ascites (most common), variceal bleeding, or encephalopathy. Causes: steatotic liver disease (alcohol or MASLD), chronic viral hepatitis B/C (not A or E), autoimmune hepatitis, hemochromatosis, PBC, drugs (MTX, amiodarone), cardiac cirrhosis (Budd-Chiari), Wilson's disease, α1-antitrypsin deficiency.

Hepatitis A and E do NOT cause chronic liver disease or cirrhosis. Cryptogenic cirrhosis often represents 'burnt-out MASH.' Fibrosis may regress if the underlying cause is treated. MELD-Na score includes creatinine, INR, total bilirubin, and serum sodium.

**Q:** Describe the progression of laboratory findings in cirrhosis in order of appearance.

**A:** Earliest: fall in platelet count <150 (from hypersplenism). Then: rise in INR (decreased clotting factor synthesis). Then: fall in albumin. Finally (in severe disease): rise in bilirubin. Pre-terminal: hypoglycemia (more characteristic of severe ALF).

The sequential lab changes reflect the progressive loss of synthetic function. Hypersplenism causing pancytopenia affects platelets first, then WBC, then hemoglobin. The INR-to-bleeding tendency relationship is controversial — some patients are hypo-, others hypercoagulable.

**Q:** What is the Child-Pugh score and how is it interpreted?

**A:** Five parameters (1–3 points each): serum bilirubin, serum albumin, INR, ascites (absent/controllable/refractory), encephalopathy (absent/minimal/severe). Class A (5–6 pts): 15–50 year life expectancy, 0% perioperative mortality. Class B (7–9 pts): transplant candidate, 30% perioperative mortality. Class C (10–15 pts): ~3 months life expectancy, 82% perioperative mortality.

Child-Pugh is useful for quantifying cirrhosis severity and perioperative risk. MELD score (creatinine, INR, bilirubin, Na) is now preferred for transplant listing (use MELD >15 as threshold for transplant consideration). Usual causes of death in cirrhosis: renal failure (HRS), sepsis, GI bleed.

**Q:** What are the subtypes of Hepatorenal Syndrome (HRS) and how do they differ?

**A:** HRS-AKI: sudden acute renal failure (rapid doubling of creatinine over <2 weeks). HRS-CKD: gradual increase in creatinine with worsening liver function (creatinine doubling over years). HRS can occur at any time in severe liver disease, precipitated by overdiuresis/dehydration, GI bleed, or sepsis.

HRS differs from pre-renal failure — in HRS, IV fluid challenge does NOT improve renal function (urine Na is low in both). Treatment: octreotide + midodrine + albumin (increases systemic vascular resistance → increases renal blood flow). Definitive treatment is liver transplantation.

**Q:** What is hepatopulmonary syndrome (HPS) and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Clinical triad: liver disease + increased alveolar-arterial gradient while breathing room air + evidence of intrapulmonary vascular dilatation. Mechanism: V/Q mismatch (intrapulmonary shunting), failure to clear pulmonary vasodilators. Features: platypnea (dyspnea worsened upright, improved supine) and orthodeoxia (desaturation upright, improved supine). Diagnosis: contrast-enhanced echocardiography — air bubbles appear in LV after >3 heartbeats (normal = no bubbles; VSD = <3 beats). Treatment: liver transplantation (only proven treatment).

HPS occurs mainly in cirrhosis but can also occur in non-cirrhotic portal hypertension. The pathognomonic finding of late bubble appearance (>3 beats) on contrast echo distinguishes intrapulmonary shunting from intracardiac shunting.

**Q:** What are the three sites of increased resistance causing portal hypertension, with examples of each?

**A:** 1) Pre-sinusoidal: portal vein thrombosis, schistosomiasis, sarcoidosis. 2) Sinusoidal: cirrhosis (most common), acute alcoholic hepatitis. 3) Post-sinusoidal: right-sided heart failure, hepatic vein thrombosis (Budd-Chiari), veno-occlusive disease, constrictive pericarditis. Portal hypertension is defined as hepatic venous pressure gradient >5 mmHg.

The site of resistance determines the pattern of disease. Pre-sinusoidal causes portal hypertension without parenchymal liver disease. HVPG >10 mmHg is the strongest predictor of variceal development. HVPG >12 mmHg is associated with variceal bleeding.

**Q:** What are the indications, mechanism, and complications of TIPS (transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt)?

**A:** Indications: acute variceal bleeding, prevention of rebleeding, refractory ascites. Mechanism: radiologically inserted stent between portal and hepatic vein via transjugular catheterization, decreasing portal venous pressure. Complications: hepatic encephalopathy, right-sided heart failure, deterioration of hepatic function. Contraindications: severe liver dysfunction, uncontrolled hepatic encephalopathy, congestive heart failure.

TIPS effectively reduces portal pressure by creating a bypass, but the shunted blood bypasses hepatocytes, increasing the risk of encephalopathy (nitrogenous compounds not metabolized). The PREDESCI trial showed carvedilol lowers risk of decompensation in portal hypertension.

**Q:** Describe the stages of hepatic encephalopathy (HE) and the mnemonic for precipitating factors.

**A:** Minimal HE (diagnosed with specialized cognitive testing). Overt HE stages: I — apathy, restlessness, sleep-wake reversal, slowed intellect, impaired handwriting; II — asterixis, lethargy, drowsiness, disorientation; III — stupor (rousable), hyperactive reflexes, Babinski sign positive; IV — coma (response to pain only or non-responsive). Precipitants (HEPATICS): Haemorrhage (GI)/Hypokalemia, Excess dietary protein, Paracentesis, Alkalosis/Anemia, Trauma, Infection (including SBP), Colon surgery/CNS depressants, Sedatives.

EEG in HE shows diffuse non-focal slow high-amplitude waves. Ammonia is usually elevated but does NOT correlate with severity and is not useful as a standalone diagnostic test. Serum ammonia can be elevated without HE and vice versa.

**Q:** What is the treatment for hepatic encephalopathy, including first-line and second-line agents?

**A:** 1) Treat underlying precipitants. 2) Lactulose/lactitol: titrated to 2–3 soft stools/day; lowers colonic pH converting NH₃ to non-diffusible NH₄⁺; promotes bacteria that produce minimal ammonia; laxative effect eliminates nitrogen-producing bacteria. 3) Oral rifaximin: high-level evidence for both acute treatment and maintenance. 4) Lactulose + rifaximin combination is more effective than lactulose alone (reduces mortality). 5) NG tube or lactulose enemas for patients who cannot take oral medications. Note: routine protein restriction is no longer recommended (malnutrition risk); vegetable protein preferred over animal protein.

The randomized double-blind trial showed rifaximin 1200 mg/day plus lactulose had 76% response rate vs. ~29% for lactulose plus placebo, with significant decrease in mortality. Protein restriction is outdated — sarcopenia worsens outcomes.

**Q:** How is ascites evaluated using the Serum-Ascites Albumin Gradient (SAAG)?

**A:** SAAG = serum albumin − ascitic fluid albumin. SAAG ≥11 g/L (1.1 g/dL): portal hypertension-related (cirrhosis, right heart failure, Budd-Chiari, massive liver metastases, portal vein thrombosis). SAAG <11 g/L: non-portal hypertension-related (peritoneal carcinomatosis, TB peritonitis, pancreatic disease, serositis, nephrotic syndrome). Note: in nephrotic syndrome, serum albumin is already low, making the gradient unreliable.

Cardiac ascites (chronic right heart failure) typically has ascitic fluid total protein >25 g/L, while cirrhotic ascites has total protein <25 g/L — both have SAAG ≥11 g/L. SAAG is more accurate than the exudate/transudate classification using LDH/protein.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic paracentesis protocol and what is the most common bacterial cause of SBP?

**A:** Paracentesis aliquots: 1st — cell count and differential; 2nd — chemistry (albumin, total protein; amylase if pancreatitis suspected; TG/chylomicrons if turbid); 3rd — C&S and Gram stain; 4th — cytology (positive in peritoneal carcinomatosis). SBP diagnosis: absolute neutrophil count >0.25×10⁹ cells/L (250 cells/mm³). Most common organisms: Gram-negatives 70% — E. coli (most common), Klebsiella. Gram stain positive in only 10–50%; culture positive in <80%.

1/3 of SBP patients are asymptomatic — do not hesitate to perform diagnostic paracentesis in any cirrhotic with ascites. SBP occurs in 10% of cirrhotic ascites and has higher risk in patients with GI bleed.

**Q:** What is the treatment and prophylaxis for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP)?

**A:** Treatment: IV cefotaxime 2 g q8h or ceftriaxone 2 g IV daily × 5 days (modify if resistant organisms). IV albumin 1.5 g/kg at diagnosis + 1 g/kg on day 3 — decreases mortality by lowering risk of acute renal failure. Prophylaxis: variceal bleeding — ceftriaxone IV daily or norfloxacin BID × 7 days. Previous SBP episode — long-term daily norfloxacin or TMP-SMX.

IV albumin in SBP treatment prevents the renal failure (a key driver of mortality) by maintaining effective arterial volume and preventing circulatory dysfunction. SBP in cirrhosis carries high 2-year mortality, highlighting the need for transplant evaluation.

**Q:** What is the treatment for refractory ascites in cirrhosis?

**A:** 1) Therapeutic (large-volume) paracentesis with IV albumin (8 g per litre of ascites removed). 2) TIPS (in appropriate patients without contraindications) — has potential transplant-free survival advantage. 3) Liver transplantation — should be considered in every case, as ascites in cirrhosis is associated with high 2-year mortality. Standard therapy: sodium restriction <2 g/day, spironolactone + furosemide, target weight loss 0.5–1 kg/day.

Ascites becomes refractory when diuretics are inadequate or not tolerated. Fluid restriction is only needed for significant hyponatremia (Na <120–125 mmol/L). Overly rapid weight loss increases risk of renal failure by depleting intravascular volume.

**Q:** Describe the approach to jaundice workup — how do dark urine and pale stools help differentiation?

**A:** Dark urine + pale stools: suggests conjugated (direct) hyperbilirubinemia — bile cannot reach gut (biliary obstruction) but conjugated bilirubin is excreted in urine. Pruritus: suggests cholestasis or chronic disease. Painless jaundice in elderly: think pancreatic cancer. Abdominal pain with jaundice: biliary tract obstruction (stone or tumour). Workup: bilirubin fractionation → ultrasound (dilated vs. normal ducts) → MRCP/EUS/ERCP depending on findings.

Unconjugated bilirubin is bound to albumin and cannot be filtered by kidneys, so it does not cause dark urine. Gilbert's syndrome and hemolysis cause unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia without dark urine. ERCP is reserved for therapeutic intervention.

**Q:** What is Gilbert's syndrome — etiology, prevalence, clinical features, and management?

**A:** Hereditary predominantly unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia due to reduced glucuronyl transferase activity (UGT1A1 gene mutation, autosomal dominant, 70% UGT gene mutation). Affects 7% of population, especially males. Presents in teens–20s. Features: intermittent mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia, typically triggered by fasting or acute illness; otherwise asymptomatic. Treatment: reassurance (entirely benign). Distinct from Crigler-Najjar: complete deficiency of glucuronyl transferase.

Gilbert's should be differentiated from hemolysis (no reticulocytosis or anemia in Gilbert's) and from Crigler-Najjar (partial vs. complete enzyme deficiency). Some patients also have decreased hepatobiliary uptake. No other clinical implications.

**Q:** What is Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC) — key associations, investigations, and complications?

**A:** PSC: inflammation, fibrosis, and stricturing of intra- and/or extrahepatic bile ducts. Key association: IBD (70–80% of PSC patients, usually UC, usually male). Investigations: ALP elevated (hallmark), p-ANCA (30–80%), elevated IgM (40–50%), MRCP shows beading pattern (alternating narrowing and dilatation of bile ducts). ERCP reserved for intervention only. Complications: cholangiocarcinoma (10–15%), secondary biliary cirrhosis, colon cancer (5-fold risk vs. UC alone in those with IBD).

PSC is idiopathic in most cases. MRCP is the diagnostic imaging modality of choice; ERCP risks cholangitis. Small-duct PSC variant (~10%) requires liver biopsy. Ursodiol has no proven benefit on natural history and may increase mortality at high doses.

**Q:** What is the surveillance strategy and treatment for PSC?

**A:** Surveillance: MRCP at least annually for cholangiocarcinoma detection (controversial); 6-monthly imaging for HCC surveillance in cirrhotic PSC; annual colonoscopy for colon cancer in those with IBD. Treatment: ABx for cholangitis; emergency drainage for suppurative cholangitis; endoscopic sphincterotomy/stent for dominant CBD strictures; liver transplantation is best treatment for advanced PSC (>90% 1-year survival; mean time to transplant 10–20 years). Ursodiol NOT recommended (no effect on natural history, may worsen at high doses).

PSC has a highly variable course — some patients have indolent disease. The mean time to transplant in those with severe disease is 10–20 years. PSC post-transplant recurrence occurs in some patients. The 5-fold colon cancer risk vs. UC alone requires annual colonoscopy.

**Q:** What drugs (other than acetaminophen) are hepatotoxic and associated with specific liver injury patterns?

**A:** Azoles, statins, methyldopa, phenytoin, propylthiouracil (PTU), rifampin, sulfonamides, tetracyclines. Herbs: khat, chaparral leaf, germander, comfrey, bush tea. Weight loss supplements with green tea extract → acute liver failure. Bodybuilding supplements → prolonged cholestatic jaundice. In cirrhosis: MTX (chronic use) and amiodarone are causes. Tamoxifen, corticosteroids, and MTX are causes of MASLD.

Drug-induced liver injury (DILI) can mimic any pattern of liver disease. PTU is a notable cause of fulminant hepatic failure. Green tea extract in weight loss supplements has been associated with ALF, which is clinically significant in Canada given supplement use.

**Q:** A 28-year-old male presents with liver disease and psychosis. Ceruloplasmin is low. What is the diagnosis, and why might serum ceruloplasmin be falsely normal in this condition?

**A:** Wilson's disease. Ceruloplasmin is an acute-phase reactant and can be falsely elevated (appearing normal or near-normal) during acute inflammation, pregnancy, or estrogen use — making it unreliable as a standalone test. Always combine with 24-hour urinary copper and slit-lamp examination for KF rings. Gold standard: quantitative liver copper by biopsy.

This question tests integration: low ceruloplasmin + psychiatric + liver disease in a young person = Wilson's. Recognizing that ceruloplasmin is an acute-phase protein that can be falsely normal is a critical clinical nuance for MCCQE1. ATP7B genetic testing is imperfect due to many possible mutations.

**Q:** What is the peripheral arterial vasodilation theory of ascites formation?

**A:** Portal hypertension in cirrhosis leads to production of local mediators (especially nitric oxide), causing splanchnic arterial vasodilation. This pulls blood away from the systemic circulation, reducing effective arterial volume. The kidneys compensate with sodium and water retention (activating RAAS, ADH, sympathetic nervous system). The result is increased total circulating volume but decreased effective arterial volume — reconciling the 'underfill' and 'overfill' hypotheses.

This is the most widely accepted theory. It explains why despite expanded total blood volume in cirrhotic ascites, patients have signs of reduced effective circulation. Treatment with spironolactone targets the secondary hyperaldosteronism. Loop diuretics (furosemide) are added to prevent hypokalemia from spironolactone.

**Q:** What clinical findings on physical exam indicate portal hypertension specifically (as distinct from liver failure)?

**A:** Portal hypertension signs: esophageal varices (hematemesis/melena), splenomegaly, caput medusae (dilated periumbilical veins), ascites, hemorrhoids. Liver failure signs (different): encephalopathy/coma, jaundice/scleral icterus, spider angioma, gynecomastia, muscle wasting, bleeding tendency (bruising), loss of sexual hair/testicular atrophy, ankle edema, palmar erythema, Dupuytren's contracture, asterixis, leukonychia (Terry's nails), clubbing, fetor hepaticus, xanthelasma.

Distinguishing these two sets of signs is clinically important — portal hypertension can occur without significant hepatocellular failure (e.g., portal vein thrombosis). Spider angiomas and palmar erythema reflect hyperestrogenemia from impaired hepatic estrogen metabolism.

**Q:** What is the role of non-selective beta-blockers in portal hypertension management?

**A:** Non-selective β-blockers (propranolol, nadolol, carvedilol) decrease risk of bleeding from varices. The PREDESCI trial (Lancet 2019) showed carvedilol in cirrhotic patients with portal hypertension (clinically significant) lowers the risk of decompensation, particularly ascites. They work by: β1-blockade reducing cardiac output; β2-blockade causing splanchnic vasoconstriction, reducing portal blood flow.

NSBBs are first-line primary prophylaxis for medium/large varices. Carvedilol has additional alpha-blocking properties, providing additional portal pressure reduction. The PREDESCI trial was a landmark showing that prevention of decompensation extends beyond just preventing variceal bleeding.

**Q:** What is Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC) and which ducts are affected?

**A:** PBC is a chronic autoimmune condition causing inflammation and fibrous obliteration of intrahepatic bile ductules only (NOT extrahepatic ducts). This contrasts with PSC, which affects both intra- and extrahepatic ducts.

PBC is likely autoimmune, associated with Sjogren's syndrome, CREST syndrome, RA, and thyroiditis. The absence of duct narrowing on MRCP (unlike PSC) is a key distinguishing feature. Diagnosis is confirmed by elevated ALP >6 months plus positive anti-mitochondrial antibodies (AMA).

**Q:** What is the demographic and autoimmune association profile of Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC)?

**A:** PBC predominantly affects middle-aged women (M:F = 1:9). Associated autoimmune conditions include Sjogren's syndrome, scleroderma, CREST syndrome (calcinosis, Raynaud's, esophageal dysfunction, sclerodactyly, telangiectasia), rheumatoid arthritis, and thyroiditis.

The strong female predominance and association with other autoimmune diseases supports an autoimmune etiology. This demographic contrast with PSC (predominantly male, associated with IBD/UC) is a classic MCCQE1 comparison.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic investigations for Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC)?

**A:** Key investigations: (1) Positive anti-mitochondrial antibodies (AMA) — 95% sensitivity and specificity; (2) Elevated ALP and GGT (bilirubin rises late); (3) Elevated IgM; (4) Increased serum cholesterol (mild LDL increase, larger HDL increase); (5) Normal bile duct on MRCP (rules out large/medium duct disease). Elevated ALP >6 months + positive AMA is sufficient for diagnosis — liver biopsy usually not required.

AMA is the hallmark serological test for PBC with high sensitivity and specificity. Normal MRCP differentiates PBC (intrahepatic only) from PSC (which shows beading/narrowing of ducts on MRCP). Bilirubin rises later in the disease course.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC), and what is the second-line agent?

**A:** First-line: Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA/ursodiol). Second-line: Obeticholic acid (if inadequate response to UDCA). Additional supportive treatments: cholestyramine for pruritus, calcium/vitamin D for bone density, bisphosphonates if osteoporosis is present.

UDCA improves survival comparable to age- and sex-matched controls when ALP normalizes. Obeticholic acid is a farnesoid X receptor agonist approved for patients with inadequate UDCA response. Osteoporosis risk is high in PBC due to cholestasis impairing fat-soluble vitamin (D) absorption.

**Q:** Compare Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC) vs. Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC) across sex predominance, associations, affected ducts, and key investigations.

**A:** PSC: Male predominance; associated with IBD (especially UC); affects BOTH intra- and extrahepatic ducts; MRCP shows narrowing and dilatations ('beaded' appearance). PBC: Female predominance (M:F=1:9); associated with other autoimmune disorders (Sjogren's, CREST, RA); affects intrahepatic ducts ONLY; diagnosed by anti-mitochondrial antibodies (AMA), elevated IgM, increased lipids, and absence of duct narrowing on MRCP.

This comparison is high-yield for MCCQE1. PSC has a much higher risk of cholangiocarcinoma. PBC has higher risk of osteoporosis. Both can progress to cirrhosis and may require liver transplantation.

**Q:** What are Charcot's Triad and Reynolds' Pentad, and in what condition do they occur?

**A:** Both occur in ascending cholangitis (infection of the biliary tree). Charcot's Triad (50-70% of cases): Fever + RUQ pain + Jaundice. Reynolds' Pentad (suppurative cholangitis): Fever + RUQ pain + Jaundice + Hypotension + Altered mental status. Reynolds' Pentad carries ~50% mortality.

Ascending cholangitis is caused by biliary stasis due to obstruction (e.g., CBD stone). Common organisms: E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Enterococcus, Bacteroides, Clostridia. Treatment priority is biliary drainage via ERCP, with broad-spectrum antibiotics covering Gram-negatives, Enterococcus, and anaerobes.

**Q:** What is the treatment priority and antibiotic regimen for ascending cholangitis?

**A:** Treatment priority: DRAINAGE (ideally via ERCP; percutaneous biliary or surgical routes if ERCP not possible). Antibiotic options: (1) Ampicillin + sulbactam or piperacillin/tazobactam; (2) Metronidazole + 3rd-generation cephalosporin (e.g., ceftriaxone) or fluoroquinolone (e.g., ciprofloxacin); (3) Carbapenem monotherapy (e.g., imipenem or meropenem). Coverage must include Gram-negatives, Enterococcus, and anaerobes.

Drainage is the cornerstone of treatment because antibiotics alone cannot clear an obstructed biliary system. The choice of antibiotics should cover the typical polymicrobial flora of biliary infections. Good prognosis with effective drainage in mild-moderate cases; Reynolds' Pentad carries ~50% mortality.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of acute pancreatitis? Use the mnemonic GET SMASHED.

**A:** GET SMASHED: Gallstones (45%), Ethanol (35%), Trauma, Steroids (controversial), Mumps/Microbiological, Autoimmune (IgG4, SLE), Scorpion stings, Hyperlipidemia (TG >11.3 mmol/L) / Hypercalcemia / Hypothermia, ERCP/Emboli, Drugs (azathioprine, furosemide, estrogens, valproic acid, etc.). The vast majority are due to gallstones or ethanol.

Gallstones cause pancreatitis via mechanical obstruction of the pancreatic duct. ALT >150 U/L is specific for a biliary cause of pancreatitis. Hypertriglyceridemia must exceed 11.3 mmol/L (1000 mg/dL) to cause pancreatitis.

**Q:** What are the clinical signs of acute pancreatitis, including Grey-Turner's sign and Cullen's sign?

**A:** Classic presentation: epigastric pain (constant, non-colicky), radiating to back, may improve leaning forward (Inglefinger's sign). Associated: N/V, fever (chemical, not infectious initially), abdominal distension from ileus, tender/rigid abdomen. Specific signs of severe/hemorrhagic pancreatitis: Grey-Turner's sign (flank ecchymosis) and Cullen's sign (periumbilical ecchymosis). Complications: jaundice, tetany (hypocalcemia), hypovolemic shock, ARDS, coma.

Grey-Turner's and Cullen's signs indicate retroperitoneal hemorrhage and suggest severe/necrotizing pancreatitis. Lipase is more specific than amylase for pancreatitis and stays elevated longer. Amylase >5x normal almost always indicates pancreatitis or renal failure.

**Q:** What are the radiological findings in acute pancreatitis on plain X-ray and CT scan?

**A:** X-ray findings: 'Sentinel loop' (dilated proximal jejunum), calcifications, 'colon cut-off sign' (colonic spasm). CT scan (most useful, ideally done >1 day after presentation): contrast uptake seen in viable tissue only — non-viable/necrotic areas do NOT take up contrast. The proportion of pancreas not taking up contrast on CT at 48 hours is the most useful severity predictor. Can biopsy necrotic areas to differentiate sterile vs. infected necrosis.

U/S is useful for evaluating the biliary tree (67% sensitivity, 100% specificity for gallstones). MRCP is used to determine etiology; ERCP is reserved for patients requiring biliary intervention. CT at 48 hours is the gold standard for severity assessment and detecting necrosis.

**Q:** According to the Revised 2012 Atlanta Classification, how are pancreatic fluid/solid collections classified?

**A:** Collections in pancreatitis are classified by fluid vs. solid AND acute vs. chronic: Acute Liquid: Acute Peripancreatic Fluid Collection (APFC). Acute Solid: Acute Necrotic Collection (ANC). Chronic Liquid: Pancreatic Pseudocyst. Chronic Solid: Walled-off Necrosis (WON). All collections are further classified as infected or not infected.

This classification has replaced the older 'pseudocyst' terminology for all collections. Walled-off necrosis (WON) is the mature form of ANC and requires drainage if infected. Endoscopic drainage is preferred over surgical necrosectomy when technically possible, and should be delayed >2 weeks to allow demarcation.

**Q:** What is the management of acute pancreatitis? List the treatment goals and key interventions.

**A:** Goals (only supportive therapy available): (1) Hemodynamic stability with IV crystalloids/colloids; (2) Analgesia; (3) Oxygen; (4) Stop progression of damage; (5) Treat local/systemic complications. Key interventions: NG suction if vomiting/distension; nutritional support via NJ feeding or TPN if cannot tolerate enteric feeds (recent evidence supports NG or oral feeds); endoscopic sphincterotomy if severe gallstone pancreatitis with cholangitis/ongoing obstruction; drain/debride if infected necrosis (delay >2 weeks). Antibiotics only if infection confirmed — NOT for prophylaxis.

The WATERFALL trial showed aggressive fluid resuscitation does NOT improve outcomes and increases fluid overload compared to moderate resuscitation. Enteral nutrition is preferred over TPN in pancreatitis (Cochrane data: EN reduces mortality RR 0.50, organ failure, and infections vs. TPN).

**Q:** What does the WATERFALL trial (NEJM 2022) conclude about fluid resuscitation in acute pancreatitis?

**A:** Aggressive fluid resuscitation does NOT improve clinical outcomes compared to moderate resuscitation in acute pancreatitis. Fluid overload developed in 20.5% of aggressively resuscitated patients vs. 6.3% with moderate resuscitation (RR 2.85, P=0.004), with no significant difference in moderately severe or severe pancreatitis development.

This landmark trial changed the management approach — moderate (not aggressive) IV fluid resuscitation is now recommended in acute pancreatitis to avoid fluid overload complications including pulmonary edema and abdominal compartment syndrome.

**Q:** What are the late complications of acute pancreatitis, and how are they managed?

**A:** Late complications (>2 weeks): (1) Pseudocysts — follow if asymptomatic; drain if symptomatic or growing (endoscopic > percutaneous > surgical); (2) Infected necrosis/abscesses — ABx + endoscopic/percutaneous drainage preferred over surgery; (3) Bleeding — from gastric varices (splenic vein thrombosis), pseudoaneurysm (especially splenic artery), or duodenal compression; (4) Splenic/portal vein thrombosis — no proven therapy; (5) Rare: DM, pancreatic duct damage.

The endoscopic transgastric vs. surgical necrosectomy trial showed endoscopic approach reduces major complications (especially new-onset organ failure) compared to surgical necrosectomy. Draining infected necrosis should be delayed >2 weeks to allow adequate encapsulation (walled-off necrosis).

**Q:** What is chronic pancreatitis? What is the most common etiology and what are the late-stage manifestations?

**A:** Chronic pancreatitis is irreversible damage to the pancreas characterized by: (1) pancreatic cell loss from necrosis, (2) inflammation, and (3) fibrosis. Most common cause: Ethanol (causes >90% of chronic pancreatitis). Late-stage manifestations (when >90% of function lost): steatorrhea (maldigestion), diabetes, calcification, jaundice, weight loss, pseudocyst, ascites, GI bleed, chronic pain.

EtOH causes chronic pancreatitis by changing pancreatic secretion composition (increasing viscosity), decreasing lithostathine (which normally solubilizes calcium — leading to duct calcification), direct toxic effects on acinar cells, and stimulating stellate cells to form collagen (fibrosis). Only a minority of people with alcohol use disorder develop pancreatitis.

**Q:** How is chronic pancreatitis investigated? What is the most sensitive test for parenchymal abnormalities?

**A:** Investigations: Lab: elevated fasting glucose, elevated ALP (less commonly bilirubin), serum amylase/lipase usually NORMAL in chronic pancreatitis, low fecal elastase (in steatorrhea). Imaging: Abdominal X-ray: pancreatic calcifications. U/S or CT: calcification, dilated ducts, pseudocyst. MRCP: duct narrowing and dilatation. EUS: most sensitive test for parenchymal and ductal abnormalities. Functional tests: 72-hour fecal fat (fecal elastase preferable); secretin test is gold standard but difficult/expensive.

Unlike acute pancreatitis where enzymes are elevated, in chronic pancreatitis the burnt-out pancreas may no longer produce sufficient amylase/lipase to elevate serum levels. EUS is preferred over CT for early detection as it can detect subtle parenchymal changes before CT findings appear.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chronic pancreatitis, including management of pain and steatorrhea?

**A:** Pain management: Complete EtOH abstinence; pancreatic enzyme replacement (reduces pain via negative feedback resting pancreas); analgesics; celiac ganglion blocks; pain decreases with time as pancreas 'burns out'. Endoscopy: sphincterotomy, stent if duct dilated, stone removal. Surgery: pancreaticojejunostomy if duct dilated (more effective than endoscopy); pancreatic resection if duct contracted. Steatorrhea: pancreatic enzyme replacement (neither endoscopy nor surgery can restore exocrine function).

Pancreatic enzyme replacement therapy (PERT) for steatorrhea replaces the lost exocrine function. The goal of analgesics is stepwise (acetaminophen → NSAIDs → opioids). Celiac ganglion blocks provide temporary pain relief. Pain often decreases as the pancreas is progressively destroyed ('burn-out' phenomenon).

**Q:** What is autoimmune pancreatitis and how is it diagnosed and treated?

**A:** Autoimmune pancreatitis most commonly mimics pancreatic cancer (pancreatic mass with jaundice + abdominal pain). Diagnosis: Histology — lymphocyte and plasma cell infiltration; Imaging — focal or diffuse pancreatic enlargement (sausage-shaped, low-density rim); Serology — elevated IgG4 (Type 1); Other organ involvement — sialadenitis, retroperitoneal fibrosis, biliary duct narrowing, nephritis. Treatment: Responds to prednisone. Refractory: immunomodulators (azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, methotrexate) or rituximab.

Autoimmune pancreatitis (AIP) is critical to recognize because it mimics pancreatic cancer but is steroid-responsive, thus avoiding unnecessary pancreatic surgery. IgG4-related disease can affect multiple organs. The 'sausage-shaped' pancreas on imaging is a classic description. Type 2 AIP is IgG4-negative and more associated with IBD.

**Q:** What is the Subjective Global Assessment (SGA) and how does it classify nutritional status?

**A:** SGA divides nutritional status into: A = Adequately nourished (no nutritional support needed); B = Mild or moderate malnutrition (may require nutritional support — risk vs. benefit considered); C = Severe malnutrition (requires nutritional support). Assessment includes: weight change in past 6 months and 2 weeks, dietary intake change, GI symptoms, functional capacity, disease effect on nutritional requirements, and physical exam (subcutaneous fat loss, muscle wasting, edema, ascites).

SGA is a validated clinical tool for nutritional assessment. The most important feature is weight loss expressed as change in BMI. Distinguishing markers of malnutrition from markers of disease (e.g., low albumin from liver disease vs. malnutrition) is clinically challenging.

**Q:** What are the indications for aggressive nutritional support with evidence of outcome improvement?

**A:** Evidence supports nutritional support for: (1) Short bowel syndrome (home TPN); (2) Before major abdominal/thoracic surgery with substantial malnutrition; (3) Before therapy for esophageal, head, and neck cancer; (4) Pancreatitis (acute and chronic); (5) Decompensated alcohol-related liver disease (no survival change, but supportive). For most ICU and acute illness indications, convincing evidence is lacking.

Current trend in ICU is 'permissive underfeeding' to prevent hyperglycemia (25-35 kcal/kg, with IV nutrition 60% carbohydrate, 40% fat). Protein requirements are 1-2 g/kg/day; disease increases the proportion of energy derived from protein compared to health.

**Q:** What are the key differences between enteral nutrition (EN) and parenteral nutrition (PN), and when is each indicated?

**A:** EN (tube feeding): Preferred whenever possible. Delivered via NG/NJ tube (brief use) or PEG/J-tube (>1 month). Feeds: polymeric (whole protein) or elemental (amino acids/simple sugars). Indicated when oral feeding inadequate or contraindicated. PN (intravenous feeding): Used when GI tract is non-functional. Proven to increase survival: bone marrow transplant, short bowel syndrome; some evidence in gastric cancer. EN is ALWAYS preferable over PN whenever possible — fewer serious complications, better understood nutritional requirements, prevents gut atrophy, less expensive.

Cochrane data show EN vs. TPN in pancreatitis reduces mortality RR by 0.50, organ failure by 0.55, and infections by 0.39. EN provides gut-specific fuels (glutamine, short-chain fatty acids), prevents villous atrophy, and stimulates gallbladder motility (prevents gallstones). PN's main complications: sepsis, pneumothorax from line insertion, metabolic complications (hyperglycemia, cholestasis).

**Q:** What are the complications of enteral nutrition (EN), including refeeding syndrome?

**A:** EN complications: Aspiration; Diarrhea; Refeeding syndrome (rare): carbohydrate stimulates insulin → cellular uptake → low serum phosphate, magnesium, and potassium; Overfeeding syndrome (rare): hypertonic dehydration, hyperglycemia, hypercapnia, azotemia (from excess protein). Relative contraindications: non-functioning gut (obstruction, fistulae), uncontrolled diarrhea, GI bleeding.

Refeeding syndrome is a potentially fatal complication of rapid refeeding in severely malnourished patients. The electrolyte shifts (hypophosphatemia, hypomagnesemia, hypokalemia) can cause arrhythmias, respiratory failure, and seizures. It is prevented by starting feeds slowly and correcting electrolytes.

**Q:** What are the indications for long-term parenteral nutrition (>1 month)?

**A:** Long-term PN indications: (1) Severe untreatable small bowel disease (e.g., radiation enteritis, extensive Crohn's disease, high-output fistulae); (2) Surgical resection of >70% of small bowel (e.g., small bowel infarction); (3) Severe motility diseases (e.g., scleroderma affecting bowel). Relative contraindications: functional GI tract (use EN instead), active infection without antibiotic coverage, inadequate venous access.

Short bowel syndrome (>70% resection) is the classic indication for home TPN. Hypomagnesemia may be an early sign of short bowel syndrome after surgical resection. In IBD, PN closes fistulae and heals acute mucosal inflammation, but the effect is transient and EN is equally effective.

**Q:** What is the mechanism, indications, and key side effects of Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPIs)?

**A:** Mechanism: Inhibit H+/K+-ATPase (proton pump) in gastric parietal cells. Indications: Duodenal/gastric ulcer, NSAID-associated ulcers, reflux esophagitis, GERD, dyspepsia, Zollinger-Ellison syndrome, H. pylori eradication (combined with antibiotics). Examples: omeprazole (Losec), lansoprazole (Prevacid), pantoprazole (Pantoloc), rabeprazole (Pariet), esomeprazole (Nexium). Side effects: Dizziness, headache, flatulence, nausea, rash, increased risk of osteoporotic fractures (secondary to impaired calcium absorption).

PPIs are irreversible inhibitors of the proton pump. For acute UGIB, IV pantoprazole is given as 80 mg bolus then 8 mg/h infusion. Long-term PPI use is associated with hypomagnesemia, C. difficile infection, and reduced absorption of vitamin B12, calcium, and iron.

**Q:** What are the gastrointestinal medications for constipation and their mechanisms? Distinguish between osmotic, stimulant, and bulk laxatives.

**A:** Osmotic laxatives: Lactulose (poorly absorbed, broken down to lactic acid in colon — lowers pH, increases osmotic pull; used for hepatic encephalopathy); PEG 3350 (Lax-a-day — osmotic water retention); Magnesium hydroxide (osmotic fluid retention distending colon). Stimulant laxatives: Senna (induces peristalsis in lower colon); Bisacodyl (enteric nerve stimulation + secretory effects). Bulk laxatives: Psyllium (Metamucil — increases stool bulk + water retention). Guanylate cyclase-C agonists: Linaclotide and plecanatide (open water channels in bowel epithelial cells; also reduce abdominal pain via colonic nociceptor inhibition — used for chronic constipation and IBS-C).

Lactulose is specifically used for hepatic encephalopathy (lowers colonic pH traps ammonia as NH4+ preventing absorption). NHE3 inhibitor tenapanor (Ibsrela) is used for IBS-C by reducing sodium absorption from small intestine/colon, increasing luminal water secretion.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action, indications, and key contraindications of loperamide (Imodium)?

**A:** Mechanism: Antidiarrheal via cholinergic, non-cholinergic, opiate, and non-opiate receptor-mediated mechanisms; decreases myenteric plexus activity. Indications: Adjunctive therapy for acute nonspecific diarrhea, chronic diarrhea in IBD, reducing ileostomy/colostomy output. Contraindications: Children <2 years, acute dysentery (bloody stools with fever), acute UC, pseudomembranous colitis associated with broad-spectrum antibiotics. Side effects: Abdominal pain, drowsiness/dizziness, dry mouth, N/V, hypersensitivity.

Loperamide is contraindicated in infectious/inflammatory diarrhea because slowing motility can worsen toxin-mediated colitis (e.g., C. difficile, EHEC). Eluxadoline (Viberzi), a bowel opioid modulator, is used for IBS-diarrhea but is contraindicated in pancreatic disease, excess alcohol use, gallstones, and sphincter of Oddi dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the antiemetic medications used in gastroenterology? Compare dimenhydrinate, prochlorperazine, metoclopramide, and ondansetron.

**A:** Dimenhydrinate (Gravol): Competitive H1 receptor antagonist; blocks chemoreceptor trigger zone, vestibular function. Used for motion sickness, radiation sickness, postoperative N/V. Prochlorperazine (Stemetil): D1/D2 receptor antagonist + alpha-adrenergic/anticholinergic. Used for postoperative N/V, anxiety. SE: dystonia, EPS, NMS. Metoclopramide (Maxeran): Dopamine + 5-HT receptor antagonist in chemoreceptor trigger zone + enhances ACh response in upper GI (promotes motility, increases LES tone). Used for GERD, gastroparesis, N/V, migraines. SE: restlessness, EPS, NMS. Ondansetron (Zofran): Selective 5-HT3 receptor antagonist centrally and peripherally on vagus nerve. Used for chemo/radiation-induced N/V. SE: constipation, cardiac dysrhythmia.

Metoclopramide is unique in that it both suppresses nausea centrally AND enhances gastric motility. It is useful in diabetic gastroparesis but is contraindicated in GI obstruction/perforation and pheochromocytoma. Domperidone (Motilium) is a peripherally acting D2 blocker (less CNS penetration) used for gastroparesis but risks cardiac arrhythmia.

**Q:** What are mesalamine (5-ASA) compounds and when are they used in IBD?

**A:** Mesalamine (Pentasa, Asacol, Salofalk) and sulfasalazine (Salazopyrin) are 5-aminosalicylic acid (5-ASA) agents. Mechanism: Block arachidonic acid metabolism (prostaglandins and leukotrienes), reducing mucosal inflammation. Indications: Mild to moderate UC (induction and maintenance); also available as suppositories/enemas for topical therapy. Sulfasalazine dose: 3-4 g/day. Side effects: Mesalamine — abdominal pain, arthralgia, headache. Sulfasalazine — rash, N/V, headache, reversible oligospermia.

5-ASAs are first-line for mild-moderate UC but are less effective in Crohn's disease. Sulfasalazine is a prodrug (5-ASA bound to sulfapyridine carrier) cleaved by colonic bacteria. Asacol contains phthalate which has potential urogenital teratogenicity for male fetuses. Topical 5-ASA (enema/suppository) is more effective than oral for distal UC.

**Q:** What are the anti-TNF biologics used in IBD? Compare infliximab, adalimumab, and vedolizumab.

**A:** Infliximab (Remicade): Monoclonal antibody to TNF-alpha; IV infusion; for severe/steroid-refractory CD and UC; drug of choice for perianal disease. Adalimumab (Humira): Monoclonal antibody to TNF-alpha; SC injection; for severe/steroid-refractory CD or poor infliximab response. Vedolizumab (Entyvio): Monoclonal antibody to alpha4-beta7 integrin (gut-selective); IV; for medically refractory CD/UC including failure of other TNF-alpha inhibitors. Key risks (all anti-TNFs): reactivated TB, PCP, lymphoma, serious infections.

The VARSITY trial showed vedolizumab was superior to adalimumab for clinical remission in UC (31.3% vs. 22.5% at week 52) but not for steroid-free remission. The SONIC trial showed infliximab + azathioprine combination had best steroid-free remission (56.8%) vs. infliximab alone (44.4%) vs. azathioprine alone (30%) in CD.

**Q:** What is ustekinumab's mechanism of action and evidence for use in IBD?

**A:** Ustekinumab (Stelara): Monoclonal antibody to IL-12 and IL-23 (specifically the p40 subunit shared by both). Induction: single IV weight-based dose; Maintenance: 90 mg SC every 8 weeks. Used for moderate-to-severe CD and UC. The UNIFI trial showed ustekinumab achieved clinical remission in 15.6% vs. 5.3% placebo in moderate-severe UC (P<0.001). Side effects: infections, headaches, joint pain, fever, N/V, Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome (PRES). Risankizumab (Skyrizi) targets the p19 subunit of IL-23 specifically.

IL-12 drives Th1 differentiation (important in CD); IL-23 drives Th17 responses (important in both CD and UC). By targeting the shared p40 subunit, ustekinumab blocks both pathways. It is an option for patients who failed anti-TNF therapy and is generally well-tolerated.

**Q:** What is rifaximin, and what are its indications in gastroenterology?

**A:** Rifaximin (Xifaxan): Non-absorbable antibiotic that affects dysbiosis of the gut microbiome. Dose: 550 mg BID. Indications: (1) Hepatic encephalopathy (maintenance of remission); (2) IBS-diarrhea; (3) Traveller's diarrhea. The rifaximin trial in hepatic encephalopathy (NEJM 2010) showed it reduced risk of HE episode (HR 0.42), with breakthrough episodes in 22.1% vs. 45.9% placebo.

Rifaximin works locally in the gut without systemic absorption, making it useful for gut-related conditions with minimal systemic side effects. In hepatic encephalopathy, it reduces ammonia-producing gut bacteria. It is often used in combination with lactulose for optimal HE prevention.

**Q:** What does the SONIC trial conclude about treatment of Crohn's disease?

**A:** The SONIC trial (NEJM 2010) compared infliximab + azathioprine combination vs. infliximab monotherapy vs. azathioprine monotherapy in biologic/immunosuppressive-naive CD patients. Results: Steroid-free remission at week 26: combination 56.8% > infliximab alone 44.4% > azathioprine alone 30% (P<0.001 for combination vs. azathioprine; P=0.06 for combination vs. infliximab). Conclusion: Combination infliximab + azathioprine achieves better steroid-free remission than either agent alone.

The mechanism behind combination superiority is likely that azathioprine reduces anti-infliximab antibody formation (immunogenicity), maintaining higher infliximab serum levels and prolonged efficacy. This trial established combination therapy as superior for moderate-to-severe CD.

**Q:** What does the VARSITY trial conclude about treatment of moderate-to-severe Ulcerative Colitis?

**A:** VARSITY trial (NEJM 2019): Vedolizumab vs. adalimumab in moderate-severe UC. At week 52: Clinical remission: vedolizumab 31.3% vs. adalimumab 22.5% (P=0.006) — vedolizumab superior. Endoscopic improvement: vedolizumab superior. Steroid-free remission: adalimumab 21.8% vs. vedolizumab 12.6% — NOT significant difference. Conclusion: Vedolizumab superior to adalimumab for clinical remission and endoscopic improvement in UC, but NOT for steroid-free clinical remission.

Vedolizumab's gut-selective mechanism (anti-alpha4-beta7 integrin, blocking lymphocyte migration specifically to gut) may explain its advantage in a mucosal disease like UC. The finding that adalimumab had higher steroid-free remission rates despite lower overall remission rates is a nuanced finding from this trial.

**Q:** A 50-year-old woman presents with pruritus, fatigue, and elevated ALP with jaundice. What antibody confirms Primary Biliary Cholangitis, and what is the first-line treatment?

**A:** Anti-mitochondrial antibodies (AMA) — 95% sensitivity and specificity for PBC. ALP elevated >6 months + positive AMA is sufficient for diagnosis; liver biopsy usually not required. First-line treatment: Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA). If inadequate response: add obeticholic acid. Pruritus: cholestyramine. Monitor with LFTs every 3-6 months, TSH annually, and bone mineral density every 2-4 years.

Middle-aged women with pruritus and elevated ALP should immediately trigger PBC on the differential. The absence of duct narrowing on MRCP distinguishes PBC from PSC. UDCA slows disease progression and, when ALP normalizes, achieves survival comparable to age- and sex-matched controls.

**Q:** A patient with acute pancreatitis develops fever, leukocytosis, and worsening abdominal pain at 3 weeks. CT shows necrotic areas. What is the most appropriate next step?

**A:** CT-guided percutaneous biopsy/aspiration of the necrotic areas to differentiate sterile from infected necrosis. If infected (FNA positive): proceed with drainage/debridement — preferably endoscopic (endoscopic transgastric necrosectomy) or percutaneous; surgical necrosectomy is last resort. Delay intervention >2 weeks to allow walled-off necrosis (WON) to develop — better demarcation between viable and necrotic tissue improves outcomes. Antibiotics alone (e.g., imipenem, cephalosporins) are added for confirmed infection.

This scenario represents infected necrotizing pancreatitis (walled-off necrosis). The 2010 endoscopic vs. surgical necrosectomy trial showed endoscopic approach reduces major complications, especially new-onset organ failure, compared to surgical necrosectomy. Antibiotics are NOT given prophylactically — only when infection is confirmed.

**Q:** What are the causes of elevated serum amylase that are NOT due to pancreatic disease?

**A:** Non-pancreatic causes of elevated amylase: Non-pancreatic abdominal: biliary tract disease, bowel obstruction/ischemia, perforated/penetrating ulcer, ruptured ectopic pregnancy, aortic aneurysm, chronic liver disease, peritonitis. Non-abdominal: cancer (lung, ovary, esophagus), salivary gland lesions, bulimia, renal transplant/insufficiency, burns, ketoacidosis, macroamylasemia. NOTE: When serum amylase is >5x normal, cause is almost always pancreatitis OR renal failure.

Lipase is more specific than amylase for pancreatic disease. The key pancreatic enzymes tested are: amylase, lipase, and chymotrypsin (mnemonic TALC: Trypsin, Amylase, Lipase, Chymotrypsin). Macroamylasemia (amylase bound to immunoglobulin) causes elevated serum amylase with normal urinary amylase.

**Q:** What JAK inhibitors are used in Ulcerative Colitis, and what are their major safety concerns?

**A:** JAK inhibitors for UC: Tofacitinib (Xeljanz) 5-10 mg BID; Upadacitinib (Rinvoq) 15 mg daily. Contraindications/concerns: TB (screen before use), Hepatitis B (risk of reactivation). Major safety concerns: Serious infections, macro-cardiac events (myocardial infarction, stroke), venous thrombosis (PE, DVT). These risks are dose-dependent and highest in patients with cardiovascular risk factors.

JAK inhibitors are small molecules (not biologics) that block cytokine signaling pathways important in UC pathogenesis. Unlike biologics (which are injected), JAK inhibitors are oral medications. Post-marketing data for tofacitinib raised concerns about cardiovascular events and malignancy, particularly at higher doses.

**Q:** What are the MELD score findings and its clinical utility based on the landmark gastroenterology trial?

**A:** MELD Score as Predictor of Death trial (Gastroenterology 2003): MELD score was prospectively applied to 3437 adult liver transplant candidates with chronic liver disease. Results: Waiting list mortality increased directly in proportion to MELD score. ROC curve: MELD = 0.83 vs. Child-Turcotte-Pugh = 0.76 for 3-month mortality prediction. Conclusion: MELD score accurately predicts 3-month mortality in chronic liver failure and should be used for liver transplant allocation.

MELD = 3.78 × ln[bilirubin (mg/dL)] + 11.2 × ln[INR] + 9.57 × ln[creatinine (mg/dL)] + 6.43. Higher MELD = worse prognosis. MELD is superior to Child-Pugh score for short-term mortality prediction and is the current basis for organ allocation in Canada/USA. MELD-Na incorporates sodium for improved accuracy.

**Q:** What does the FAMOUS trial demonstrate about famotidine in patients taking low-dose aspirin?

**A:** FAMOUS trial (Lancet 2009): Patients on low-dose aspirin without existing erosions/ulcers were randomized to famotidine 20 mg BID vs. placebo. At 12 weeks: gastric ulcers — famotidine 3.4% vs. placebo 15% (OR 0.20, P=0.0002); duodenal ulcers — 0.5% vs. 8.5% (OR 0.05, P=0.0045). Conclusion: Famotidine (H2 blocker) is effective for preventing gastric and duodenal ulcers, and erosive esophagitis in patients taking low-dose aspirin.

Low-dose aspirin (75-325 mg/day) carries significant GI ulcer risk via COX-1 inhibition reducing gastroprotective prostaglandins. Gastroprotection with H2 blockers or PPIs is recommended for patients on aspirin with risk factors (age >65, prior ulcer, concurrent NSAIDs/anticoagulants). This trial supports H2 blocker use as an alternative to PPIs for aspirin-related GI prophylaxis.

## Gastroenterology Gerd

**Q:** What is the role of esophageal manometry in GERD management?

**A:** Esophageal manometry studies esophageal motility. It is indicated for: (1) non-cardiac chest pain or dysphagia with normal gastroscopy; (2) before surgical fundoplication to ensure intact esophageal function; (3) to exclude alternative diagnoses such as scleroderma and achalasia. It diagnoses abnormalities of peristalsis and/or decreased LES tone, but cannot detect the presence of reflux.

Manometry is essential before anti-reflux surgery to confirm adequate peristalsis. Surgical fundoplication is more likely to succeed if lower esophageal pressure is diminished but is less likely to be successful if there is abnormal peristalsis — making pre-operative manometry critical.

**Q:** What are the only beneficial lifestyle changes for GERD (per evidence)?

**A:** Only two lifestyle changes have demonstrated benefit: (1) weight loss (if obese or with significant weight gain), and (2) elevating the head of the bed (for nocturnal symptoms). Dietary changes help symptoms but do not treat the underlying disease.

While patients are commonly counselled to avoid EtOH, coffee, spices, tomatoes, and citrus juices (which may help symptoms), evidence-based beneficial interventions are limited to weight reduction and positional modifications during sleep.

## Gastroenterology Barretts

**Q:** What is Barrett's esophagus (BE) and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** BE is the replacement of normal squamous esophageal epithelium with intestinal columnar epithelium (intestinal metaplasia). It is thought to be acquired through long-standing GERD. Diagnosis requires endoscopy showing salmon-pink mucosa in the distal esophagus confirmed by biopsy demonstrating specialized intestinal epithelium of any length within the esophagus.

BE is a premalignant condition — it predisposes to low-grade dysplasia → high-grade dysplasia → adenocarcinoma. Biopsy is mandatory because the endoscopic appearance alone is insufficient for diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the rate of malignant transformation in Barrett's esophagus without dysplasia, and what is the risk in high-grade dysplasia?

**A:** Rate of malignant transformation for all BE patients prior to dysplasia: approximately 0.12% per year. For high-grade dysplasia: 32–59% transformation rate over 5–8 years of surveillance.

The annual transformation risk in non-dysplastic BE is very low (~0.12%/yr), which is why the Canadian Task Force does not recommend routine screening endoscopy for GERD without alarm features. However, once high-grade dysplasia is identified, the cancer risk is dramatically higher, justifying aggressive intervention.

**Q:** What is the Canadian Task Force position on screening endoscopy for Barrett's esophagus in patients with chronic GERD?

**A:** The Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care currently does NOT recommend routine screening endoscopy for Barrett's esophagus and precursor conditions in patients with chronic GERD without red flag features (e.g. dysphagia, odynophagia, weight loss, anemia, GI bleeding). Patients with known BE should be referred to a gastroenterologist for endoscopic surveillance.

Despite BE increasing the risk of esophageal adenocarcinoma, most cases were not identified during screening. Additionally, patients undergoing screening may be unnecessarily exposed to harm. Risk factors (age, male sex, family history) alone are not sufficient to warrant screening in chronic GERD patients per Canadian guidelines.

**Q:** What is the surveillance and treatment protocol for Barrett's esophagus based on dysplasia grade?

**A:** No dysplasia: surveillance gastroscopy every 3–5 years. Low-grade dysplasia: surveillance every 6 months for 1 year, then annually; endoscopic ablation/resection is also a satisfactory option. High-grade dysplasia: intensive biopsy + endoscopic ablation/mucosal resection or radiofrequency ablation (RFA); esophagectomy is an option. Acid suppressive therapy with high-dose PPI indefinitely (or surgical fundoplication) to reduce transformation.

Evidence increasingly favours endoscopic ablation with mucosal resection or RFA over esophagectomy for high-grade dysplasia. All patients with BE require indefinite acid suppression to slow progression.

## Gastroenterology Dysphagia

**Q:** Distinguish oropharyngeal from esophageal dysphagia.

**A:** Oropharyngeal dysphagia: difficulty initiating swallowing, with choking, coughing, and nasal regurgitation. Causes include neurological (cortical, bulbar, peripheral), muscular (muscular dystrophy, polymyositis, myasthenia gravis), and structural (Zenker's diverticulum, thyromegaly, cervical spur, cricopharyngeal dysfunction).

Esophageal dysphagia: inability to move food down the esophagus. Causes include mechanical obstruction and neuromuscular disorders.

Distinguishing oropharyngeal from esophageal dysphagia guides the differential diagnosis. Key questions include: solids only vs. solids+liquids; intermittent vs. progressive; and associated symptoms (regurgitation, chest pain, voice change, weight loss).

**Q:** What does dysphagia to solids only versus solids AND liquids suggest in terms of etiology?

**A:** Dysphagia to solids only suggests mechanical obstruction (structural narrowing such as carcinoma, peptic stricture, Schatzki ring). Dysphagia to both solids and liquids suggests a neuromuscular disorder (e.g. achalasia, scleroderma, diffuse esophageal spasm).

This clinical distinction is a key diagnostic clue: structural lesions impede solid boluses first, while motility disorders (affecting peristalsis and LES function) impair transport of both solid and liquid.

## Gastroenterology Motility

**Q:** What is achalasia and what are its diagnostic findings?

**A:** Achalasia is failure of smooth muscle relaxation at the LES with swallowing, resulting in absent peristalsis. Usually idiopathic (Auerbach's plexus degeneration); secondary causes include malignancy and Chagas disease (Trypanosoma cruzi).

Diagnostic findings:
- CXR: no air in stomach, dilated esophagus
- Barium swallow: esophagus terminates in narrowing at LES ('bird's beak' appearance)
- Endoscopy: normal mucosa, tight LES, residual liquid/food debris in dilated esophagus
- Manometry: definitive diagnosis — increased LES pressure, incomplete LES relaxation with swallowing, aperistalsis

Manometry is the definitive diagnostic test for achalasia. The 'bird's beak' on barium swallow is classic but not diagnostic alone. Endoscopy is important to exclude pseudoachalasia due to malignancy.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for achalasia?

**A:** 1. Pneumatic dilation (5% risk of perforation)
2. Injection of botulinum toxin into LES (temporary effect)
3. Surgical myotomy (Heller's myotomy)
4. POEM (peroral endoscopic myotomy) — non-inferior to laparoscopic Heller's myotomy at 2 years

Per a 2019 NEJM RCT, POEM was non-inferior to laparoscopic Heller's myotomy (LHM) for symptomatic achalasia at 2 years (83% vs 81.7% clinical success). POEM was associated with more reflux esophagitis at 12 months (44% vs 29%). Botulinum toxin is temporary and reserved for poor surgical candidates.

**Q:** What are the pathophysiology, diagnosis, and treatment of scleroderma-related esophageal disease?

**A:** Pathophysiology: Systemic autoimmune/fibrotic disease causing blood vessel damage → intramural neuronal dysfunction → distal esophageal muscle weakening → aperistalsis and loss of LES tone → reflux → stricture → dysphagia.

Diagnosis: Clinical features of scleroderma; manometry showing decreased LES pressure and decreased/absent peristalsis in the esophageal body.

Treatment: Aggressive GERD therapy (PPIs BID) and prokinetic agents (domperidone, metoclopramide).

Unlike achalasia (increased LES pressure), scleroderma causes decreased LES pressure — distinguishable on manometry. The aperistalsis combined with low LES tone creates severe GERD and dysphagia.

**Q:** What is diffuse esophageal spasm (DES) and how is it diagnosed and treated?

**A:** DES is normal peristalsis interspersed with frequent, repetitive, spontaneous, high-pressure, non-peristaltic waves (tertiary peristalsis). It is rare and can be difficult to diagnose due to intermittent presentation.

Diagnosis:
- Barium x-ray: 'corkscrew pattern'
- Manometry: >20% premature contractions
- Endoscopy: normal mucosa

Treatment:
- Reassurance that symptoms are not cardiac
- Medical: nitrates, calcium channel blockers, anticholinergics (variable benefit)
- Surgical: long esophageal myotomy if unresponsive (rarely helpful); balloon dilatation

DES presents with chest pain and dysphagia, making differentiation from cardiac chest pain important. The corkscrew pattern on barium swallow is classic. Manometry with >20% premature contractions is diagnostic.

## Gastroenterology Esophagus

**Q:** What is Zenker's diverticulum and how is it treated?

**A:** Zenker's diverticulum is the most common form of esophageal diverticulum. It is a posterior pharyngeal outpouching, most often on the left side, above the cricopharyngeal muscle and below the inferior pharyngeal constrictor muscle.

Symptoms: dysphagia, regurgitation of undigested food, halitosis.

Treatment:
- Small and asymptomatic: no treatment required
- Large and symptomatic: endoscopic or surgical myotomy of cricopharyngeal muscle + surgical excision of the sac

Zenker's diverticulum results from a defect at Killian's triangle where the inferior pharyngeal constrictor and cricopharyngeal muscle meet. Food accumulates and regurgitates, causing halitosis and aspiration risk. Treatment targets the cricopharyngeal muscle dysfunction.

**Q:** What is a peptic stricture and how is it managed?

**A:** A peptic stricture is a smooth, concentric narrowing most commonly seen in the lower esophagus, resulting from GERD-induced esophagitis and scarring.

Clinical features: dysphagia alongside a long history of reflux, but reflux symptoms may paradoxically disappear as the stricture develops (because food is retained above the narrowing).

Diagnosis: endoscopy (or barium study if endoscopy is contraindicated/unavailable).

Treatment: endoscopic dilatation and indefinite PPI therapy.

The disappearance of heartburn with new-onset dysphagia in a GERD patient should raise suspicion for peptic stricture. Endoscopic dilatation relieves the narrowing; indefinite PPI prevents re-stricturing.

**Q:** What is Plummer-Vinson syndrome and what is its triad?

**A:** Plummer-Vinson syndrome is a rare condition (prevalence <1 in 1,000,000) characterized by the triad: (1) iron deficiency anemia, (2) dysphagia, and (3) esophageal webs (upper esophagus).

Prognosis is good when treated with iron supplementation and esophageal dilatation.

Esophageal webs are eccentric smooth extensions of normal esophageal tissue (mucosa and submucosa) in the upper esophagus. The association with iron deficiency anemia is the hallmark of Plummer-Vinson syndrome. Treatment of the iron deficiency often leads to resolution.

**Q:** What is a Schatzki ring and how does it present?

**A:** A Schatzki ring is a mucosal ring at the squamocolumnar junction (lower esophagus). It causes intermittent dysphagia with solids. Treatment involves disrupting the ring with endoscopic dilation.

Schatzki rings are circumferential narrowings at the gastroesophageal junction. Unlike peptic strictures, the dysphagia is typically intermittent rather than progressive. Endoscopic disruption is curative in most cases.

**Q:** What is eosinophilic esophagitis (EoE), how does it present, and how is it treated?

**A:** EoE is an eosinophilic infiltrate of the esophageal epithelium, usually primary but can be secondary. Often associated with allergies. Most characteristically occurs in young men.

Presentation: dysphagia and food impaction.

Diagnosis: endoscopic biopsy (mucosal rings seen at endoscopy on endoscopy).

Treatment:
(a) Diet — trial exclusion of milk, soy, eggs, wheat, peanuts/tree nuts, seafood (6-food elimination)
(b) PPI
(c) Swallowed topical corticosteroid (fluticasone or orodispersible budesonide)
(d) Rarely prednisone
(e) Biologic therapy (dupilumab)

EoE is distinct from GERD-induced eosinophilia. The 6-food elimination diet can identify triggering allergens. Swallowed topical steroids (not inhaled) are the primary pharmacological therapy. Dupilumab (IL-4/IL-13 blocker) is a newer biologic option.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of infectious esophagitis, and what is the characteristic appearance and treatment of each?

**A:** 1. Candida (most common): whitish-yellow plaques without visible ulceration. Treatment: nystatin swish-and-swallow for simple infection; ketoconazole or fluconazole for severe/systemic.

2. HSV (second most common): focal ulcers. Treatment: often self-limiting; acyclovir, valacyclovir, or famciclovir.

3. CMV: focal ulcers (particularly in immunocompromised). Treatment: IV ganciclovir, famciclovir, or oral valganciclovir.

Risk factors: DM, chemotherapeutic agents, immunocompromised states.
Characteristic symptom: odynophagia (less often dysphagia).

Infectious esophagitis is predominantly a disease of immunocompromised patients. Candida causes white plaques (thrush), while HSV and CMV cause discrete ulcers. Odynophagia is more characteristic than dysphagia. Diagnosis requires endoscopic visualization with biopsy/brushing.

## Gastroenterology Dyspepsia

**Q:** What are the red flag symptoms of dyspepsia that raise suspicion for gastrointestinal malignancy?

**A:** Red flags in dyspepsia (raise suspicion for GI malignancy):
- Unintended weight loss
- Persistent vomiting
- Progressive dysphagia
- Odynophagia
- Unexplained anemia or iron deficiency
- Melena/hematemesis
- Palpable abdominal mass or lymphadenopathy
- Family history of upper GI cancer
- Previous gastric surgery

Dyspepsia is most commonly functional, but these alarm features mandate prompt gastroscopy to exclude malignancy. In Canada, gastroscopy is recommended for patients >60 years with dyspepsia, or younger patients with red flag features or risk factors for gastric cancer.

## Gastroenterology Stomach

**Q:** What are the cells of the gastric mucosa and their secretory products?

**A:** - Parietal cells: gastric acid (HCl) and intrinsic factor (IF); stimulated by histamine, acetylcholine (ACh), and gastrin
- Chief cells: pepsinogen; stimulated by vagal input and local acid
- D-cells: somatostatin; inhibits release of hormones including gastrin
- G-cells: gastrin; stimulates H+ production from parietal cells
- Superficial epithelial cells: mucus and HCO3−; protect gastric mucosa

Understanding gastric cell physiology is fundamental to the pharmacology of acid suppression (PPIs block H+/K+-ATPase on parietal cells; H2 blockers antagonize histamine at parietal cells) and to conditions like pernicious anemia (loss of IF from parietal cells causes B12 deficiency).

## Gastroenterology Gastritis

**Q:** What is the Updated Sydney Classification of gastritis? List the types and common etiologies.

**A:** Acute Gastritis:
- Hemorrhagic/erosive gastritis: EtOH, aspirin/NSAIDs, shock/physiological stress (ICU patients)
- Helicobacter gastritis: H. pylori

Chronic Gastritis:
- Non-atrophic: H. pylori
- Atrophic: H. pylori, dietary/environmental factors, autoimmunity
- Chemical: NSAIDs, bile acid
- Radiation: radiation injury
- Lymphocytic: celiac disease, drugs
- Eosinophilic: food allergies
- Non-infectious granulomatous: Crohn's disease, sarcoidosis
- Other infectious: bacteria, viruses, fungi, parasites, TB, syphilis

Non-erosive gastritis is often asymptomatic and requires biopsy for diagnosis. Erosive gastritis can cause bleeding. Management is directed at the underlying etiology — notably H. pylori eradication and NSAID cessation.

## Gastroenterology Pud

**Q:** What are the relative contributions of H. pylori, NSAIDs, and other factors to duodenal versus gastric ulcers?

**A:** Duodenal ulcer etiology: H. pylori 90%, NSAIDs ~35% (some overlap), physiologic stress-induced ~4%, Zollinger-Ellison syndrome ~4%, idiopathic ~15%.

Gastric ulcer etiology: H. pylori 60%, NSAIDs ~35%, physiologic stress-induced ~5%, Zollinger-Ellison syndrome <1%, idiopathic ~10%.

Note: NSAIDs more commonly cause gastric ulcers than duodenal ulcers. H. pylori-negative, NSAID-negative ulcers are increasingly recognized as H. pylori prevalence decreases.

H. pylori is the dominant cause of both types but is more strongly linked to duodenal ulcers. NSAIDs preferentially cause gastric ulcers via inhibition of prostaglandin synthesis. Recognizing the etiology guides treatment and secondary prevention.

**Q:** What are the classic 6 clinical features of duodenal ulcer?

**A:** Classic features of duodenal ulcer (6):
1. Epigastric pain (may localize to tip of xiphoid)
2. Burning quality
3. Develops 1–3 hours after meals
4. Relieved by eating and antacids
5. Interrupts sleep (nocturnal pain)
6. Periodicity — occurs in clusters of weeks with subsequent periods of remission

While these 6 features are classic, history alone cannot distinguish duodenal ulcer from functional dyspepsia. Notably, 70% of peptic ulcers are asymptomatic — underscoring the importance of testing for complications. Gastric ulcers have more atypical symptoms and require biopsy to exclude malignancy.

**Q:** What are the complications of peptic ulcer disease and their approximate frequencies?

**A:** - Bleeding: 10% (severe if from gastroduodenal artery)
- Perforation: 2% (usually anterior ulcers)
- Gastric outlet obstruction: 2%
- Posterior inflammation/penetration: 2% (may also cause pancreatitis)

70% of peptic ulcers are asymptomatic, meaning complications may be the first presentation. Anterior ulcers tend to perforate; posterior ulcers penetrate into the pancreas causing pancreatitis. Bleeding is the most common complication.

**Q:** What factors contribute to increased mortality risk in bleeding peptic ulcer?

**A:** Risk factors for increased mortality in bleeding peptic ulcer:
- Co-existent illness (comorbidities)
- Hemodynamic instability
- Age >60 years

Clinical risk factors for rebleeding: age >60, bleeding diathesis, history of PUD, comorbid disease, hemodynamic instability.

Endoscopic signs of high rebleeding risk (more predictive than clinical): active bleeding, visible vessel, adherent clot, red spot.

Endoscopic risk stratification guides therapy. High-risk stigmata warrant endoscopic hemostasis (clips, thermal coagulation + epinephrine) and ICU admission. Low-risk (flat pigmented spot or clean base) can be managed without hemostasis and with early discharge.

**Q:** Describe the management algorithm for a suspected bleeding peptic ulcer.

**A:** 1. ABCs: assess vitals (BP, HR, orthostatic changes)
2. Labs: CBC, electrolytes, BUN, Cr, INR, blood type and crossmatch
3. Resuscitate with crystalloids and blood products as indicated
4. Consider NG placement + aspiration to confirm upper GI source
5. IV pantoprazole: 80 mg bolus + 8 mg/h continuous infusion
6. Erythromycin 250 mg IV 30 min before endoscopy (prokinetic to clear stomach)
7. Endoscopy:
   - High-risk stigmata: hemostasis (clips, thermal coagulation + epinephrine), continue IV PPI, monitor in hospital
   - Low-risk: no hemostasis, start/continue oral PPI, decreased in-hospital monitoring
8. Post-endoscopy: resume clear fluids at 6 h, test for H. pylori, counsel re: NSAIDs/antiplatelet agents
9. Re-bleeding: repeat endoscopy; escalate to interventional radiology or surgery if needed

IV pantoprazole suppresses acid to stabilize the clot. Erythromycin accelerates gastric emptying to improve endoscopic visualization. Endoscopic therapy reduces rebleeding rates and need for surgery in high-risk lesions.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of NSAID-induced ulceration?

**A:** Direct mechanism: local (topical) effect of NSAID on gastric mucosa → erosions and petechiae.

Indirect (systemic) mechanism: NSAIDs inhibit mucosal cyclooxygenase → decreased prostaglandin synthesis → reduced secretion of protective bicarbonate and mucus → decreased mucosal blood flow → ulcers.

Key point: IV NSAIDs cause ulcers (via systemic effect) but NOT erosions (which require direct topical contact). NSAIDs more commonly cause gastric than duodenal ulcers.

The distinction between direct and indirect mechanisms explains why enteric-coated aspirin (ECASA) provides minor benefit — it decreases erosions (direct effect) but does NOT decrease ulcer incidence (systemic effect remains). Misoprostol and PPIs work by replacing or stimulating prostaglandin-mediated protective mechanisms.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for NSAID-induced peptic ulcer?

**A:** Risk factors for NSAID-induced peptic ulcer:
1. Previous peptic ulcers or upper GI bleeding
2. Age ≥65 years
3. High dose of NSAID or multiple NSAIDs
4. Concomitant corticosteroid use
5. Concomitant cardiovascular disease or other significant comorbidities

When any of these risk factors are present, prophylactic PPI co-therapy is recommended with NSAID/ASA use. Misoprostol is an alternative but less effective and poorly tolerated option. The combination of corticosteroids + NSAIDs dramatically increases ulcer risk.

**Q:** What is stress-induced ulceration, what are its risk factors, and how is it treated?

**A:** Stress-induced ulceration is ulceration/erosion in the upper GI tract of critically ill patients (physiological stress, not psychiatric). Most common in fundus; usually multiple, superficial erosions.

Risk factors:
- Mechanical ventilation (most important)
- Anti-coagulation
- Multi-organ failure
- Septicemia
- Severe surgery/trauma
- CNS injury (Cushing's ulcers)
- Burns >35% body surface area (Curling's ulcers)

Treatment:
- Prophylaxis: gastric acid suppressants (PPI most potent but may increase pneumonia risk; H2 blockers less potent but lower pneumonia risk)
- Treat underlying cause; aggressive fluid resuscitation
- Ulcer treatment same as for bleeding peptic ulcer (often less successful in ICU setting)

Mechanical ventilation is the single most important risk factor. PPIs are more effective at preventing UGIB but the associated increased pneumonia risk has led some guidelines to favour H2 blockers in low-to-moderate risk patients. Stress ulcers are clinically silent (painless) until they bleed.

**Q:** Distinguish Curling's ulcer from Cushing's ulcer.

**A:** Curling's ulcer: acute peptic ulcer of the duodenum resulting from severe burns (>35% BSA). Mnemonic: BURN from a CURLING iron.

Cushing's ulcer: peptic ulcer produced by elevated intracranial pressure (ICP). Mechanism: elevated ICP stimulates vagal nuclei → increased gastric acid secretion → ulceration. May occur anywhere in the upper GI tract.

Both are forms of stress ulceration but with specific inciting triggers. Cushing's ulcers tend to be single and deep (due to vagal hyperstimulation causing acid hypersecretion), while other stress ulcers are typically multiple and superficial.

## Gastroenterology H Pylori

**Q:** What is the diagnostic approach to H. pylori infection? List the tests, their sensitivity/specificity, and key limitations.

**A:** Non-invasive tests:
- Urea breath test: sensitivity 90–100%, specificity 89–100%. Affected by PPI therapy (false negatives); hold PPI ≥2 weeks before test.
- Serology: sensitivity 88–99%, specificity 89–95%. Does NOT distinguish active vs. past infection; should NOT be used for post-eradication status. Can remain positive after treatment.
- Fecal antigen: only rarely used in clinical practice.

Invasive tests (require endoscopy):
- Histology: sensitivity 93–99%, specificity 95–99%; gold standard; affected by PPI (false negatives)
- Rapid urease test (on biopsy): sensitivity 89–98%, specificity 93–100%; rapid
- Microbiology culture: sensitivity 98%, specificity 95–100%; not widely available; used for antibiotic susceptibility testing (research only)

The urea breath test is the preferred non-invasive test for both diagnosis and test of cure (not serology). PPIs must be held ≥2 weeks and antibiotics/bismuth ≥4 weeks before test of cure with urea breath test or stool antigen test.

**Q:** What is the first-line H. pylori eradication regimen in Canada?

**A:** Bismuth quadruple therapy for 14 days (recommended first-line):
- PPI (e.g. lansoprazole 30 mg BID)
- Bismuth 525 mg QID
- Metronidazole 500 mg QID
- Tetracycline 500 mg QID

Alternative — Concomitant non-bismuth quadruple therapy for 14 days:
- PPI + amoxicillin + metronidazole + clarithromycin

Test of cure: urea breath test or stool antigen test (not serology) for all patients treated for H. pylori. Hold PPI >2 weeks and antibiotics/bismuth >4 weeks before test of cure.

Bismuth quadruple therapy is preferred due to rising clarithromycin resistance in Canada. The 14-day duration is more effective than 7-day courses. Test of cure is mandatory for all treated patients to confirm eradication and prevent complications.

**Q:** What are the epidemiological features of H. pylori infection in Canada?

**A:** - H. pylori affects approximately 20% of Canadians
- Increased prevalence in Indigenous populations and immigrants from developing countries
- Highest prevalence in those raised during the 1930s
- Infection most commonly acquired in childhood, presumably by fecal-oral route
- High prevalence associated with developing countries, low socioeconomic status, poor sanitation, and overcrowding
