The Canadian-specific data is important for the MCCQE1. The cohort effect (higher prevalence in those raised in the 1930s) reflects historical sanitation conditions. Understanding transmission guides public health interventions, particularly in high-risk communities.

**Q:** What outcomes are associated with H. pylori infection if left untreated?

**A:** - Asymptomatic (non-erosive) gastritis initially occurs in all patients
- Peptic ulcer in 15% of patients
- Gastric carcinoma or MALT lymphoma in 0.5% of patients
- Most are asymptomatic

Antral gastritis (15% of patients): high gastric acid, associated with duodenal ulcer, may progress to duodenal metaplasia.
Pangastritis (85% of patients): low gastric acid, associated with stomach ulcer and cancer.

H. pylori eradication prevents recurrence of PUD and reduces the risk of gastric cancer. A 2018 NEJM RCT showed that H. pylori eradication after subtotal gastrectomy for gastric cancer reduced metachronous cancer incidence (7.2% vs 13% control), but did not completely eliminate the risk, suggesting eradication cannot fully reverse established carcinogenic processes.

## Gastroenterology Diarrhea

**Q:** What is the definition and classification of diarrhea?

**A:** Clinical definition: stools that are looser and/or more frequent than normal (≥3×/day). Physiological definition: 24-hour stool weight >200 g (less useful clinically).

Classification:
1. Acute vs. chronic
2. Small volume (tablespoons; typical of colonic diseases) vs. large volume (>½ cup; typical of small bowel diseases)
3. Watery: secretory (persists with fasting) vs. osmotic (stops with fasting)
4. Steatorrhea (fatty diarrhea)
5. Inflammatory (bloody or watery)

This classification framework guides the investigation strategy. The secretory vs. osmotic distinction is made clinically by fasting: osmotic diarrhea stops when intake ceases because the osmotic agent is removed, whereas secretory diarrhea continues regardless of fasting.

**Q:** What distinguishes inflammatory from non-inflammatory diarrhea?

**A:** Inflammatory diarrhea:
- Site: usually colon
- Mechanism: organisms/cytotoxins invade mucosa → destruction of mucosal cells
- Symptoms: bloody (not always), small volume, high frequency, lower abdominal cramping with urgency and tenesmus, may have fever and shock
- Sigmoidoscopy: usually abnormal mucosa
- Investigations: fecal WBC and RBC positive
- Higher yield with stool C&S
- Risk of life-threatening megacolon, perforation, hemorrhage
- ABx may be helpful

Non-inflammatory diarrhea:
- Site: usually small intestine
- Mechanism: stimulation of intestinal water secretion + inhibition of water absorption (net secretion)
- Symptoms: watery, little or no blood, large volume, low frequency, upper/periumbilical pain/cramping ± shock
- Sigmoidoscopy: usually normal mucosa
- Investigations: fecal WBC negative
- Lower yield with stool C&S
- Chief life-threatening problem is electrolyte disturbances/fluid depletion
- ABx unlikely helpful

This distinction guides investigation and treatment. Inflammatory diarrhea from bacterial invasion (CHESS organisms) requires stool cultures and sometimes antibiotics. Non-inflammatory diarrhea management focuses on rehydration.

**Q:** What are the infectious causes of inflammatory (bloody) diarrhea? Use the mnemonic CHESS.

**A:** CHESS:
- C: Campylobacter
- H: Hemorrhagic E. coli (e.g. O157:H7 — EHEC)
- E: Entamoeba histolytica
- S: Salmonella
- S: Shigella

Also: C. difficile

These organisms invade the colonic mucosa producing bloody, small-volume, high-frequency diarrhea with tenesmus. EHEC O157:H7 is particularly dangerous due to Shiga toxin causing hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). Antibiotics are contraindicated in EHEC because they may increase Shiga toxin release.

**Q:** What are the indications for antimicrobial therapy in acute diarrhea?

**A:** Always indicated:
- Shigella
- Vibrio cholerae
- C. difficile
- Traveller's diarrhea (E. coli ETEC)
- Giardia
- Entamoeba histolytica
- Cyclospora

Situational (case-by-case):
- Salmonella (treat only if: Salmonella typhi; OR underlying immunodeficiency, hemolytic anemia, extremes of age, aneurysms, prosthetic valve/grafts/joints, sickle cell disease)
- Campylobacter
- Yersinia
- Non-enterotoxigenic E. coli

General indications: septicemia; prolonged fever with fecal blood or WBCs on O&P.

Antibiotics are NOT routinely indicated in acute diarrhea — most infections are self-limiting. EHEC O157:H7 is a key contraindication to antibiotics (increases HUS risk). Non-typhi Salmonella typically should NOT be treated unless specific high-risk conditions are present.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to antimotility agents (e.g. loperamide) in diarrhea, and what are their side effects?

**A:** Contraindications: mucosal inflammation; bloody diarrhea.

Side effects of antimotility agents (diphenoxylate, loperamide):
- Abdominal cramps
- Toxic megacolon

Note: Bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) — a modifier of fluid transport — may be helpful but should NOT be used in the presence of bloody diarrhea or fever.

In inflammatory diarrhea, antimotility agents can trap infectious organisms and toxins in the gut, promoting toxic megacolon — especially dangerous in C. difficile colitis and EHEC infection. Loperamide is appropriate only in non-inflammatory, non-bloody diarrhea.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Salmonella typhi (typhoid fever)?

**A:** Salmonella typhi (typhoid/enteric fever) features:
- Prodrome: high fever, relative bradycardia (pulse-temperature dissociation), headache, abdominal pain
- Rose spot rash: transient maculopapular rash on anterior thorax and upper abdomen
- Diarrhea is NOT the initial presentation

Treatment: always treat Salmonella typhi (unlike non-typhi Salmonella).

Typhoid fever's classic features — relative bradycardia + high fever + rose spot rash — distinguish it from other febrile illnesses. Diarrhea typically occurs later in the illness. All typhoid cases must be treated with antibiotics (fluoroquinolones or azithromycin) and reported to public health.

**Q:** What are the indications for stool culture (C&S/O&P) in acute diarrhea?

**A:** Stool cultures are required only if diarrhea is:
1. Inflammatory (bloody diarrhea)
2. Severe
3. For epidemiological purposes:
   - Daycare worker or nursing home resident
   - Community outbreaks (e.g. Walkerton)
   - Travel history

Important limitations:
- O&P results take up to 3 weeks
- C&S only tests Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, E. coli, Yersinia — other organisms must be ordered separately
- C. difficile toxin: indicated if recent/remote antibiotic use, hospitalization, nursing home living, or recent chemotherapy

The Walkerton outbreak (2000, Ontario) — E. coli O157:H7 and Campylobacter contaminating municipal water — is a landmark Canadian public health event that shaped infectious diarrhea reporting protocols. Routine stool cultures in mild, self-limited diarrhea are not cost-effective.
